\boxed{f'(x_0) = \lim_{\Delta x \to 0}\frac{f(x_0 + \Delta x) - f(x_0)}{\Delta x}}
\label{eq:derivative_definition}
\end{equation}
A function is \textbf{differentiable} at $x_0$ if this derivative exists; it can be written $f'(x)$, $Df(x)$ or $dy/dx$. As $\Delta x \to 0$ the secant line rotates around $A$ and approaches a limiting position, which we identify with the \textbf{tangent line} (Figure~\ref{fig:derivative_secant_to_tangent}). The derivative at $x_0$ is therefore the slope of the tangent to the graph at that point, and the tangent line at $P(x_0, f(x_0))$ is
\begin{equation}
\boxed{y - f(x_0) = f'(x_0)(x - x_0)}
\label{eq:tangent_line_equation}
\end{equation}

\begin{figure}[!htbp]
\centering
\includegraphics[page=31, width=0.62\linewidth, keepaspectratio]{\pdf}
\caption{From the secant to the tangent: as $\Delta x \to 0$, the line through $A$ and $B$ approaches the tangent at $A$, whose slope is $f'(x_0)$.}
\label{fig:derivative_secant_to_tangent}
\end{figure}

\subsection{What the derivatives tell us about the data}

The first derivative tells us whether a function increases or decreases. If $f$ is increasing on an interval, the slopes of its tangents are positive (or zero), so $f'(x) = \tan\theta \ge 0$; conversely, if $f'(x) \ge 0$ for every $x$ in an interval $I$, then $f$ is increasing on $I$. In the same way, $f'(x) \le 0$ on $I$ means that $f$ is decreasing.

Derivatives also tell us about the shape of a curve. A function differentiable on $I$ is \textbf{convex} if, for every $x \neq x_0$ in $I$, its graph lies above the tangent at $x_0$,
\begin{equation}
f(x) > f(x_0) + f'(x_0)(x - x_0),
\label{eq:convexity_tangent_def}
\end{equation}
and \textbf{concave} if it lies below it, $f(x) < f(x_0) + f'(x_0)(x - x_0)$. If $f$ is twice differentiable, there is a simpler test based on the second derivative:
\begin{equation}
\boxed{f''(x) \ge 0 \;\; \forall x \in I \iff f \text{ convex on } I, \qquad f''(x) \le 0 \;\; \forall x \in I \iff f \text{ concave on } I}
\label{eq:second_derivative_convexity}
\end{equation}

\subsection{Stationary points, maxima and minima}

If $f'(x_0) = 0$, the point $P(x_0, f(x_0))$ is a \textbf{stationary point}, a point with a horizontal tangent $y = k$. To identify and classify stationary points we compute the derivative, find the points where it vanishes, and study its sign to the left and to the right of each of them. If $f' > 0$ on the left and $f' < 0$ on the right, the function increases and then decreases, and $x_0$ is a \textbf{local maximum}. If $f' < 0$ on the left and $f' > 0$ on the right, $x_0$ is a \textbf{local minimum}. If the sign is the same on both sides (both positive or both negative), $x_0$ is a \textbf{horizontal inflection point}.

Be careful, though: a maximum or minimum does not always have a zero derivative. A function can have a local or absolute extremum where it is not differentiable, and possibly not even continuous; there the derivative is not zero, it simply does not exist. A corner point is the typical example: at $x = 1$ a function can be continuous but not differentiable, because the left and right derivatives exist but are different, and still have a local maximum there. Extrema can also sit at the boundary of the interval: a function can reach its absolute minimum at an endpoint $b$ where $f'(b)$ exists but is not zero, because Fermat's theorem ($f'(x_0) = 0$ at an extremum) applies only to interior points. So, to understand the trend of the data, we cannot simply look for the points where the derivative vanishes.

\section{Geometric Approximation and Curvature}
\label{sec:geometric_approximation}

\subsection{Arcs of conic sections}

Instead of a functional model, a profile can sometimes be approximated geometrically. A complete conic section (a circle, an ellipse or a complete hyperbola) is not a function, because it fails the vertical line test: one value of $x$ may correspond to two values of $y$. However, a segment of a conic can satisfy the definition of a function $y = f(x)$. Typical examples are the parabolic arc $y = \sqrt{ax + b}$ ($a \neq 0$), the semicircumference $y = \sqrt{a^2 - x^2}$ ($a > 0$), the hyperbola branch $y = \sqrt{a^2 + x^2}$ ($a > 0$) and the positive branch of an equilateral hyperbola $y = \sqrt{x^2 - a^2}$ ($a > 0$).

\subsection{Local approximation with circles}

If what we care about is the local geometry, a measured profile can be approximated piecewise by arcs of circles fitted at different positions along the curve. Around a selected position, a small neighbourhood of points is fitted with a circle (Figure~\ref{fig:membrane_circle_fitting}),
\begin{equation}
\boxed{(x - x_c)^2 + (y - y_c)^2 = R^2}
\label{eq:circle_equation}
\end{equation}
whose parameters are the centre coordinates $(x_c, y_c)$ and the radius $R$. The local curvature is then
\begin{equation}
\boxed{\kappa = \frac{1}{R}}
\label{eq:curvature_radius_relation}
\end{equation}
Moving the fitting window along the profile, a new circle is found at each position, which gives a sequence of radii $R_1, R_2, R_3, \dots$ and therefore a spatial map of the curvature.

\begin{figure}[h]
\centering
\includegraphics[page=30, width=0.65\linewidth, keepaspectratio]{\pdf}
\caption{Local approximation of a measured profile with a circle of centre $(x_c, y_c)$ and radius $R$.}
\label{fig:membrane_circle_fitting}
\end{figure}

\subsection{Curvature from derivatives}

Imagine that your supervisor studies mitochondrial division and wants to know whether a mitochondrion starts to divide every time its membrane reaches a certain curvature. They give you the extracted membrane profile and ask: what is the curvature locally, at each point? First of all we need a mathematical definition. The \textbf{curvature} is the rate at which the angle $\theta$ of the tangent changes as we move along the curve by an arc length $s$:
\begin{equation}
\boxed{\kappa = \frac{d\theta}{ds}}
\label{eq:curvature_fundamental_def}
\end{equation}
It measures how fast the tangent direction changes along the curve, and it is related to the radius of the local circle by $|\kappa| = 1/R$: higher curvature means a smaller radius.
\clearpage
\begin{figure}[h]
\centering
\includegraphics[page=32, width=\linewidth, keepaspectratio]{\pdf}
\caption{Geometry of curvature: along the arc $ds$, made of the displacements $dx$ and $dy$, the tangent angle changes by $d\theta$, with $\tan\theta = f'(x)$.}
\label{fig:curvature_derivation_geometry}
\end{figure}

We can express $\kappa$ through the derivatives of $f$ (Figure~\ref{fig:curvature_derivation_geometry}). We start from what we know: the slope of the curve is $f'(x) = \tan\theta$, so the angle is obtained by inverting the tangent,
\begin{equation}
\theta = \arctan\big(f'(x)\big).
\label{eq:theta_arctan}
\end{equation}
Since we are interested in how this angle varies, we differentiate with respect to $x$. Recalling that $\frac{d}{dx}\arctan u(x) = \frac{u'(x)}{1 + u(x)^2}$, by the chain rule
\begin{equation}
\frac{d\theta}{dx} = \frac{f''(x)}{1 + [f'(x)]^2}.
\label{eq:dtheta_dx}
\end{equation}
Curvature, however, is defined per unit of arc length, not per unit of $x$. For a small displacement along the curve, by Pythagoras, $ds = \sqrt{dx^2 + dy^2}$, and since $dy = f'(x)\,dx$,
\begin{equation}
ds = \sqrt{dx^2 + [f'(x)\,dx]^2} = \sqrt{1 + [f'(x)]^2}\,dx \quad \Longrightarrow \quad \frac{ds}{dx} = \sqrt{1 + [f'(x)]^2}.
\label{eq:ds_dx}
\end{equation}
Finally we put the two pieces together, using $\frac{d\theta}{ds} = \frac{d\theta/dx}{ds/dx}$:
\begin{equation}
\frac{d\theta}{ds} = \frac{f''(x)}{1 + [f'(x)]^2}\cdot\frac{1}{\sqrt{1 + [f'(x)]^2}},
\end{equation}
which gives the curvature of a curve $y = f(x)$:
\begin{equation}
\boxed{\kappa(x) = \frac{f''(x)}{\left(1 + [f'(x)]^2\right)^{3/2}}}
\label{eq:curvature_final_formula}
\end{equation}
Curvature is thus defined locally through the first and second derivatives of the model, another reason why derivatives are useful to understand the trend of the data. Exercise~\ref{ex:membrane_curvature} is the practical version of this problem.

\section{Model Complexity and Overfitting}
\label{sec:overfitting}

Does a more complex model always give a better description of the data? No. Physicists like to quote \emph{von Neumann's elephant}: ``with four parameters I can fit an elephant, and with five I can make him wiggle his trunk''. The expression describes a model with so many parameters that it is overfit: it will match any set of experimental data, and is therefore unfalsifiable and unscientific.

\begin{figure}[!htbp]
\centering
\includegraphics[page=33, width=0.7\linewidth, keepaspectratio]{\pdf}
\caption{Overfitting. Noisy data following an approximately linear trend are fitted with a linear function (green) and a high-degree polynomial (red), which passes through all the points but oscillates unrealistically between them.}
\label{fig:overfitting_elephant_comparison}
\end{figure}

Figure~\ref{fig:overfitting_elephant_comparison} shows what happens. Noisy data with an approximately linear trend are fitted with a straight line and with a high-degree polynomial. The polynomial has enough free parameters to pass through all the points, giving an almost perfect fit to the training data, but it also captures the random fluctuations due to noise and produces large, unrealistic oscillations between the observations. This is \textbf{overfitting}: the model reproduces the training data very accurately but fails to capture the underlying physical relationship, and can give unreliable predictions for new observations. The simpler linear model does not fit every point, but it represents the overall trend better and is more likely to generalise. A model that fits the data perfectly does not necessarily describe the underlying process well.

To check whether the model complexity is close to optimal, we compare two errors. The \textbf{fitting (training) error} measures how well the model reproduces the observations used to estimate its parameters, for example through the mean squared error
\begin{equation}
\boxed{\text{MSE}_{\text{train}} = \frac{1}{N_{\text{train}}}\sum_{i=1}^{N_{\text{train}}}(y_i - \hat{y}_i)^2}
\label{eq:mse_training}
\end{equation}
where $y_i$ are the measured values and $\hat{y}_i$ the predictions of the fitted model. The \textbf{prediction (validation) error} measures how accurately the fitted model predicts independent observations that were not used to estimate the parameters:
\begin{equation}
\boxed{\text{MSE}_{\text{val}} = \frac{1}{N_{\text{val}}}\sum_{i=1}^{N_{\text{val}}}(y_i - \hat{y}_i)^2}
\label{eq:mse_validation}
\end{equation}
Increasing the complexity generally reduces the fitting error. Beyond a certain point, however, the model starts to fit the noise rather than the underlying relationship, and the validation error increases (Figure~\ref{fig:bias_variance_sweet_spot}).

\begin{figure}[!htbp]
\centering
\includegraphics[page=34, width=0.7\linewidth, keepaspectratio]{\pdf}
\caption{Fitting (training) error and validation error as a function of model complexity: the fitting error keeps decreasing, while the validation error starts to grow when the model begins to fit the noise.}
\label{fig:bias_variance_sweet_spot}
\end{figure}

In practice the check works as follows. We use part of the observations (for instance 50 training points) to fit the model and compute the fitting error. Then we randomly select a subset (say 20 observations) from the held-out measurements and compute the validation error; we shuffle the held-out dataset and repeat with different random subsets. Finally, we compare the average validation errors of models with different numbers of free parameters to identify an appropriate complexity and avoid overfitting. A good model should not only fit the training data, but also give consistently accurate predictions across different validation subsets.

\section{Reporting the Result and Checking the Figures}
\label{sec:reporting_result}

The result of a series of measurements is the take-home message of the whole procedure. It may be a mean value with its unit and uncertainty, a percentage, or even a simple yes/no outcome. What matters is that it is clearly defined and accompanied by the information needed to interpret it: the measurement conditions, the unit and an estimate of the uncertainty. Take the thickness of a metal bar reported as
\begin{equation*}
D = (2.50 \pm 0.03)\text{ mm}.
\end{equation*}
Here $2.50$ is the numerical value, $0.03$ the estimated uncertainty and mm the unit, but the result must be read together with the measurement information: it is the mean of 100 repeated measurements on the same sample under the same conditions, taken with a caliper of resolution $0.01\text{ mm}$ at room temperature.

To sum up, when we prepare a figure we should check a few points. What is its main purpose: comparing categories, showing distributions, showing images? What is the structure of the dataset, and are the axis labels, legend and title clear? What information should the reader see, and what does the plot hide? Which mathematical tools could help us show what is happening or extract parameters, as discussed in Sections~\ref{sec:why_approximate}--\ref{sec:overfitting}? And does the final result have a clear value, unit and uncertainty, with a description of the underlying criteria? A figure is part of the scientific argument: we should show individual data whenever possible, especially for small $n$, because bar and line graphs can hide the distribution, the outliers and the pairing; every plot needs units, $n$ and the meaning of the error bars.

% =============================================================================
% CHAPTER 3: SENSORS AND MEASUREMENT SYSTEMS
% =============================================================================
\chapter{Sensors and Measurement Systems}
\label{ch:sensors}

Every measurement relies on some apparatus that interacts with the quantity we want to know and turns it into a readable value. In this chapter we first define the elements involved (instruments, transducers, sensors and measurement systems), then we describe the sensor response, first in static conditions and then when the input changes in time, and finally we go through the characteristics that specify the performance of a sensor.

\section{Instruments, Transducers and Sensors}
\label{sec:instruments}

A \textbf{measurement instrument} is a complete device, or an assembled set of devices, that converts the interaction with a \textbf{quantity under measurement} (QUM), such as a temperature, a pressure, a light intensity or a current, into a readable value, to which the user must associate a unit and an uncertainty according to a defined operating principle and calibration. A complete instrument may include mechanical, optical or electronic components, software, and a display or data-output system. Its behaviour is described by properties such as the type of response, the operating range, the sensitivity and the resolution, which we will define in Section~\ref{sec:sensor_characteristics}.

A \textbf{transducer} is a device that establishes a specified relation between input and output quantities and typically converts a signal from one form into another. A microphone is a good example: it converts sound waves (pressure) into an electrical signal (a voltage or a current), so it acts as an \emph{input} device that detects sound, i.e.\ as a \textbf{sensor}. A loudspeaker does the opposite: it converts an electrical signal into mechanical motion, so it is an \textbf{actuator} (Figure~\ref{fig:transducer_sensor_actuator}).

\begin{figure}[!htbp]
\centering
\includegraphics[page=35, width=\linewidth, keepaspectratio]{\pdf}
\caption{A transducer converts a signal from one form into another. A microphone (input device) is a sensor, converting sound pressure into an electrical signal; a loudspeaker (output device) is an actuator, converting an electrical signal into mechanical motion.}
\label{fig:transducer_sensor_actuator}
\end{figure}

There is no unique definition of a sensor. The VIM (International Vocabulary of Metrology) defines it as the element of a measuring system that is directly affected by the phenomenon, body or substance carrying the quantity being measured. More generally, a sensor is the input element of a measurement system: it interacts with the QUM and converts its changes (the information about it) into a signal that can be processed further, typically an electrical signal. Different input quantities mean different stimuli, and therefore different types of sensors. The sensor is often only the first element of a much larger instrument.

These three concepts overlap without coinciding. A measurement instrument receives information related to a physical quantity and provides an output that can be interpreted as a measurement result; it may or may not contain a transducer. Sensors are a subset of transducers, those specifically used to detect or measure a physical quantity. Not all transducers are sensors: an incandescent lamp or a laser converts electrical energy into light, so it is a transducer but not a sensor, whereas a photodetector converts incident light into an electrical signal and is both a transducer and a sensor. Transducers can therefore be used both inside and outside measurement chains. When a transducer is part of an instrument, it converts the input quantity into another physical quantity or signal, often electrical, which can then be amplified, conditioned, digitised, processed, and displayed or stored: a transducer converts information.

\subsection{Classifying sensors}

Sensors can be classified according to whether they need an external excitation. A \textbf{passive} (self-generating) sensor produces an electrical output by drawing energy from the QUM itself, with no external excitation. The thermocouple is the classic example: a temperature difference between its two junctions generates a voltage, thanks to the presence of two different conductors, without any external source ($\Delta T \to V_{\text{thermocouple}}$). An \textbf{active} sensor, instead, requires an external excitation. A thermoresistance (RTD) changes its electrical resistance with temperature, but to measure that resistance we need, for instance, an excitation current $I_{\text{exc}}$: $T \to R(T)$, followed by $I_{\text{exc}} \times R(T) \to V_{\text{meas}}$. Note that in remote sensing the words have a different meaning: ``active'' means that the system emits a probing signal, ``passive'' that it only receives radiation or signals.

Sensors also differ in the reference they use. An \textbf{absolute} measurement uses a defined physical reference or datum: in a thermistor, the resistance is directly related to the thermodynamic temperature of the sensor. A \textbf{relative} measurement determines a difference with respect to another value or reference state: in a thermocouple the voltage depends on the temperature gradient across the sensor, so we get no absolute information on the temperature, only on its difference. Absolute does not mean error-free or perfectly accurate; it only describes the reference convention. An absolute-pressure sensor still needs calibration and has uncertainty, drift, and so on.

\subsection{Measurement systems and measurement chains}

A \textbf{measurement system} is the complete set of components that converts a stimulus related to a QUM into a usable measurement result. Most modern systems include a sensor, although it is not strictly required in every case. A typical system contains a sensor; a stage of signal pre-processing and conditioning (amplification, filtering, impedance matching, and any transformation needed to make the signal suitable for the next stage); an analogue-to-digital conversion; and digital processing and/or display. It may therefore combine physical sensing principles, mechanical and electronic components, and signal-processing elements.

\begin{figure}[h]
\centering
\includegraphics[page=36, width=\linewidth, keepaspectratio]{\pdf}
\caption{The measurement chain: the sequence of stages through which the information about the input stimulus flows, from sensing to processing and display (from Kock's book).}
\label{fig:measurement_chain_sequence}
\end{figure}

The \textbf{measurement chain} is the functional sequence of steps through which information about the input stimulus is transformed into a measurement result (Figure~\ref{fig:measurement_chain_sequence}): it describes how information flows through the system, from sensing to processing and final display. The VIM defines a measuring chain as the sequence of elements constituting a signal path from a sensor to an output element. In a real system the sensor is only one stage, and the chain may include excitation, multiple sensors, amplifier, filter, multiplexer, sample-and-hold circuit, ADC, digital register, scaling, compensation algorithms, display and storage. Calibration can be performed stage by stage, end to end, or both.

\section{The Static Sensor Response}
\label{sec:static_response}

Let us now treat the sensor as a ``black box'' and consider only the relationship between its output electrical signal and the input stimulus, without looking at its internal working principle. Can we describe this relationship mathematically? We first need to distinguish two situations. The \textbf{ideal response} is the input--output relationship of an ideal, infinitely fast sensor, with no noise or other effects. The \textbf{dynamic response} describes how the output evolves in time when the input changes: a real sensor may not respond instantaneously, introducing delays, transients or distortions, and its output may keep changing even after the input has become constant. We start from the first, and deal with the second in Section~\ref{sec:lti}.

\subsection{Transfer function and sensitivity}

The input--output relationship is described by the \textbf{transfer function} (TF), a function
\begin{equation}
\boxed{y = f(x)}
\label{eq:static_transfer_function}
\end{equation}
where $x$ is the input quantity (the stimulus) and $y$ the output signal (a voltage, a current, digital counts\dots). The transfer function tells us how the output changes when the input changes, and knowing it allows us to estimate the unknown input from the measured output.

Transfer functions can be approximated by linear or non-linear analytical descriptions. Within an interval around a reference point $x_0$, the transfer function can be approximated linearly as
\begin{equation}
\boxed{y = A + B(x - x_0)}
\label{eq:taylor_linearization}
\end{equation}
where $A = f(x_0)$ is the output at the reference point and the slope $B$ encodes how much $y$ varies as a function of $x$. In this case the \textbf{sensitivity} is $B$, the first derivative of the transfer function:
\begin{equation}
\boxed{S = \frac{dy}{dx}}
\label{eq:sensitivity_definition}
\end{equation}
But beware: the response of a sensor is not necessarily linear. Depending on the sensing mechanism, it may be described by the same logarithmic, exponential or power-law models of Table~\ref{tab:functional_models}, and for a non-linear relationship the sensitivity depends on the input, i.e.\ it is different at different points $x_i$.

A non-linear transfer function can also be simplified with a \textbf{piecewise-linear approximation}: its curved segments are replaced by straight lines joining a set of sample points, called \textbf{knots}. The number and spacing of the knots should be chosen to keep the approximation error $\delta$ within an acceptable limit; they do not need to be equally spaced, and should be closer to each other where the non-linearity is high and farther apart where it is small. A smoother alternative is the \textbf{spline approximation}: each segment between two knots is fitted with a low-degree polynomial, and all the pieces are ``glued'' together into a smooth curve. Spline interpolation fits low-degree polynomials to small subsets of the values, instead of fitting a single high-degree polynomial to all of them at once.

\subsection{From the output back to the input}

In a measurement, the stimulus $x$ is generally unknown, while the output $y$ can be measured. If the transfer function is known and invertible, we can estimate the input with the inverse function,
\begin{equation}
\boxed{x = f^{-1}(y)}
\label{eq:inverse_transfer_function}
\end{equation}
using the inverse forms of Table~\ref{tab:functional_models}. An inverse transfer function exists only when the input--output relationship is one-to-one over the measurement range.

Let us see an example. A Pt100 is a thermoresistance whose resistance $R$ depends on temperature $T$ approximately as
\begin{equation}
\boxed{R(T) = R_0\left[1 + \alpha(T - T_0)\right]}
\label{eq:pt100_linear_model}
\end{equation}
with $R_0 = 100.00\,\Omega$, $T_0 = 0\;^\circ\text{C}$ and $\alpha \approx 0.00385\;^\circ\text{C}^{-1}$. If we measure $R = 119.25\,\Omega$, we recover the temperature by inverting the function: $R/R_0 = 1 + \alpha(T - T_0)$, so
\begin{equation}
\boxed{T = T_0 + \frac{R/R_0 - 1}{\alpha}}
\label{eq:pt100_inversion}
\end{equation}
and $T = 0 + (119.25/100 - 1)/0.00385 = 50\;^\circ\text{C}$. Remember that a Pt100 is a resistive sensor and does not generate a voltage directly: the resistance must be measured with an excitation current or another circuit. Applying a constant current $I$, the voltage is $V(T) = IR_0[1 + \alpha(T - T_0)]$, and the actual chain is $T \to R(T)$ (Pt100) $\to V = IR(T)$ (conditioning) $\to T = f^{-1}(V)$ (estimated input).

\subsection{Calibration}
\label{sec:calibration}

How do we determine the transfer function of a sensor in practice? Through \textbf{calibration}. Several known input stimuli are paired with the corresponding output responses over the whole span of the sensor, and each input--output pair defines a calibration point. These pairs are then fitted with a mathematical model of the transfer function to compute its parameters. Once the parameters are established and stored, the sensor is ready for use: the calibration function can compute any stimulus in the desired range, not only at the calibration points but anywhere in between. Calibration thus provides the experimental link between known inputs and sensor outputs, which allows us to estimate unknown inputs in future measurements. The two phases are mirror images of each other: during calibration we apply known reference inputs $x_i$, measure the outputs $y_i$ and establish $y = f(x)$; during measurement we measure the output $y$ and use $x = f^{-1}(y)$ to estimate the unknown input.

\begin{figure}[h]
\centering
\includegraphics[page=38, width=0.62\linewidth, keepaspectratio]{\pdf}
\caption{Effect of a calibration error (from Fraden). The thick line is the real response, the thin line the calibrated one: the first calibration point is correct, but the second contains an output error $\Delta$, so the fitted line has the wrong slope and intercept.}
\label{fig:calibration_error_propagation}
\end{figure}

Even so, the estimated transfer function may be inaccurate, for three reasons. First, \emph{calibration errors}: the reference inputs and the measured outputs are themselves uncertain, and errors in the calibration points lead to wrong parameters. Second, the \emph{model approximation}: the chosen model must describe the response adequately over the intended range, otherwise it introduces systematic deviations. Third, \emph{operating conditions and validity}: a calibration is valid only within specified input ranges and conditions, and temperature changes, ageing and drift may alter the response over time. An error in the calibration propagates into the estimated transfer function and therefore into all subsequent measurements (Figure~\ref{fig:calibration_error_propagation}). For a linear calibration based on two points $s_1$ and $s_2$, if the second output is underestimated by $\Delta$, the estimated slope differs from the correct one by
\begin{equation}
\boxed{\Delta m = -\frac{\Delta}{s_2 - s_1}}
\label{eq:delta_m_error}
\end{equation}
Calibration still leaves room for calibration errors, which may be reported by the manufacturer. In most cases the mathematical models of transfer functions are smooth and monotonic; they very rarely contain singularities, and when they do, the singularities are the useful phenomena employed for sensing (an ionising particle detector is an example).

\section{Dynamic Response of Linear and Time-Invariant Sensors}
\label{sec:lti}

All sensors need some time to respond to a stimulus. What happens if the input changes rapidly, and what does ``rapidly'' mean? A real sensor does not respond instantaneously: its output is delayed by its finite response time. To describe this dynamic behaviour we must consider both the input $x(t)$ and the output $y(t)$ as functions of time, and unlike the ideal response, the dynamic response depends on how fast the input varies.

We restrict ourselves to \textbf{linear and time-invariant} (LTI) sensors, because only for these can the response be fully characterised by a transfer function. A sensor is \textbf{time-invariant} if its input--output behaviour does not depend on the time instant: delaying the input produces an equally delayed output, without changing its shape. If $x(t)$ produces $y(t)$, then
\begin{equation}
\boxed{x(t - t_0) \longrightarrow y(t - t_0)}
\label{eq:lti_time_invariance}
\end{equation}
The sensor responds in exactly the same way, regardless of when the stimulus is applied. Time-invariant does not mean static: input and output may both vary with time, giving a dynamic response; it only means that the behaviour does not depend explicitly on \emph{when} the input is applied. A sensor is \textbf{linear} if, for inputs $x_a(t)$ and $x_b(t)$ and a constant $k$,
\begin{equation}
\boxed{y(x_a + x_b) = y(x_a) + y(x_b), \qquad y(kx) = k\,y(x)}
\label{eq:lti_linearity}
\end{equation}
Remember that a product such as $y(x_a)\cdot y(x_b)$ or $y(x_a \cdot x_b)$ is not linear.

\subsection{Zero-, first- and second-order sensors}

Because of the finite response time, a sudden change of the stimulus requires a settling time before a stable reading is obtained. For an LTI sensor the relationship between input and output is described by a differential equation, whose order determines the order of the dynamic model (Figure~\ref{fig:zero_vs_first_order}). A \textbf{zero-order} sensor is an ideal sensor whose output is linked to the input by an algebraic relationship, with no differential terms: its output follows the input instantaneously,
\begin{equation}
\boxed{Ay(t) = x(t)}
\label{eq:zero_order_sensor}
\end{equation}
A \textbf{first-order} sensor is described by a first-order differential equation and is typically characterised by a single time constant:
\begin{equation}
\boxed{B\frac{dy(t)}{dt} + Ay(t) = x(t)}
\label{eq:first_order_sensor_ode}
\end{equation}
More complex sensors may need higher-order models. A \textbf{second-order} sensor, for example, can show oscillations and damping when the input changes suddenly:
\begin{equation}
\boxed{C\frac{d^2y(t)}{dt^2} + B\frac{dy(t)}{dt} + Ay(t) = x(t)}
\label{eq:second_order_sensor_ode}
\end{equation}

\begin{figure}[h]
\centering
\includegraphics[page=39, width=\linewidth, keepaspectratio]{\pdf}
\caption{Response to a sudden input change: a zero-order sensor responds instantaneously, a first-order sensor responds gradually.}
\label{fig:zero_vs_first_order}
\end{figure}

To solve these equations easily and describe $y(t)$, we will move to the Laplace domain. Before that, let us see what we can already do with periodic signals.

\subsection{Periodic signals and frequency response}

Fourier tells us that any periodic signal, $f(x + P) = f(x)$ with $P$ a non-zero constant called the period (provided its integral exists and it is piecewise continuous, with reasonably smooth derivatives), can be approximated by a \textbf{Fourier series}, a superposition of sines and cosines with angular frequencies $\omega_n = n\omega_0$:
\begin{equation}
\boxed{f(t) = \frac{a_0}{2} + \sum_{n=1}^{\infty}\left[a_n\cos(n\omega_0t) + b_n\sin(n\omega_0t)\right]}
\label{eq:fourier_series_expansion}
\end{equation}
For example, a square wave of amplitude $\pm A$ and fundamental angular frequency $\omega_0 = 2\pi/T$ contains only odd sine harmonics,
\begin{equation}
f(t) = \frac{4A}{\pi}\sum_{n=0}^{\infty}\frac{\sin\big((2n+1)\omega_0t\big)}{2n+1},
\label{eq:square_wave}
\end{equation}
and it is approximated better and better by adding the first harmonic $\frac{4A}{\pi}\sin\omega_0t$, then $\frac{4A}{\pi}\frac{\sin 3\omega_0t}{3}$, then $\frac{4A}{\pi}\frac{\sin 5\omega_0t}{5}$, and so on. Using $e^{i\theta} = \cos\theta + i\sin\theta$, so that $\cos\theta = (e^{i\theta} + e^{-i\theta})/2$ and $\sin\theta = (e^{i\theta} - e^{-i\theta})/2i$, the series can be written in complex form, $f(t) = \sum_{n=-\infty}^{+\infty}c_ne^{in\omega_0t}$. The \textbf{Fourier transform} and its inverse move a signal between the time domain and the frequency domain (its frequency spectrum):
\begin{equation}
\boxed{X(\omega) = \int_{-\infty}^{+\infty}x(t)e^{-i\omega t}\,dt, \qquad x(t) = \frac{1}{2\pi}\int_{-\infty}^{+\infty}X(\omega)e^{i\omega t}\,d\omega}
\label{eq:fourier_transform_integral}
\end{equation}

Why is this interesting for sensors? Imagine applying a sinusoidal stimulus $x(t) = A_x\sin(2\pi ft)$, with amplitude $A_x$ and frequency $f$. After any initial transient has disappeared, a stable LTI sensor produces a sinusoidal output at the \emph{same frequency}, but generally with a different amplitude and phase (Figure~\ref{fig:sinusoidal_response}):
\begin{equation}
\boxed{y(t) = A_y\sin(2\pi ft + \phi)}
\label{eq:steady_state_sinusoidal_response}
\end{equation}

\begin{figure}[!htbp]
\centering
\includegraphics[page=42, width=0.72\linewidth, keepaspectratio]{\pdf}
\caption{A stable LTI sensor responds to a sinusoidal input $x(t) = A_x\sin(2\pi ft)$ with a sinusoid of the same frequency, but with a different amplitude and phase, $y(t) = A_y\sin(2\pi ft + \phi)$.}
\label{fig:sinusoidal_response}
\end{figure}

An RC filter, the basic example of an LTI system, shows this clearly: it attenuates and phase-shifts any input sine depending only on its frequency, and at higher frequencies the amplitude attenuation and the phase shift both grow. Since a periodic signal is simply the sum of many sines, an LTI system just changes the amplitude and phase of each sine component of a complex signal. The transfer function for periodic signals is therefore
\begin{equation}
H(\omega) = \frac{Y(\omega)}{X(\omega)}, \qquad |H(\omega)| = \frac{A_{\text{out}}(\omega)}{A_{\text{in}}(\omega)}, \qquad \varphi(\omega) = \arg H(\omega) = \arctan\frac{\operatorname{Im}H(\omega)}{\operatorname{Re}H(\omega)},
\label{eq:tf_periodic}
\end{equation}
where $X(\omega)$ and $Y(\omega)$ are the Fourier representations of input and output; for the RC filter, $H(\omega) = 1/(1 + i\omega RC)$, with $|H(\omega)| = 1/\sqrt{1 + (\omega RC)^2}$ and $\varphi(\omega) = -\arctan(\omega RC)$.

\subsection{The Laplace transform and the transfer function}

Can Fourier series describe even the simplest sensor responses? No: the response to a step (a Heaviside function) or to a pulse is \emph{non-periodic}. We need a set of functions able to describe both periodic and non-periodic signals. Combining the oscillating part $\sin(\omega t) = \operatorname{Im}e^{i\omega t}$ with a decaying exponential $e^{-\sigma t}$, as in $y(t) = A_0e^{-\sigma t}\sin(\omega t)$, gives a basis of functions $e^{-(\sigma + i\omega)t} = e^{-st}$ that depend on the complex variable $s = \sigma + i\omega$, called the \textbf{complex frequency}. The \textbf{Laplace transform} of a function $f(t)$ defined for $t \ge 0$ is
\begin{equation}
\boxed{F(s) = \int_0^{\infty}f(t)\,e^{-st}\,dt}
\label{eq:laplace_transform_def}
\end{equation}
and it converts the input $x(t)$ and the output $y(t)$ into functions $X(s)$ and $Y(s)$. Its main advantage is that differentiation and integration in the time domain become multiplication and division in the Laplace domain, so the differential equations describing the sensor become algebraic equations, which are much easier to solve. Assuming $y(0) = y'(0) = 0$, the first- and second-order equations become
\begin{equation}
BsY(s) + AY(s) = X(s), \qquad Cs^2Y(s) + BsY(s) + AY(s) = X(s).
\label{eq:laplace_odes}
\end{equation}

The \textbf{impulse response} $h(t)$ is the output of an LTI sensor in response to an ideal unit impulse (a Dirac delta $\delta(t)$), with zero initial conditions. Knowing it, we can predict the output for any input, because the output is the \textbf{convolution} of the input with $h$:
\begin{equation}
\boxed{y(t) = h(t) * x(t) = \int_0^t h(t - u)\,x(u)\,du}
\label{eq:convolution}
\end{equation}
The \textbf{transfer function} $H(s)$ of an LTI sensor is the Laplace transform of its impulse response. Since convolution in time becomes multiplication in the Laplace domain, the transfer function is also the ratio between the transforms of output and input, with zero initial conditions:
\begin{equation}
\boxed{H(s) = \mathcal{L}\{h(t)\} = \frac{Y(s)}{X(s)}}
\label{eq:transfer_function_definition}
\end{equation}
The impulse response and the transfer function are two equivalent descriptions of an LTI sensor, one in the time domain and one in the Laplace domain. $H(s)$ describes the dynamic behaviour of the sensor independently of the particular input signal, and it contains all the information needed to describe how the sensor responds to time-varying inputs: not only how much the output changes with the input (the gain), but also transients and delays. Once $H(s)$ is known and the input is specified, we compute $Y(s) = H(s)X(s)$ and use the inverse Laplace transform to recover $y(t)$. The transform pair we will need most is
\begin{equation}
\mathcal{L}\{e^{-at}\} = \frac{1}{s + a}.
\label{eq:laplace_pair_exp}
\end{equation}

\subsection{A first-order example: the RC filter and the reconstruction of the input}
\label{sec:rc_filter}

\begin{figure}[h]
\centering
\includegraphics[page=40, width=\linewidth, keepaspectratio]{\pdf}
\caption{Impulse response $h(t) = \frac{K}{\tau}e^{-t/\tau}$ and step response $y(t) = K\Delta x(1 - e^{-t/\tau})$ of a first-order sensor.}
\label{fig:first_order_transient_responses}
\end{figure}

A low-pass RC filter obeys $V_{\text{in}}(t) = RC\,\frac{dV_{\text{out}}(t)}{dt} + V_{\text{out}}(t)$, which for a sensor with gain $A$ we write as $Ax(t) = RC\,\frac{dy(t)}{dt} + y(t)$. In the Laplace domain this becomes $RCsY(s) + Y(s) = AX(s)$, i.e.\ $Y(s)(RCs + 1) = AX(s)$, and with the time constant $\tau = RC$ the transfer function is
\begin{equation}
\boxed{H(s) = \frac{Y(s)}{X(s)} = \frac{A}{1 + s\tau}}
\label{eq:first_order_transfer_function}
\end{equation}
Since $\frac{A}{1 + s\tau} = \frac{A/\tau}{s + 1/\tau}$, the pair~\eqref{eq:laplace_pair_exp} gives immediately the \textbf{impulse response}
\begin{equation}
\boxed{h(t) = \frac{A}{\tau}e^{-t/\tau}}
\label{eq:impulse_response_first_order}
\end{equation}
Now suppose that at $t = 0$ the input suddenly increases by $\Delta x$ and then remains constant: this is a \textbf{step input}, $x(t) = \Delta x\,u(t)$, where $u(t)$ is $0$ for $t < 0$ and $1$ for $t \ge 0$. Its Laplace transform is $X(s) = \Delta x/s$. We know both the transfer function and the input, so
\begin{equation}
Y(s) = H(s)X(s) = \frac{A}{1 + \tau s}\cdot\frac{\Delta x}{s} = \frac{A\Delta x}{s(1 + \tau s)}.
\end{equation}
We decompose the fraction into two simpler terms, $\frac{1}{s(1 + \tau s)} = \frac{1}{s} - \frac{1}{s + 1/\tau}$, so that $Y(s) = A\Delta x\left(\frac{1}{s} - \frac{1}{s + 1/\tau}\right)$. Applying the inverse transforms we obtain the \textbf{step response}
\begin{equation}
\boxed{y(t) = A\Delta x\left(1 - e^{-t/\tau}\right), \qquad t \ge 0}
\label{eq:first_order_step_response}
\end{equation}
The inverse Laplace transform allows us to predict the time response of the sensor to a known stimulus (Figure~\ref{fig:first_order_transient_responses}). Note that the shape of a transient response alone does not identify the device (low-pass or high-pass filter): the response depends both on the input waveform and on the transfer function.

Often we have the opposite problem: we know the output and want to reconstruct the unknown input. If the transfer function is known we can invert it, $X(s) = Y(s)/H(s)$. For a first-order sensor with $H(s) = A/(1 + \tau s)$ this gives $X(s) = \frac{1 + \tau s}{A}Y(s)$, and in the time domain, with the appropriate initial conditions,
\begin{equation}
\boxed{x(t) = \frac{1}{A}\left[y(t) + \tau\frac{dy(t)}{dt}\right]}
\label{eq:input_reconstruction}
\end{equation}
This general result shows how a changing input can be reconstructed from the sensor output and its rate of change, not only for a known step input. Ignoring the dynamics and using the ideal zero-order relation $x = y/A$ can lead to large errors: in the Pt100 example worked out in Exercise~\ref{ex:recover_input}, reading the sensor $2\text{ s}$ after a temperature step with the ideal relation underestimates the change by about 37\%. Knowing the ideal transfer characteristic is not enough when the input changes rapidly: the dynamic response must be taken into account.

\subsection{A second-order example: the mass--spring--damper force sensor}
\label{sec:second_order}

Consider a force sensor in which an applied force $F(t)$ (the input) causes the displacement $x(t)$ (the response) of a mass $M$ connected to a spring of stiffness $k$. The mass provides inertia, while the spring produces a restoring force, so the sensor can oscillate when the force changes suddenly; a damping element with coefficient $d$ dissipates mechanical energy and reduces these oscillations, helping the sensor reach a stable response. The equation of motion is
\begin{equation}
\boxed{F(t) = M\frac{d^2x}{dt^2} + d\frac{dx}{dt} + kx(t)}
\label{eq:mass_spring_damper_ode}
\end{equation}
Taking the Laplace transform with zero initial conditions, $F(s) = (Ms^2 + ds + k)X(s)$, so the transfer function, which describes how the force is converted into displacement, is
\begin{equation}
\boxed{H(s) = \frac{X(s)}{F(s)} = \frac{1}{Ms^2 + ds + k}}
\label{eq:second_order_tf}
\end{equation}
The response is predicted by the roots of the characteristic equation $Ms^2 + ds + k = 0$,
\begin{equation}
\boxed{s_{1,2} = \frac{-d \pm \sqrt{d^2 - 4Mk}}{2M}}
\label{eq:characteristic_roots_second_order}
\end{equation}
whose nature determines the behaviour (Figure~\ref{fig:second_order_dynamics}). If $d^2 < 4Mk$ the roots are complex conjugate and the system is \textbf{underdamped}: the response oscillates with decaying amplitude before settling. If $d^2 = 4Mk$ there is a repeated negative real root and the system is \textbf{critically damped}: this is the fastest non-oscillatory response, reaching equilibrium rapidly without oscillations. If $d^2 > 4Mk$ there are two distinct negative real roots and the system is \textbf{overdamped}: it reaches equilibrium without oscillations, but more slowly than in the critical case.

\begin{figure}[!htbp]
\centering
\includegraphics[page=41, width=\linewidth, keepaspectratio]{\pdf}
\caption{Response of a second-order sensor: underdamped, critically damped and overdamped.}
\label{fig:second_order_dynamics}
\end{figure}

Can we determine the applied force from the measured displacement? At static equilibrium the force is balanced by the spring, $F = kx$. During a transient, however, inertia and damping also contribute, and the force must be reconstructed from displacement, velocity and acceleration, $F(t) = Mx''(t) + dx'(t) + kx(t)$. Suppose $M = 0.10\text{ kg}$, $d = 1.0\text{ N}\,\text{s/m}$, $k = 100\text{ N/m}$, and that at a given instant $x = 0.002\text{ m}$, $x' = 0.020\text{ m/s}$ and $x'' = 0.50\text{ m/s}^2$. Then
\begin{equation*}
F = 0.10 \times 0.50 + 1.0 \times 0.020 + 100 \times 0.002 = 0.27\text{ N},
\end{equation*}
whereas the static, zero-order approximation would give $F_{\text{static}} = kx = 0.20\text{ N}$, underestimating the force by $0.07\text{ N}$. (The static estimate does not always underestimate the force: it depends on the signs of velocity and acceleration.)

\section{Frequency Response and Time Constant}
\label{sec:frequency_response}

The \textbf{frequency response} is the transfer function evaluated for sinusoidal signals of frequency $f$, i.e.\ at $s = i\omega = i2\pi f$:
\begin{equation}
\boxed{H(i\omega) = H(s)\big|_{s = i\omega}}
\label{eq:freq_resp_sub}
\end{equation}
For an LTI sensor it is characterised by two quantities. The \textbf{gain} (amplitude response) $|H(i2\pi f)| = |Y(i2\pi f)|/|X(i2\pi f)|$ is the magnitude of the frequency response: how much the sensor amplifies or attenuates each frequency component. The \textbf{phase response} $\phi(f) = \arg H(i2\pi f) = \arctan\frac{\operatorname{Im}H}{\operatorname{Re}H}$ tells how much the output is shifted in phase relative to the input.

For a first-order low-pass sensor with unit gain, $H(s) = 1/(1 + s\tau)$, so
\begin{equation}
H(i\omega) = \frac{1}{1 + i\omega\tau} = \frac{1 - i\omega\tau}{1 + (\omega\tau)^2}, \qquad \operatorname{Re}H = \frac{1}{1 + (\omega\tau)^2}, \quad \operatorname{Im}H = \frac{-\omega\tau}{1 + (\omega\tau)^2},
\label{eq:freq_resp_first_order_complex}
\end{equation}
and, since $\omega = 2\pi f$,
\begin{equation}
\boxed{|H(i2\pi f)| = \frac{1}{\sqrt{1 + (2\pi f\tau)^2}}, \qquad \phi(f) = -\arctan(2\pi f\tau)}
\label{eq:magnitude_and_phase_first_order}
\end{equation}

\subsection{Cut-off frequency}

The \textbf{cut-off frequency} $f_c$ is conventionally defined as the frequency at which the amplitude response falls by $3\text{ dB}$ relative to its passband value:
\begin{equation}
\boxed{\frac{|H(f_c)|}{|H|_{\text{passband}}} = \frac{1}{\sqrt{2}} \approx 0.7}
\label{eq:cutoff_magnitude}
\end{equation}
Depending on the sensor we may identify an \textbf{upper cut-off frequency} $f_H$, above which high-frequency components are increasingly attenuated, and a \textbf{lower cut-off frequency} $f_L$, below which low-frequency components are increasingly attenuated.

Why 3 dB? Power and intensity are proportional to the square of the amplitude, $P \propto A^2$, and a power ratio is expressed in decibels as $10\log_{10}(P_{\text{out}}/P_0)$. Let $P_0$ be the reference power in the passband and $H_0$ the corresponding amplitude gain. The cut-off frequency is defined as the frequency at which the output power is half of the reference power, $P(f_c)/P_0 = 0.5$. Since $P(f_c)/P_0 = (|H(f_c)|/H_0)^2$, half power corresponds to an amplitude ratio $|H(f_c)|/H_0 = 1/\sqrt{2} \approx 0.7$, and
\begin{equation}
\boxed{10\log_{10}\frac{1}{2} = 20\log_{10}\frac{1}{\sqrt{2}} \approx -3.01\text{ dB}}
\label{eq:minus_3db_derivation}
\end{equation}
At the cut-off, the power is 50\% and the amplitude 71\% of the passband values.

\subsection{Time constant and cut-off frequency}

The dynamic response can also be described in the time domain. For a first-order low-pass sensor, with zero initial output and a constant input step, the response is
\begin{equation}
\boxed{y(t) = y_\infty\left(1 - e^{-t/\tau}\right)}
\label{eq:step_response_yinf}
\end{equation}
and the time constant $\tau$ characterises how quickly the sensor approaches its steady state: at $t = \tau$ the output is at $1 - e^{-1} \approx 63.2\%$ of $y_\infty$, at $t = 2\tau$ at $86.5\%$, at $t = 3\tau$ at $95.0\%$, and it reaches $90.0\%$ at $t \approx 2.3\tau$. The time constant describes the response to a sudden input change, while the frequency response describes how the sensor attenuates and phase-shifts different frequency components. For a first-order low-pass sensor the two are connected through the upper cut-off frequency:
\begin{equation}
\boxed{f_c = \frac{1}{2\pi\tau} \approx \frac{0.159}{\tau}}
\label{eq:cutoff_tau}
\end{equation}
A smaller time constant therefore means a higher cut-off frequency and a faster sensor.

\subsection{How the frequency response shapes the output}
\label{sec:dynamic_error}

A time-varying signal can be decomposed into frequency components. When a sensor has a limited frequency response, different components are attenuated and phase-shifted by different amounts, so the output may not reproduce the input waveform faithfully. Consider a sudden step in the stimulus. An \emph{ideal} response follows the input instantaneously. A \emph{low-pass} response attenuates the high frequencies and produces a gradual rise. A \emph{high-pass} response attenuates the low frequencies: the output responds to the change but then returns towards zero. A \emph{band-pass} response attenuates both, giving a transient that eventually returns towards zero.

Many sensors are the rate-limiting element of a measurement system. A sudden change in the QUM may require a settling time before a stable reading is obtained, and if the instrument is read too early we get a \emph{dynamic measurement error}, caused by the finite response of the system, which has nothing to do with a static calibration error. The transfer function describes how the sensor responds to time-varying inputs, and accounting for this behaviour is essential to reconstruct rapidly changing stimuli.

\section{Sensor Characteristics and Specifications}
\label{sec:sensor_characteristics}

Beyond the transfer function, several characteristics are needed to describe the performance, operating limits and measurement quality of a sensor: its input/output range and full scale; threshold, saturation and dead band; sensitivity and resolution; repeatability and reliability; inaccuracy; hysteresis; and noise.

\subsection{Range, span and full scale}

The \textbf{input range} is the interval of stimulus values, $x_{\min} \le x \le x_{\max}$, over which a sensor can operate within its specified performance limits (Figure~\ref{fig:range_span_fso}). The \textbf{input span} is the difference between the maximum and minimum inputs, the \textbf{full-scale input} is the upper limit of the specified range (terminology varies between manufacturers), and the \textbf{full-scale output} (FSO) is the difference between the outputs corresponding to the maximum and minimum inputs:
\begin{equation}
\boxed{\text{Input span} = x_{\max} - x_{\min}, \qquad \text{FSO} = y(x_{\max}) - y(x_{\min})}
\label{eq:fso_def}
\end{equation}
The operating range defines which inputs can be measured, while span and FSO describe the extent of the input and output intervals.

\begin{figure}[!htbp]
\centering
\includegraphics[page=43, width=0.65\linewidth, keepaspectratio]{\pdf}
\caption{Input range (span) and full-scale output (FSO) of a sensor.}
\label{fig:range_span_fso}
\end{figure}

For sensors working over a very wide range, spanning several orders of magnitude, the full-scale input range can be characterised by the ratio $P_{\max}/P_{\min}$ expressed in decibels, the \textbf{dynamic range}. A photodetector measuring optical powers from $1\text{ nW}$ to $1\text{ mW}$, for example, has
\begin{equation}
\boxed{\text{DR} = 10\log_{10}\frac{P_{\max}}{P_{\min}}} = 10\log_{10}\frac{10^{-3}}{10^{-9}} = 60\text{ dB}.
\label{eq:dynamic_range_db_calc}
\end{equation}

\subsection{Threshold, saturation and dead band}

A sensor cannot necessarily provide useful information for every input value (Figure~\ref{fig:saturation_and_deadband}). The \textbf{threshold} is the minimum input level, or change, needed to produce a specified detectable response. \textbf{Saturation} is the condition in which increasing the input no longer produces the expected increase in output. A \textbf{dead band} is an interval of input values over which the output remains essentially unchanged. For a sensor with a low-input threshold and high-input saturation, the useful operating region lies between these two limits: for the measurement to be meaningful, the sensor must produce a detectable response without reaching saturation. Saturation is very typical of imaging sensors: we increase the excitation power, but the recorded signal no longer increases. This is not a problem of the sensor, it means we are not operating in the right range, and usually we simply have to decrease the laser power. A dead band is rarer, but it can occur in an imaging system, for instance when a wavelength filter cuts some wavelengths: whatever we send within that range, the sensor does not respond.

\begin{figure}[!htbp]
\centering
\includegraphics[page=44, width=0.8\linewidth, keepaspectratio]{\pdf}
\caption{Threshold and saturation delimit the responsive region of a sensor (top); a dead band is an interval in which the output does not change (bottom).}
\label{fig:saturation_and_deadband}
\end{figure}

\subsection{Sensitivity and resolution}
\label{sec:resolution}

These two properties are often confused, but they are different (Figure~\ref{fig:sensitivity_vs_resolution}). \textbf{Sensitivity} describes how strongly the output changes in response to a change of the input; for a static characteristic $y = f(x)$ it is $S(x) = dy/dx$ (Equation~\eqref{eq:sensitivity_definition}). Only for a sensor with a linear response, $y = A + Bx$, in steady state, is the sensitivity constant over the input range, $S = B$. \textbf{Resolution} is the smallest change in the input that can be distinguished, given the smallest output increment $\Delta y_{\text{LSB}}$ (the least significant bit):
\begin{equation}
\boxed{\Delta x_{\text{res}} \sim \frac{\Delta y_{\text{LSB}}}{S}}
\label{eq:resolution_lsb_formula}
\end{equation}
The actual resolution may be worse, because of noise and other limitations. Sensitivity, then, says how strongly the output changes with the input, resolution says what is the smallest input change we can distinguish, and a highly sensitive sensor does not necessarily have a high resolution. In the real world every sensor has some imperfection, and its real transfer function may be neither linear nor monotonic.

\begin{figure}[!htbp]
\centering
\includegraphics[page=45, width=\linewidth, keepaspectratio]{\pdf}
\caption{Sensitivity (the slope $S = \Delta y/\Delta x$ of the transfer function, left) versus finite resolution (the input step $\Delta x_{\text{res}} = \Delta y_{\text{LSB}}/S$ corresponding to one output quantisation step, right).}
\label{fig:sensitivity_vs_resolution}
\end{figure}

For example, a temperature sensor with $S = 10\text{ mV}/^\circ\text{C}$ read with $\Delta y_{\text{LSB}} = 1\text{ mV}$ has $\Delta x_{\text{LSB}} = 1\text{ mV}/(10\text{ mV}/^\circ\text{C}) = 0.1\;^\circ\text{C}$: a temperature change of $0.1\;^\circ\text{C}$ produces an output change of one digital step. This is the nominal input-equivalent quantisation step, not necessarily the actual resolution, since noise may make the smallest reliably distinguishable change larger.

\subsection{Resolution and measurement uncertainty}
\label{sec:resolution_uncertainty}

How does a finite resolution contribute to the uncertainty? A digital sensor reports values in discrete steps, so even without noise the displayed value may differ slightly from the value before quantisation. If the instrument rounds to the nearest step, the quantisation error $e_q$ is bounded by half the resolution,
\begin{equation}
-\frac{\Delta x_{\text{res}}}{2} \le e_q \le +\frac{\Delta x_{\text{res}}}{2},
\label{eq:quantization_bounds}
\end{equation}
so the maximum absolute rounding error is $\pm\Delta x_{\text{res}}/2$. We assume that the error is uniformly (rectangularly) distributed in this interval, and take as standard uncertainty due to resolution the standard deviation of this uniform distribution:
\begin{equation}
\boxed{u_{\text{res}} = \frac{\Delta x_{\text{res}}}{\sqrt{12}} = \frac{\Delta y_{\text{LSB}}}{S\sqrt{12}}}
\label{eq:standard_uncertainty_resolution_sqrt12}
\end{equation}
Why $\sqrt{12}$? For a continuous random variable with mean $\mu$ and probability density $f(x)$, the variance is $\sigma^2 = \int_{-\infty}^{+\infty}(x - \mu)^2f(x)\,dx$. The uniform distribution of the error is symmetric around zero, so $\mu_e = 0$, and its density is $f(e) = 1/\Delta x$ for $-\Delta x/2 \le e \le \Delta x/2$. The variance is then the mean of the squared errors:
\begin{equation}
\sigma_e^2 = \frac{1}{\Delta x}\int_{-\Delta x/2}^{\Delta x/2}e^2\,de = \frac{1}{\Delta x}\left[\frac{e^3}{3}\right]_{-\Delta x/2}^{\Delta x/2} = \frac{\Delta x^2}{12},
\label{eq:variance_uniform_proof}
\end{equation}
and the standard uncertainty is its square root, $u_{\text{res}} = \sigma_e = \Delta x_{\text{res}}/\sqrt{12}$. Keep in mind that instrument resolution is not the same as measurement uncertainty: resolution is one contribution to the uncertainty budget, together with noise, calibration uncertainty and other effects.

\subsection{Repeatability and reliability}

\textbf{Repeatability} is the ability of a sensor to produce closely agreeing results when the same input is measured repeatedly under the same specified conditions: it describes consistency. \textbf{Reliability} is the ability of a sensor to keep performing its intended function without failure over a period of time and under specified operating conditions (it depends on the environment); it is often given as the \textbf{mean time between failures} (MTBF), which indicates statistically for how long the sensor works consistently. Good repeatability does not guarantee reliability: the sensor might still fail after prolonged operation or repeated thermal cycles. A typical example is an imaging experiment repeated in summer, when the room temperature changes a lot: the sensor may not respond as it did in winter, and the results are no longer reproducible. This is why microscopes are usually kept in temperature-controlled rooms, and why the stability of the sensor matters in very long acquisitions, such as super-resolution experiments lasting hours.

\subsection{Inaccuracy}
\label{sec:inaccuracy}

A very important characteristic of a sensor is its accuracy, which really means its \textbf{inaccuracy} (Figure~\ref{fig:sensor_inaccuracy_envelope}). The inaccuracy $\delta$ is measured as the highest deviation of the value represented by the sensor from the ideal or true value of the stimulus at its input. In other words, it is the discrepancy between the input value computed from the calibration curve and the actual input value, i.e.\ between the actual transfer function and the calibration curve. If we repeat the experiment over and over and every time observe an error of exactly $\delta$ over a certain span, we may say that the sensor has a \emph{systematic} inaccuracy $\delta$ over that span. The band $\Delta$ around the curve represents the manufacturing permissive limits. Inaccuracy can be quantified in several ways; for a thermistor measuring a temperature as a voltage, for instance: in terms of the measured value (e.g.\ $0.1\;^\circ\text{C}$), in absolute terms on the output signal (e.g.\ $0.1\text{ V}$), in relative terms on the full input scale (e.g.\ 1\% of $10\text{ V}$), or in relative terms on the input signal (e.g.\ 1\% of $S$).

\begin{figure}[h]
\centering
\includegraphics[page=46, width=0.5\linewidth, keepaspectratio]{\pdf}
\caption{Inaccuracy $\delta$ between the actual transfer function and the calibration curve, and manufacturing permissive limits $\pm\Delta$ (from Fraden).}
\label{fig:sensor_inaccuracy_envelope}
\end{figure}

The permissive limits exist partly because manufacturers usually do not calibrate every single sensor: relying on a very reproducible production, they calibrate a sample and derive the tolerance from it. Can we improve the inaccuracy of a sensor, then? Yes: instead of relying only on the nominal characteristics and tolerances declared by the manufacturer, we can calibrate each sensor individually under well-defined conditions, possibly repeating the calibration from day to day. With an \emph{individual calibration} we determine the actual input--output relationship using known references, and with a \emph{calibration correction} we use this curve to correct systematic deviations and obtain a more accurate estimate of the input. Does calibration eliminate the uncertainty, then? No. Even after calibration the result remains affected by the uncertainty of the calibration procedure, of the reference standards and of other sensor limitations, $u_{\text{cal}}$. The different contributions should not simply be added: if they are uncorrelated, the final uncertainty of the measurement is
\begin{equation}
\boxed{u_c = \sqrt{u_{\text{res}}^2 + u_{\text{cal}}^2 + u_A^2 + \dots}}
\label{eq:combined_uncertainty_quadrature}
\end{equation}
We will come back to this way of combining uncertainties in Chapter~\ref{ch:errors}.

\subsection{Hysteresis}

The response of a sensor can also depend on the direction from which the input is approached. It happens, for instance, with Hall-effect sensors. \textbf{Hysteresis} occurs when a sensor gives different outputs for the same input, depending on whether the input is reached from increasing or from decreasing values (Figure~\ref{fig:sensor_hysteresis_loop}). Consider, for instance, a displacement sensor with sensitivity $S = 10\text{ mV/mm}$ that, at $x = 10\text{ mm}$, gives $V_\uparrow = 100\text{ mV}$ for increasing displacement and $V_\downarrow = 120\text{ mV}$ for decreasing displacement. The hysteresis deviation in the output is $|V_\uparrow - V_\downarrow| = 20\text{ mV}$, which corresponds to an equivalent input displacement $\Delta x_{\text{hyst}} = \Delta V_{\text{hyst}}/S = 20\text{ mV}/(10\text{ mV/mm}) = 2\text{ mm}$: the same physical displacement gives two readings separated by the equivalent of $2\text{ mm}$. In such cases we may need two calibration curves, one for each direction.

\begin{figure}[h]
\centering
\includegraphics[page=47, width=0.5\linewidth, keepaspectratio]{\pdf}
\caption{Hysteresis loop: the output differs depending on whether the stimulus is increasing or decreasing (from Fraden).}
\label{fig:sensor_hysteresis_loop}
\end{figure}

The last characteristic in the list, \textbf{noise}, is postponed to the part of the course on imaging and positioning sensors, where we discuss it for CMOS cameras (Section~\ref{sec:cmos_characterization}).

% =============================================================================
% CHAPTER 4: MEASUREMENT ERRORS, UNCERTAINTY AND STATISTICS
% =============================================================================
\chapter{Measurement Errors, Uncertainty and Statistics}
\label{ch:errors}

With the previous chapter we have finished describing the measuring instrument and its characteristics. We now move to the last box of the measurement chain, the result, and in particular we learn how to assign an uncertainty to it. We already know from Chapter~\ref{ch:metrology} that a reliable quantification needs a common measurement language, a documented procedure with calibrated sensors and known conditions, and an estimate of the uncertainty. Every physical quantity must, at least in principle, be assigned a numerical value in an unambiguous and objective way, meaning that the value must be reproducible under the same conditions by any observer. This chapter provides the tools to estimate errors and uncertainties: first their sources, then the probability and statistics needed to describe repeated measurements, and finally how uncertainties propagate in indirect measurements. (The professor acknowledges Professor Maurizio Loreti's textbook on error theory as the main source of inspiration for this part of the course.)

\section{Sources of Errors and Uncertainties}
\label{sec:error_sources}

\subsection{Direct and indirect measurements}

The first distinction to make is between direct and indirect measurements, because errors and uncertainties are computed very differently in the two cases. A measurement is \textbf{direct} when the quantity is compared directly with the unit of measurement (the reference standard), or with its multiples or submultiples. In an \textbf{indirect} measurement the quantity of interest is not measured directly: other quantities are measured, and the desired one is obtained through a known functional relationship. For example, we measure a length and a time and then compute a velocity. In direct measurements the errors are mainly associated with the instrument and the procedure, whereas in indirect measurements the errors of the measured quantities propagate through the mathematical relationship used to calculate the result. The uncertainty on the velocity will of course depend on the uncertainties on space and time, but, as we will see in Section~\ref{sec:indirect}, they cannot simply be summed.

\subsection{Precision, trueness and accuracy}

When we move from one measurement to many repeated measurements, we can ask how good they are. The traditional way of putting it distinguished precision and accuracy only, but modern metrology (the VIM) has redefined these terms and introduced a third one.

\textbf{Measurement precision} is the closeness of agreement among repeated measurements of the same or similar quantities under specified conditions: high precision means low dispersion of the values. It answers the question: how reproducible are repeated measurements? \textbf{Measurement trueness} is the closeness of the \emph{mean} of repeated results to a reference value: high trueness means a small systematic deviation (bias). It answers: how large is the bias? Precision does not imply trueness: an instrument may produce nearly identical results every time (high precision) but consistently report a value different from the reference (low trueness). Finally, \textbf{measurement accuracy} is the closeness of a measured value to the true value, considering both systematic and random effects. Good overall accuracy requires both high precision and high trueness. Precision is about the spread, trueness about the systematic bias, accuracy about the closeness to the true value (Figure~\ref{fig:target_bullseye_metrology}).

\begin{figure}[h]
\centering
\includegraphics[page=50, width=0.7\linewidth, keepaspectratio]{\pdf}
\caption{Precision (vertical axis, closeness of repeated measurements) and trueness (horizontal axis, closeness of the average result to the true value). High precision with low trueness gives low accuracy (top left, big systematic error and small random error); low precision with high trueness gives medium accuracy (bottom right, small systematic error and big random error); only high precision and high trueness give high accuracy (top right).}
\label{fig:target_bullseye_metrology}
\end{figure}

The two axes are affected by different types of error. The top-left case of Figure~\ref{fig:target_bullseye_metrology} is the typical case of a big \textbf{systematic error} with a small random error. A systematic error can be eliminated by modifying the instrument or the procedure, or by applying an appropriate correction to the result: if we forgot to set the zero of our instrument, we have a constant offset that is easy to fix. The bottom row shows big \textbf{random errors}. A random error can only be reduced, by improving the characteristics of the instrument or by controlling its operating conditions more strictly, and by performing many measurements; random errors can never be completely eliminated, and they will be present in any kind of repeated measurement.

\subsection{Where do systematic errors come from?}

Systematic errors can arise from many sources, and we should absolutely try to avoid them. The first are \textbf{instrument imperfections or calibration errors}: an incorrect zero offset, a defective or non-uniform scale, the ageing of the instrument. Then come \textbf{incorrect operating conditions}, such as using at $30\;^\circ\text{C}$ an instrument that should work at $10\;^\circ\text{C}$, or at a temperature different from the calibration one, and \textbf{observer bias}, such as consistently incorrect scale readings or parallax errors, when we do not look perpendicularly at the scale. \textbf{External disturbances} may be static, like temperature variations, or dynamic, like mechanical vibrations: if we are acquiring images with nanometric precision next to a passing underground train, we had better wait for the train to pass. The \textbf{interaction between the instrument and the measured system} matters too: measuring a thickness while pressing too hard with the instrument deforms the object and gives a shorter value. Finally, we may use \textbf{incorrect or approximate mathematical models}, neglecting relevant physical effects in an indirect measurement or approximating the sensor response with the wrong function.
Most of the time these problems are not completely decoupled from random errors. Many sources of systematic error can also produce random errors, when their effect varies unpredictably between repeated measurements: mechanical play and friction in the instrument, variable operating conditions (uncontrolled fluctuations of temperature or pressure), observer variability (such as the reaction time when operating a stopwatch), fluctuating external disturbances, variations in the interaction between instrument and object (such as changes in contact force), and intrinsic physical fluctuations (thermal motion, molecular fluctuations, the statistical nature of quantities such as temperature and pressure). The same physical cause may introduce both a systematic component (a reproducible bias) and a random component (unpredictable fluctuations).

\subsection{Systematic versus random errors}

A \textbf{systematic error} produces a consistent deviation of the measured value from the reference value, for example an \emph{offset error}, where the output is shifted by a constant amount, or a \emph{scale-factor error}, where the sensitivity differs from its nominal value (Figure~\ref{fig:error_morphologies_and_random}). Systematic errors introduce a bias and are not eliminated by averaging repeated measurements; they can often be reduced through calibration and correction. Note that they do not necessarily produce a constant offset: a zero-calibration error gives an approximately constant offset, but an incorrect scale factor produces an error that increases with the measured quantity, and a non-linear instrument response produces an error that varies non-linearly across the range.

\textbf{Random errors} cause unpredictable fluctuations in repeated measurements under the same conditions. Their sources include instrumental and electronic noise, fluctuations in the environmental conditions and random variations in the instrument response. If we collect many errors and plot their distribution, it is usually symmetric: sometimes the error is positive, sometimes negative, and its mean value is zero. Random errors are indeed assumed to have zero mean, and their effect on the estimated mean decreases as the number of independent measurements increases. Here we already see that random errors have to do with statistics and probability, which is where we are going next.

\begin{figure}[h]
\centering
\includegraphics[page=52, width=0.7\linewidth, keepaspectratio]{\pdf}
\caption{Systematic errors: an offset error shifts the response by a constant amount, a scale-factor error changes its slope (top). Random errors fluctuate around a zero mean (bottom).}
\label{fig:error_morphologies_and_random}
\end{figure}

\section{Best Estimate, Uncertainty and Significant Figures}
\label{sec:reporting}

The aim of measuring a physical quantity is to determine both the \textbf{best estimate} of the QUM (its numerical value relative to a chosen unit) and the \textbf{uncertainty} associated with that value. The \textbf{measurement uncertainty} is a non-negative parameter characterising the dispersion of the values, around the true value of the QUM, that can reasonably be attributed to the measured quantity. It expresses how well we know our estimate. Every measurement is affected by uncertainty, which reflects our limited knowledge of the value of the quantity.

\subsection{Error versus uncertainty}

Uncertainty must not be confused with error. The \textbf{error} is the difference between the measured value and a reference value: it tells us how far the measurement is from the reference, it can be positive or negative, and we can know it only if a suitable reference is available. The \textbf{uncertainty} is a parameter characterising the dispersion of the values reasonably attributable to the measured quantity: it tells us how well we know the value, it is always non-negative, and it can be evaluated from the experimental data and other available information. Errors can sometimes be identified and corrected, but a measurement result always retains some uncertainty.

For example, suppose a calibrated reference has a value of $10.0\text{ mm}$ while an instrument reads $10.2\text{ mm}$. Relative to that reference the error is $e = 10.2 - 10.0 = +0.2\text{ mm}$. Separately, an uncertainty evaluation might give $x = (10.2 \pm 0.1)\text{ mm}$, where $0.1\text{ mm}$ is an illustrative expanded uncertainty (assuming its coverage factor and probability are specified). It is not the measurement error, and it does not guarantee that the true value lies inside the stated interval: the reference comparison reveals an error of $0.2\text{ mm}$, the uncertainty describes the remaining doubt about the result.

\subsection{Significant figures and rounding}

It is meaningless to report a result with more precision or resolution than the measurement process can achieve. \textbf{Significant figures} are the digits retained in a reported value to express it at a level of precision consistent with the available information, and it is the uncertainty that determines which digits are meaningful. In $R = (2.50 \pm 0.03)\text{ mm}$, for example, the final zero is significant: it says that the result is reported to the nearest $0.01\text{ mm}$. A few general rules follow.

First, the \textbf{uncertainty} is reported with one or two significant digits. The usual convention is: if its first non-zero digit is 1 or 2, keep two significant digits; if it is between 3 and 9, keep one. So $0.147\text{ m} \to 0.15\text{ m}$, $0.0246\text{ s} \to 0.025\text{ s} = 2.5 \times 10^{-2}\text{ s}$, $0.367\text{ m} \to 0.4\text{ m}$, $0.514\;^\circ\text{C} \to 0.5\;^\circ\text{C}$. (Keeping two digits when the first is 1 or 2 is common, but not a strict requirement.)

Second, the \textbf{value} is rounded to the same decimal position as its uncertainty. For example, $L = (10.2 \pm 0.05)\text{ m}$ is wrong and should be $L = (10.20 \pm 0.05)\text{ m}$; $T = (25.347 \pm 0.2)\;^\circ\text{C}$ should be $T = (25.3 \pm 0.2)\;^\circ\text{C}$; $V = (3.5 \pm 0.012)\text{ V}$ should be $V = (3.500 \pm 0.012)\text{ V}$; $t = (1.2345 \pm 0.03)\text{ s}$ should be $t = (1.23 \pm 0.03)\text{ s}$.

Third, we use consistent units and scientific notation when appropriate: $d = (0.00345 \pm 0.00012)\text{ m} = (3.45 \pm 0.12)\text{ mm}$, $f = (1\,250\,000 \pm 30\,000)\text{ Hz} = (1.25 \pm 0.03)\text{ MHz}$, $L = (12\,300 \pm 200)\text{ m} = (1.23 \pm 0.02) \times 10^4\text{ m}$.

How do we eliminate the digits that are not justified by the uncertainty? There are two ways. \textbf{Truncation} discards all the digits beyond a selected decimal place without adjusting the last one; \textbf{rounding} replaces the number with the nearest value at the selected decimal place. If the calculated value is $x = 120.3070065\text{ m}$ with $\Delta x = 0.01\text{ m}$, truncation gives $x = (120.30 \pm 0.01)\text{ m}$, while correct rounding gives $x = (120.31 \pm 0.01)\text{ m}$. We should always round, because truncation can introduce a systematic rounding bias. Moreover, enough digits must be kept during the intermediate calculations, to avoid premature rounding: the result is rounded only at the end, after the final uncertainty has been evaluated.

\section{A Statistical Approach to Measurement}
\label{sec:probability}

Because of unavoidable errors, repeated measurements of the same quantity generally give different results, none of which can be assumed to coincide exactly with the true value. Probability theory gives us a mathematical framework to describe this variability and quantify the uncertainty. Our aim is to estimate the most plausible value of the measured quantity, and an interval of values associated with a specified level of confidence. Not all real numbers are equally plausible: values close to our observations are generally more likely to be consistent with the measured quantity, provided systematic effects have been accounted for. From the experimental data we will be able to determine an interval that has a specified probability of containing the true value.

\subsection{Events, random variables and probability}

Probability theory describes random phenomena: experiments that can give different outcomes even when repeated under nominally identical conditions. The \textbf{outcome space} $S$ is the set of all possible outcomes; an \textbf{event} $E$ is a subset of $S$, corresponding to one or more outcomes; a \textbf{random variable} $X$ is a function that assigns a numerical value to each outcome, and it can be discrete or continuous. When we roll a fair die, $S = \{1, 2, 3, 4, 5, 6\}$, the event ``odd number'' is $E = \{1, 3, 5\}$, and $X$ is the number shown on the die. For equally likely outcomes the classical probability is
\begin{equation}
\boxed{P(E) = \frac{\text{number of favourable outcomes}}{\text{total number of possible outcomes}}}
\label{eq:classical_probability}
\end{equation}
so here $P(E) = 3/6 = 0.5$.

Experimentally, if an experiment is repeated $N$ times and the event $E$ occurs $n$ times, its \textbf{relative frequency} is $f_N(E) = n/N$, and as the number of independent repetitions grows it tends to the probability of the event (under the assumptions of the frequentist model):
\begin{equation}
\boxed{P(E) = \lim_{N \to \infty}\frac{n}{N}}
\label{eq:frequentist_probability}
\end{equation}
Since repeated measurements fluctuate, the numerical outcome of a measurement can be modelled as a random variable.

The \textbf{axiomatic definition} of probability states that $P(E)$ is a number assigned to each event $E \subseteq S$ satisfying three axioms: \emph{non-negativity}, $P(E) \ge 0$ for every event; \emph{normalisation}, $P(S) = 1$, i.e.\ the probability of the certain event is one; and \emph{additivity}: for any finite or countable collection of mutually exclusive events ($E_i \cap E_j = \emptyset$ for $i \neq j$),
\begin{equation}
\boxed{P\Big(\bigcup_i E_i\Big) = \sum_i P(E_i)}
\label{eq:additivity}
\end{equation}
so that $0 \le P(E) \le 1$. Probabilities can be added directly only when the events cannot occur simultaneously. The axioms establish the rules that probabilities must satisfy, but they do not say how probability values are assigned: these can be determined through probabilistic models and compared with experimental observations.

\subsection{Population and sample}
\label{sec:population_sample}

The \textbf{population} is the theoretical set of all the measurement results that could be obtained under the same conditions, i.e.\ the complete set of all possible observations of a quantity $x$; it may be finite or infinite. It is characterised by statistical parameters such as the population mean $\mu$ and the population variance $\text{Var}(x)$, $\sigma_x^2$ or just $\sigma^2$. A \textbf{sample} is a subset of $N$ observations drawn from the population: in our case, a finite set of results obtained by repeating the measurement of the same quantity with the same instrument and procedure.
