It is characterised by the sample mean $\bar{x}$ and the sample variance $s^2$, and its mean provides an estimate of the population mean. We use the statistical properties of a sample to estimate the unknown properties of the population. Different samples from the same population generally give different sample means; increasing the number of independent observations generally improves the precision of the estimated mean; and a representative sample allows us to estimate the properties of the population. Unlike a real population of individuals, this statistical population is a conceptual model, not a physically existing collection of measurements, but it allows us to apply probability theory to experimental measurements and draw conclusions from a finite sample.

\subsection{The law of large numbers}

The empirical definition of probability assumes that the relative frequency of an event approaches a well-defined value as the number of trials increases. Exact convergence, however, is not guaranteed: no finite number of trials ensures that the observed frequency will be arbitrarily close to the theoretical probability. What holds is a \textbf{statistical convergence}: as the number of trials increases, the probability of observing a significant deviation from the theoretical probability becomes smaller and smaller. If $E$ has probability $P(E)$ and its relative frequency after $N$ trials is $f_N(E)$, then for any $\varepsilon > 0$
\begin{equation}
\boxed{P\big(|f_N(E) - P(E)| \ge \varepsilon\big) \xrightarrow[N \to \infty]{} 0}
\label{eq:lln_frequency}
\end{equation}
This is the \textbf{law of large numbers} (Bernoulli's theorem): within the axiomatic framework, the observed relative frequency approaches the theoretical probability in a statistical sense. More observations make large deviations less likely, but never absolutely impossible. If we roll a fair die many times, the fraction of sixes is increasingly likely to approach $P(6) = 1/6 \approx 0.167$, but an unusual sequence always remains possible. Repeating measurements gives more stable estimates and reduces the influence of random fluctuations, but it does not eliminate the uncertainty or a systematic bias.

Let us apply this to a measurement with $M$ possible outcomes $x_j$. If $n_j$ is the absolute frequency with which $x_j$ occurs in $N$ trials, then $\sum_{j=1}^{M}n_j = N$ (some $n_j$ may be zero, if the corresponding value was never observed). The relative frequency of $x_j$ is $f_j = n_j/N$, and
\begin{equation}
\sum_{j=1}^{M}f_j = \sum_{j=1}^{M}\frac{n_j}{N} = \frac{1}{N}\sum_{j=1}^{M}n_j = 1.
\label{eq:frequencies_normalised}
\end{equation}
If $N$ grows indefinitely, each $f_j$ tends statistically to the probability $p_j$ of observing $x_j$, and therefore $\sum_j p_j = 1$, as required by normalisation.

\subsection{From the normalised histogram to the probability density}
\label{sec:pdf}

Suppose we perform $N$ measurements of a quantity $x$ and divide its range into bins of equal width $\Delta x$. In a \textbf{normalised histogram} the \emph{area} of each bar is the relative frequency $f_j$ of the measurements falling in that interval, so its \emph{height} is the relative frequency divided by the bin width,
\begin{equation}
\boxed{h_i = \frac{n_i}{N\,\Delta x} = \frac{f_j}{\Delta x}}
\label{eq:normalised_histogram}
\end{equation}
where $n_i$ is the number of measurements in the $i$-th bin, and the total area is one, $\sum_i h_i\,\Delta x = \sum_j f_j = 1$.

As $N$ increases, the law of large numbers tells us that the relative frequency in each bin approaches the probability of that event, $f_j \to p_j = \Delta p$, so the height tends to $\Delta p/\Delta x$. If, at the same time, the bin width decreases ($\Delta x \to dx$, $\Delta p \to dp$), the normalised histogram approaches a continuous curve, the \textbf{probability density function} (PDF) $f(x)$, which can describe the probability of a continuous (or discrete) random variable over its possible values (Figure~\ref{fig:histogram_to_pdf}). For an infinitesimal interval $dx$ the probability $dp$ of a value within it goes to zero, but the ratio can stay finite and defines the PDF:
\begin{equation}
\boxed{f(x) = \frac{dp}{dx}}
\label{eq:pdf_definition}
\end{equation}
So a PDF can be seen as the limit of a normalised histogram when the number of measurements becomes very large and the bins small enough (this assumes an underlying continuous distribution and an appropriate relation between sample size and bin width). Three properties are important. The height $f(x)$ is a probability \emph{per unit of $x$}, not a probability. The area under the curve over an interval gives the probability of observing a value in that interval. And the PDF has the units of the inverse of $x$: if $x$ is a length in metres, $f(x)$ is in $\text{m}^{-1}$.

\begin{figure}[!htbp]
\centering
\includegraphics[page=51, width=0.45\linewidth, keepaspectratio]{\pdf}
\caption{With few measurements ($N = 60$, $\Delta x = 0.8$) the normalised histogram is coarse; with many measurements ($N = 20\,000$, $\Delta x = 0.1$) and narrow bins it approaches the probability density function $f(x)$.}
\label{fig:histogram_to_pdf}
\end{figure}

The probability that a continuous random variable lies in an interval $[a, b]$ is therefore the area under the density curve over that interval,
\begin{equation}
\boxed{P(a \le X \le b) = \int_a^b f(x)\,dx}
\label{eq:probability_interval}
\end{equation}
and the integral of the PDF over the whole outcome space is one. A probability distribution thus quantifies how likely it is that the result falls in a given range, and it can be defined for continuous or discrete sets of outcomes. Is it more likely to have births in January or in May? If we measure the temperature of this room, are results colder than the average more likely than those hotter than the average? These are the kinds of question a distribution answers. In what follows we will learn how to define the best estimate and the dispersion of a random variable, first for discrete and then for continuous variables.

\subsection{Deviations and least squares}

When we measure a constant quantity, repeated measurements generally give slightly different values. The \textbf{true value} $x^*$ is the ideal, generally unknown value of the QUM that a perfect measurement under specified ideal conditions would give. The \textbf{deviation} of a measurement from it (or from a reference value),
\begin{equation}
d_i = x_i - x^*,
\label{eq:deviation}
\end{equation}
describes how the observations are distributed around the true value, and can be positive or negative. To study their average behaviour we must get rid of the sign: we could take the modulus $|x_i - x^*|$, but it is more convenient to square them, sum the squared deviations $\sum_i(x_i - x^*)^2$, and average them, obtaining the \textbf{variance}
\begin{equation}
\boxed{\sigma^2 = \frac{1}{N}\sum_{i=1}^{N}(x_i - x^*)^2}
\label{eq:variance_true_value}
\end{equation}
Small deviations from the sample mean indicate good repeatability (precision), but do not necessarily mean that the measurements are close to the true value (accuracy).

The same idea of minimising squared differences is behind \textbf{least-squares fitting}, whose objective is to find the parameters of a model that best describe a set of measurements. Given $n$ experimental pairs $(x_i, y_i)$ and a model $f(x;\boldsymbol{\beta})$, where $\boldsymbol{\beta}$ are the adjustable parameters, the \textbf{residual} $r_i = y_i - f(x_i;\boldsymbol{\beta})$ is the difference between the observed value and the model prediction (as in Equation~\eqref{eq:residuals_definition}), and the least-squares method chooses the parameters that minimise the sum of squared residuals,
\begin{equation}
\boxed{S(\boldsymbol{\beta}) = \sum_{i=1}^{n}r_i^2 = \sum_{i=1}^{n}\big(y_i - f(x_i;\boldsymbol{\beta})\big)^2}
\label{eq:least_squares}
\end{equation}
For a straight line, $y = \beta_0 + \beta_1x$, $\beta_0$ is the intercept and $\beta_1$ the slope. The best-fitting model minimises the overall squared differences between observations and predictions.

\section{Best Estimate and Dispersion of Repeated Measurements}
\label{sec:best_estimate}

\subsection{Measures of central tendency}

When we obtain $N$ repeated measurements of the same quantity, which generally give different results, an important question arises: how can we determine the best estimate of the true value from the available observations? If systematic errors have been eliminated or adequately corrected, if random errors are symmetrically distributed around zero (positive and negative deviations are equally likely), and if the observed relative frequencies tend to their theoretical probabilities according to the law of large numbers (for a large enough number of measurements), then we expect the measured values to be distributed approximately symmetrically around the unknown true value. Intuitively, the best estimate should lie near the centre of this distribution. But how do we identify the centre mathematically? There are three common measures of central tendency.

The \textbf{mode} is the value that occurs most frequently, the maximum of the frequency distribution. A distribution may have one mode (unimodal), several (multimodal) or none clearly defined; the mode is not always a good estimate of the centre and is rarely used to summarise repeated physical measurements. The \textbf{median} $\tilde{x}$ is the value that divides the ordered observations into two equal halves: for an odd number of observations it is the middle value, for an even number it is conventionally the average of the two middle values. It is the 50th percentile of the data. The \textbf{mean} is the arithmetic average of all the observed values,
\begin{equation}
\boxed{\bar{x} = \frac{1}{N}\sum_{i=1}^{N}x_i = \frac{x_1 + x_2 + \dots + x_N}{N}}
\label{eq:arithmetic_mean}
\end{equation}
and it is the most commonly used measure of central tendency for repeated physical measurements.

Suppose some values in the sample are repeated: $x_1$ occurs $n_1$ times, $x_2$ occurs $n_2$ times, and so on, with $N = n_1 + n_2 + \dots$. Then $\bar{x} = (n_1x_1 + n_2x_2 + \dots)/N$. Calling $x_j$ ($j = 1, \dots, M$) the $M$ distinct values, $n_j$ their absolute frequencies and $f_j = n_j/N$ their relative frequencies, we can write
\begin{equation}
\bar{x} = \frac{1}{N}\sum_{i=1}^{N}x_i = \frac{1}{N}\sum_{j=1}^{M}n_jx_j = \sum_{j=1}^{M}\frac{n_j}{N}x_j \quad \Longrightarrow \quad \boxed{\bar{x} = \sum_{j=1}^{M}f_jx_j}
\label{eq:mean_frequencies}
\end{equation}
where the sum extends over all possible values of $x$ (values that were not observed have zero frequency). As $N \to \infty$ the frequencies tend to the probabilities, $f_j \to p_j$, and we can define a new quantity associated with the whole population (we are still dealing with a discrete variable, i.e.\ with a histogram):
\begin{equation}
\boxed{\mu = \sum_i p_ix_i}
\label{eq:expectation_discrete}
\end{equation}
$\mu$ is the \textbf{mathematical expectation} of the random variable $x$, the extension of the arithmetic mean from a finite sample to the entire population. If probability were defined empirically, it would be the statistical limit of the sample mean as the sample size grows; for this reason it is also called, somewhat less precisely, the \textbf{population mean}. For a population of finite size $P$ (usually $P = \infty$) it is $\mu = \frac{1}{P}\sum_{i=1}^{P}x_i$, while the sample mean of $N$ measurements randomly selected from it is $\bar{x} = \frac{1}{N}\sum_{i=1}^{N}x_i$.

\subsection{Why the mean?}

Why should the arithmetic mean be the best estimate? It has three important mathematical properties. First, among all possible central values $x$, the \emph{median} minimises the sum of absolute deviations, $\min_x\sum_i|x_i - x| = \sum_i|x_i - \tilde{x}|$, which makes the median less sensitive to extreme observations (outliers). Second, the sum of the deviations from the arithmetic mean is zero:
\begin{equation}
\boxed{\sum_{i=1}^{N}(x_i - \bar{x}) = 0}
\label{eq:sum_deviations_zero}
\end{equation}
Indeed, $\sum_i(x_i - \bar{x}) = \sum_i x_i - N\bar{x} = N\bar{x} - N\bar{x} = 0$: the mean is the balance point of the data, where positive and negative deviations cancel exactly. Third, the mean minimises the sum of squared deviations:
\begin{equation}
\boxed{\sum_{i=1}^{N}(x_i - x)^2 \ge \sum_{i=1}^{N}(x_i - \bar{x})^2}
\label{eq:mean_least_squares}
\end{equation}
To prove it, we add and subtract $\bar{x}$:
\begin{equation*}
\sum_i(x_i - x)^2 = \sum_i\big[(x_i - \bar{x}) + (\bar{x} - x)\big]^2 = \sum_i(x_i - \bar{x})^2 + N(\bar{x} - x)^2 + 2(\bar{x} - x)\sum_i(x_i - \bar{x}),
\end{equation*}
and by Equation~\eqref{eq:sum_deviations_zero} the last term vanishes, so $\sum_i(x_i - x)^2 = \sum_i(x_i - \bar{x})^2 + N(\bar{x} - x)^2$, and since $N(\bar{x} - x)^2 \ge 0$ the inequality follows. So the median minimises the sum of absolute deviations and is robust to outliers, whereas the mean minimises the sum of squared deviations and is more sensitive to extreme values; this property makes the mean particularly important in the statistical treatment of data.

The mean is also a good estimator because it reduces the influence of random errors. If $x^*$ is the true value, the error of each measurement is $\varepsilon_i = x_i - x^*$, and the error of the mean is
\begin{equation}
\bar{\varepsilon} = \bar{x} - x^* = \frac{1}{N}\sum_{i=1}^{N}(x_i - x^*) = \frac{1}{N}\sum_{i=1}^{N}\varepsilon_i.
\end{equation}
If only unbiased random errors are present, positive and negative errors tend to cancel when many measurements are averaged, and the random error of the mean generally decreases as $N$ grows: the mean of repeated independent measurements is expected to have a smaller random error than the individual measurements.

What happens, then, if we take many samples? Consider $M$ randomly selected samples of $N$ measurements each. The mean of sample $j$ is $\bar{x}_j = \frac{1}{N}\sum_i x_{ij}$, and each sample will generally have a different mean. The mean of the sample means,
\begin{equation}
\bar{\bar{x}} = \frac{1}{M}\sum_{j=1}^{M}\bar{x}_j = \frac{1}{MN}\sum_{j=1}^{M}\sum_{i=1}^{N}x_{ij},
\end{equation}
approaches the population mean, $\bar{\bar{x}} \to \mu$ as $M \to \infty$, regardless of the fixed sample size $N \ge 1$, provided that the samples are selected randomly and without systematic sampling bias.

\subsection{Measures of dispersion: variance, standard deviation and standard error}

Central tendency gives us a representative value; \textbf{dispersion} describes how widely the measured values are spread around it, and it is closely related to the variability introduced by random errors. The simplest measure is the \textbf{half-range} (maximum semi-dispersion),
\begin{equation}
\boxed{\Delta x_{\text{range}} = \frac{x_{\max} - x_{\min}}{2}}
\label{eq:half_range}
\end{equation}
but it depends only on the two extreme observations and ignores all the others. A more informative and widely used measure is the standard deviation, the square root of the variance of the individual measurements,
\begin{equation}
s_N^2 = \frac{1}{N}\sum_{i=1}^{N}(x_i - \bar{x})^2, \qquad s_N = \sqrt{\frac{1}{N}\sum_{i=1}^{N}(x_i - \bar{x})^2},
\label{eq:variance_N}
\end{equation}
which quantifies the typical spread of the observations around their mean and has the same unit as the measured quantity: the larger it is, the more dispersed the values. Careful, though: when the $N$ observations are a sample used to estimate the variance of a larger population, this formula must be corrected, as we will see shortly. To compute it, a useful identity is obtained by multiplying the definition by $N$ and expanding the square:
\begin{equation*}
Ns^2 = \sum_i(x_i - \bar{x})^2 = \sum_i x_i^2 + N\bar{x}^2 - 2\bar{x}\sum_i x_i = \sum_i x_i^2 + N\bar{x}^2 - 2N\bar{x}^2 = \sum_i x_i^2 - N\bar{x}^2,
\end{equation*}
where we used $\sum_i x_i = N\bar{x}$. Dividing by $N$,
\begin{equation}
\boxed{s^2 = \frac{1}{N}\sum_{i=1}^{N}x_i^2 - \bar{x}^2}
\label{eq:variance_identity}
\end{equation}
the variance is the mean of the squares minus the square of the mean.

\label{sec:variance_definitions}

Let us now fix the definitions we will use from here on. The \textbf{variance} measures the average squared deviation of the values of a random variable from its expected value $\mathbb{E}(x) = \mu$, $\text{Var}(x) = \overline{(x - \mu)^2}$, and the \textbf{standard deviation} is its square root. For the population,
\begin{equation}
\boxed{\sigma^2 = \frac{1}{P}\sum_{i=1}^{P}(x_i - \mu)^2, \qquad \sigma = \sqrt{\sigma^2}}
\label{eq:population_variance}
\end{equation}
The variance computed from a sample of $N$ measurements estimates the population variance, and the sample standard deviation $s$ (also called SD) measures the dispersion of the individual observations around their mean:
\begin{equation}
\boxed{s^2 = \frac{1}{N - 1}\sum_{i=1}^{N}(x_i - \bar{x})^2, \qquad s = \sqrt{s^2}}
\label{eq:sample_variance}
\end{equation}
Finally, the \textbf{standard deviation of the mean}, or \textbf{standard error of the mean} (SEM), estimates the standard deviation of the sample mean across repeated samples of size $N$:
\begin{equation}
\boxed{\text{SEM} = \frac{s}{\sqrt{N}}}
\label{eq:sem_definition}
\end{equation}
These are the same quantities met qualitatively in Section~\ref{sec:sd_sem}; the rest of this section shows why they have this form.

\subsection{Linear combinations of independent variables}
\label{sec:linear_combination}

Consider two independent random variables $x$ and $y$, with means $\mu_x$, $\mu_y$ and variances $\sigma_x^2$, $\sigma_y^2$, and define a new variable as their linear combination with constant coefficients,
\begin{equation}
z = ax + by.
\end{equation}
Its mean is the same linear combination of the individual means: from $\mu_z = \frac{1}{N}\sum_i z_i = \frac{1}{N}\sum_i(ax_i + by_i)$, separating the sums, $\mu_z = a\frac{1}{N}\sum_i x_i + b\frac{1}{N}\sum_i y_i$, i.e.
\begin{equation}
\boxed{\mu_z = a\mu_x + b\mu_y, \qquad \bar{z} = a\bar{x} + b\bar{y}}
\label{eq:mean_linear_combination}
\end{equation}
Its variance is the average squared deviation from its mean:
\begin{equation}
\sigma_z^2 = \overline{(z - \mu_z)^2} = \overline{\big[a(x - \mu_x) + b(y - \mu_y)\big]^2} = a^2\sigma_x^2 + b^2\sigma_y^2 + 2ab\,\overline{(x - \mu_x)(y - \mu_y)}.
\end{equation}
For independent variables the average product of the deviations from their respective means is zero, so
\begin{equation}
\boxed{\sigma_z^2 = a^2\sigma_x^2 + b^2\sigma_y^2, \qquad s_z^2 = a^2s_x^2 + b^2s_y^2}
\label{eq:variance_linear_combination}
\end{equation}
and in general, for $F = ax + by + cz + \dots$ with independent variables, $\sigma_F^2 = a^2\sigma_x^2 + b^2\sigma_y^2 + c^2\sigma_z^2 + \dots$. Variances add quadratically: the variance of a linear combination is the sum of the individual variances multiplied by the squares of their coefficients. This result is fundamental for the propagation of random uncertainties (Section~\ref{sec:indirect}). Two useful consequences are that adding a constant does not change the variance, $\text{Var}(x + c) = \text{Var}(x)$, while multiplying by a constant multiplies it by the square, $\text{Var}(cx) = c^2\,\text{Var}(x)$. Exercise~\ref{ex:background_correction} applies these rules to a background correction in fluorescence microscopy.

\subsection{Why averaging reduces the uncertainty: Chebyshev and the law of large numbers}

Consider $N$ independent measurements from the same population, each with the same variance $\sigma^2$ (so $\sigma_x = \sigma$). Their mean $\bar{x} = \frac{1}{N}\sum_i x_i$ is a linear combination with coefficients $1/N$, so we can apply Equation~\eqref{eq:variance_linear_combination}:
\begin{equation*}
\sigma_{\bar{x}}^2 = \frac{1}{N^2}\sum_{i=1}^{N}\sigma_{x_i}^2 = \frac{1}{N^2}\cdot N\sigma_x^2 = \frac{\sigma_x^2}{N},
\end{equation*}
and taking the square root,
\begin{equation}
\boxed{\sigma_{\bar{x}} = \frac{\sigma_x}{\sqrt{N}} = \frac{\sigma}{\sqrt{N}}}
\label{eq:sigma_mean}
\end{equation}
If we repeatedly draw independent samples of size $N$ and compute their means, these means form a \emph{sampling distribution} whose variance is the variance of the individual measurements divided by $N$. The sample mean therefore has a smaller variance than the individual measurements, increasing the number of independent measurements reduces the random uncertainty of the mean, and the standard deviation of the mean decreases as $1/\sqrt{N}$. The standard deviation of the individual measurements, instead, does not decrease just because we collect more of them: averaging $N$ independent measurements reduces the dispersion of the estimated mean by a factor $\sqrt{N}$, without reducing the variability of the individual measurements.

The sample mean generally differs from the population mean because of random fluctuations. How does the probability of a large deviation change with $N$? \textbf{Chebyshev's inequality} gives an upper bound to the probability that a random variable differs from its mean by more than a given amount $\varepsilon$:
\begin{equation}
\boxed{\Pr\big(|x - \mu| \ge \varepsilon\big) \le \frac{\sigma^2}{\varepsilon^2}}
\label{eq:chebyshev}
\end{equation}
The proof is short. By the additivity of probability (the probability of a union of mutually exclusive events is the sum of their probabilities), $\Pr(|x - \mu| \ge \varepsilon) = \sum_{|x_i - \mu| \ge \varepsilon}p_i$, where the sum includes only the values that satisfy the condition. Now, $\sigma^2 = \sum_i p_i(x_i - \mu)^2$; if we restrict the sum to the terms with $|x_i - \mu| \ge \varepsilon$, it can only decrease or stay the same, and in each remaining term $(x_i - \mu)^2 \ge \varepsilon^2$, so
\begin{equation*}
\sigma^2 \ge \sum_{|x_i - \mu| \ge \varepsilon}p_i(x_i - \mu)^2 \ge \varepsilon^2\sum_{|x_i - \mu| \ge \varepsilon}p_i = \varepsilon^2\Pr\big(|x - \mu| \ge \varepsilon\big),
\end{equation*}
which is the Bienaym\'e--Chebyshev inequality. Setting $\varepsilon = k\sigma$ we obtain $\Pr(|x - \mu| \ge k\sigma) \le 1/k^2$: an upper bound on the probability that a variable differs from its mean by at least $k$ standard deviations. For $k = 2$ this probability is at most $1/4$, so at least 75\% of the values lie within two standard deviations of the mean, whatever the shape of the distribution, provided its variance is finite.

Applying the inequality to the sample mean, whose variance is $\sigma^2/N$, gives
\begin{equation}
\boxed{\Pr\big(|\bar{x} - \mu| \ge \varepsilon\big) \le \frac{\sigma^2}{N\varepsilon^2}}
\label{eq:chebyshev_mean}
\end{equation}
For any chosen positive $\varepsilon$ and $\delta$, we can make this probability smaller than $\delta$ by taking $N > \sigma^2/(\delta\varepsilon^2)$; therefore $\Pr(|\bar{x} - \mu| \ge \varepsilon) \to 0$ as $N \to \infty$, i.e.\ $\bar{x} \to \mu$. This is \textbf{Chebyshev's theorem}: the mean of a sample of $N$ independent measurements, drawn from a population with finite variance, converges in probability to the population mean as $N$ increases.

\textbf{Bernoulli's theorem} follows as a special case. Consider an event $E$ with probability $p$ (and $q = 1 - p$ of not occurring), and repeat the experiment $N$ independent times. For each trial define $y_i = 1$ if $E$ occurs and $y_i = 0$ otherwise. The relative frequency of $E$ is then simply the mean of these binary observations, $f_N = \frac{1}{N}\sum_i y_i = \bar{y}$. The binary variable has population mean $\mu_y = p$ and variance $\sigma_y^2 = p(1 - p)$, so by Chebyshev's inequality
\begin{equation}
\Pr\big(|f_N - p| \ge \varepsilon\big) \le \frac{p(1 - p)}{N\varepsilon^2} \xrightarrow[N \to \infty]{} 0.
\label{eq:bernoulli_theorem}
\end{equation}
The relative frequency of a random event converges in probability to its probability as the number of independent trials increases: this is the law of large numbers of Equation~\eqref{eq:lln_frequency}.

Putting things together, $\bar{x} = \sum_j f_jx_j$ and $f_j \to p_j$, so $\lim_{N \to \infty}\bar{x} = \sum_j p_jx_j = \mu$. In the absence of systematic errors we assume that the expected value and the true value coincide, $\mu \equiv x^*$. Not only does $\bar{x}$ converge to $\mu$ as the sample grows, but, whatever the sample size, the mean of $\bar{x}$ is on average equal to $\mu$: the sample mean is an \textbf{unbiased estimator} of the population mean. (Unbiasedness alone does not guarantee equal probabilities of overestimating and underestimating $\mu$; this requires further assumptions, such as a continuous sampling distribution symmetric around $\mu$.)

\subsection{Why \texorpdfstring{$N - 1$}{N - 1}? The unbiased estimate of the variance}
\label{sec:n_minus_one}

Suppose we want to estimate the population variance $\sigma^2$ from a sample of $N$ independent measurements. Can we use the variance $s_N^2$ of Equation~\eqref{eq:variance_N}, with denominator $N$? The sample mean is calculated from the same measurements, so it tends to lie near their centre, and this reduces the squared deviations computed around it. Let us see by how much. Writing the deviations from the true value $x^*$ (with $\mathbb{E}(x) = x^*$, so $\sigma^2 = \mathbb{E}[(x - x^*)^2]$) and adding and subtracting $\bar{x}$,
\begin{equation*}
\frac{1}{N}\sum_i(x_i - x^*)^2 = \frac{1}{N}\left[\sum_i(x_i - \bar{x})^2 + N(\bar{x} - x^*)^2 + 2(\bar{x} - x^*)\sum_i(x_i - \bar{x})\right] = s_N^2 + (\bar{x} - x^*)^2,
\end{equation*}
because the sum of the deviations from the mean is zero (Equation~\eqref{eq:sum_deviations_zero}). Hence
\begin{equation*}
s_N^2 = \frac{1}{N}\sum_i(x_i - x^*)^2 - (\bar{x} - x^*)^2.
\end{equation*}
Now we average both sides over all the (infinitely many) samples of size $N$ that could be drawn from the population. The first term on the right becomes $\sigma^2$; the second is the mean squared deviation of $\bar{x}$ from its own mean $\mathbb{E}(\bar{x}) = x^*$, which by definition is the variance of the mean, $\sigma_{\bar{x}}^2$. Therefore
\begin{equation}
\boxed{\langle s_N^2\rangle_{\text{samples}} = \sigma^2 - \sigma_{\bar{x}}^2 < \sigma^2}
\label{eq:sample_variance_bias}
\end{equation}
On average, the variance computed with denominator $N$ is smaller than the population variance, and the difference is exactly the variance of the sample mean. Since $\sigma_{\bar{x}}^2 = \sigma^2/N$ (Equation~\eqref{eq:sigma_mean}),
\begin{equation}
\langle s_N^2\rangle_{\text{samples}} = \frac{N - 1}{N}\,\sigma^2,
\end{equation}
so $s_N^2$ systematically underestimates the population variance. To compensate, we multiply by $N/(N - 1)$, i.e.\ we use $N - 1$ instead of $N$ in the denominator, obtaining the \textbf{corrected sample variance}
\begin{equation}
\boxed{s^2 = \frac{N}{N - 1}s_N^2 = \frac{1}{N - 1}\sum_{i=1}^{N}(x_i - \bar{x})^2, \qquad \langle s^2\rangle_{\text{samples}} = \sigma^2}
\label{eq:corrected_sample_variance}
\end{equation}
Averaged over many independent samples, the corrected variance gives exactly $\sigma^2$: this is why it is called an \textbf{unbiased estimator} of the population variance. We divide by $N - 1$ to correct the systematic underestimation introduced by using the sample mean.

Finally, the theoretical dispersion of the sample means is $\sigma_{\bar{x}} = \sigma/\sqrt{N}$, and the estimate we can obtain from a single sample is the standard error of the mean of Equation~\eqref{eq:sem_definition}, $\text{SEM} = s/\sqrt{N}$, with $s$ computed with $N - 1$.

\subsection{Summary: best estimate and combined uncertainty}
\label{sec:summary_discrete}

It is worth summarising how these quantities converge. The mean of a single sample approaches the population mean as the sample size increases, $\bar{x}_j \to \mu$ for $N \to \infty$; the mean of the sample means approaches it as the number of independent samples increases, $\bar{\bar{x}}_M \to \mu$ for $M \to \infty$, even when the size of each sample is fixed. For independent observations with population variance $\sigma^2$, $\text{Var}(\bar{x}_j) = \sigma^2/N$, while for $M$ independent samples of the same size $N$, $\text{Var}(\bar{\bar{x}}_M) = \sigma^2/(MN)$. Both increasing the number of measurements per sample and averaging the means of many samples improve the precision of our estimate of $\mu$; the difference is that increasing $N$ improves each individual sample mean, whereas increasing $M$ improves the average across sample means.

When repeated measurements are available, and assuming that systematic errors have been corrected and random errors are unbiased, we therefore proceed as follows. The \emph{best estimate} of the true value is the arithmetic mean $\bar{x}$: positive and negative random deviations tend to compensate, the distribution of averages is narrower than that of individual observations, and the more measurements we have, the smaller the random uncertainty of the mean. The \emph{uncertainty of the individual measurements}, i.e.\ their dispersion, is estimated by the sample standard deviation
\begin{equation}
\boxed{\text{SD} = s = \sqrt{\frac{\sum_{i=1}^{N}(x_i - \bar{x})^2}{N - 1}}}
\label{eq:sd_summary}
\end{equation}
which describes the individual measurements, not the uncertainty of their mean. The \emph{standard uncertainty of the mean}, for independent measurements, is $s/\sqrt{N}$.

We should keep the two kinds of uncertainty distinct. The uncertainty of an \emph{individual measurement} may include random variability, calibration uncertainty, instrument limitations and other contributions. The uncertainty of the \emph{mean} refers to the average of repeated measurements, and its random component decreases as the number of independent measurements increases. Averaging reduces the random uncertainty, but it does not necessarily eliminate systematic errors or reduce contributions shared by all the measurements; the uncertainty of the mean describes how precisely we estimate a quantity, not how far our estimate actually is from the true value.

\label{sec:combined_uncertainty}

A result is usually affected by several contributions: the repeatability of the measurements, $u_A = s/\sqrt{N}$; the instrument resolution $r$, $u_{\text{res}} = r/\sqrt{12}$ (Equation~\eqref{eq:standard_uncertainty_resolution_sqrt12}, assuming rounding to the nearest increment and a uniform quantisation error); the calibration, $u_{\text{cal}}$; and possibly others. For uncorrelated contributions of independent variables, expressed in the same units, the \textbf{combined standard uncertainty} is
\begin{equation}
\boxed{u_c = \sqrt{u_A^2 + u_{\text{res}}^2 + u_{\text{cal}}^2 + \dots}}
\label{eq:combined_standard_uncertainty}
\end{equation}
which is the rule anticipated in Equation~\eqref{eq:combined_uncertainty_quadrature} and justified by Equation~\eqref{eq:variance_linear_combination}. The result is then reported as
\begin{equation}
\boxed{x = \bar{x} \pm U, \qquad U = k\,u_c}
\label{eq:expanded_uncertainty}
\end{equation}
where $U$ is the \textbf{expanded uncertainty} and $k$ the \textbf{coverage factor}.

\section{Continuous Random Variables}
\label{sec:continuous}

We defined the probability that a continuous variable lies in an interval $[a, b]$ as the area under the density curve over that interval (Equation~\eqref{eq:probability_interval}). Similarly, the \textbf{cumulative probability}, or \textbf{cumulative distribution function} (CDF), is the probability that the variable takes a value less than or equal to a given number:
\begin{equation}
\boxed{F_X(b) = P(X \le b) = \int_{-\infty}^{b}f(x)\,dx}
\label{eq:cdf}
\end{equation}

\subsection{Mean and variance of a continuous variable}

For a discrete variable the expectation value is $\mu = \sum_i p_ix_i$ (Equation~\eqref{eq:expectation_discrete}), with $p_i$ the probabilities of the values $x_i$. For a continuous variable we can imagine dividing the $x$ axis into a very large number of extremely small intervals of width $dx$, each with an equally small probability $dp = f(x)\,dx$, where $f(x)$ is the PDF. Substituting into the discrete formula ($\Delta x \to 0$, $\Delta p \to 0$), the sum becomes an integral:
\begin{equation}
\boxed{\mu = \int_{-\infty}^{+\infty}x\,f(x)\,dx}
\label{eq:expectation_continuous}
\end{equation}
and, by Chebyshev's theorem, the means of finite samples converge to this expectation value as $N$ grows.

For the variance we start from the identity~\eqref{eq:variance_identity}, $s^2 = \frac{1}{N}\sum_i x_i^2 - \bar{x}^2$. As $N \to \infty$, for a population with finite variance, $\bar{x} \to \mu$ and $\frac{1}{N}\sum_i x_i^2 = \sum_j f_jx_j^2 \to \sum_j p_jx_j^2$, so $s^2 \to \sigma^2 = \sum_j p_jx_j^2 - \mu^2$. Passing to the continuous case,
\begin{equation}
\boxed{\sigma^2 = \int_{-\infty}^{+\infty}(x - \mu)^2f(x)\,dx = \int_{-\infty}^{+\infty}x^2f(x)\,dx - \mu^2}
\label{eq:variance_continuous}
\end{equation}
Just as the expected value replaces a probability-weighted sum with an integral, the variance integrates the squared deviations from the mean, weighted by the PDF. Values farther from the mean contribute more strongly. The standard deviation $\sigma = \sqrt{\sigma^2}$ expresses the dispersion in the same physical units as the measured quantity.

\subsection{Moments of a distribution}

For any random variable we can define, with reference to the whole population, its \textbf{moments}. The $k$-th moment about the origin is the expectation of $x^k$, and the $k$-th moment about the mean is the expectation of $(x - \mu)^k$:
\begin{equation}
\boxed{\mu_k = \sum_i p_ix_i^k, \qquad \sigma_k = \sum_i p_i(x_i - \mu)^k}
\label{eq:moments_discrete}
\end{equation}
and, for a continuous variable,
\begin{equation}
\mu_k = \int_{-\infty}^{+\infty}x^kf(x)\,dx, \qquad \sigma_k = \int_{-\infty}^{+\infty}(x - \mu)^kf(x)\,dx.
\label{eq:moments_continuous}
\end{equation}
Analogous moments can be defined for a sample, using frequencies. If the population is infinite, as for continuous variables, the moments are not guaranteed to exist.

The mean is the first moment about the origin, $\mu_1$, and the variance is the second moment about the mean, $\sigma_2 = \mu_2 - \mu_1^2$ with $\mu_2 = \overline{x^2}$ (the \emph{second central moment}). The first moment about the mean is always zero, for any population for which $\mu_1$ exists:
\begin{equation}
\sigma_1 = \int_{-\infty}^{+\infty}(x - \mu_1)f(x)\,dx = \int_{-\infty}^{+\infty}xf(x)\,dx - \mu_1\int_{-\infty}^{+\infty}f(x)\,dx = \mu_1 - \mu_1 = 0,
\end{equation}
the continuous analogue of Equation~\eqref{eq:sum_deviations_zero}. These relations hold whether the distribution is symmetric or not. Moreover, for distributions symmetric about the mean, \emph{all odd moments about the mean are zero} (if they exist), because the contributions from two intervals located symmetrically on opposite sides of the mean cancel each other. The third moment about the mean can therefore be regarded as a measure of the asymmetry of a distribution; in practice, instead of $\sigma_3$, it is preferable to use a dimensionless parameter, the coefficient of asymmetry or \textbf{skewness} $\gamma_1$, defined in the next section.

\section{Some Notable Distributions}
\label{sec:distributions}

\subsection{The uniform distribution}
\label{sec:uniform}

A continuous random variable $X$ follows a \textbf{uniform distribution} on an interval $[a, b]$ when its probability density is constant in that interval and zero elsewhere:
\begin{equation}
\boxed{f_X(x) = \begin{cases} \dfrac{1}{b - a}, & a \le x \le b, \\[1ex] 0, & \text{otherwise}. \end{cases}}
\label{eq:uniform_pdf}
\end{equation}
The height $1/(b - a)$ ensures that the total area under the curve is one, and within the interval the cumulative probability increases linearly from 0 to 1. Equal-width intervals inside $[a, b]$ have equal probabilities: this is exactly the model we used for the quantisation error in Section~\ref{sec:resolution}.

Using Equations~\eqref{eq:expectation_continuous} and~\eqref{eq:variance_continuous} we can compute its mean and variance. Calling $\Delta x = b - a$ and taking the interval $[-\Delta x/2, +\Delta x/2]$, where $f(x) = 1/\Delta x$,
\begin{equation*}
\mu = \frac{1}{\Delta x}\int_{-\Delta x/2}^{\Delta x/2}x\,dx = \frac{1}{\Delta x}\left[\frac{x^2}{2}\right]_{-\Delta x/2}^{\Delta x/2} = 0, \qquad \sigma^2 = \frac{1}{\Delta x}\int_{-\Delta x/2}^{\Delta x/2}x^2\,dx = \frac{1}{\Delta x}\left[\frac{x^3}{3}\right]_{-\Delta x/2}^{\Delta x/2} = \frac{\Delta x^2}{12}.
\end{equation*}
The same holds for a non-symmetric interval $[a, b]$: the mean is the midpoint, and the variance depends only on the width,
\begin{equation}
\boxed{\mu = \frac{a + b}{2}, \qquad \sigma^2 = \frac{(b - a)^2}{12}, \qquad \sigma = \frac{b - a}{\sqrt{12}}}
\label{eq:uniform_mean_variance}
\end{equation}
Exercise~\ref{ex:pixel_localisation} applies it to the localisation of a fluorescent marker within a pixel.

\subsection{Four important distributions}

Four other distributions are particularly important. The \textbf{binomial distribution} counts successes: it describes the number of successes in a fixed number of independent trials, each with the same probability of success (for example, the number of heads in 20 coin tosses). The \textbf{Poisson distribution} counts events: it describes the number of independent events occurring in a fixed time interval or region, under a constant average rate (for example, the number of photons detected in one second); its mean and variance are both equal to its parameter $\lambda$, so its standard deviation is $\sqrt{\lambda}$. Both are discrete distributions, and both approach a bell shape, i.e.\ a Gaussian, when the number of trials or the mean count increases (Figures~\ref{fig:binomial_gauss} and~\ref{fig:poisson_gauss}).

\clearpage
\begin{figure}[!htbp]
\centering
\includegraphics[page=53, width=\linewidth, keepaspectratio]{\pdf}
\caption{Many Bernoulli trials: the binomial distribution (here with $p = 0.30$ and $n = 5, 20, 100$) approaches a Gaussian. The normal approximation improves as $np$ and $n(1-p)$ become large.}
\label{fig:binomial_gauss}
\end{figure}

\begin{figure}[h]
\centering
\includegraphics[page=54, width=\linewidth, keepaspectratio]{\pdf}
\caption{As the mean count $\lambda$ increases ($\lambda = 2, 10, 40$), the Poisson distribution approaches a Gaussian with $\mu = \lambda$ and $\sigma = \sqrt{\lambda}$.}
\label{fig:poisson_gauss}
\end{figure}

The \textbf{normal (Gaussian) distribution} describes continuous measurements: it is symmetric and bell-shaped, characterised by its mean and standard deviation, and it often describes random fluctuations arising from many small, approximately independent effects; the \emph{standard} normal distribution is the special case with mean 0 and standard deviation 1. \textbf{Student's $t$ distribution} is similar to the Gaussian, but with heavier tails, and is used to estimate a population mean from a sample when the population standard deviation is unknown, especially for small samples. These last two are the subject of the next sections.

\subsection{Skewness and kurtosis}

Two dimensionless parameters, derived from the third and fourth moments about the mean, characterise the shape of a distribution (Figure~\ref{fig:skew_kurt}). The \textbf{skewness} describes the asymmetry with respect to the mean,
\begin{equation}
\boxed{\gamma_1 = \frac{\sigma_3}{\sigma_2^{3/2}} = \frac{\sigma_3}{\sigma^3}}
\label{eq:skewness}
\end{equation}
where $\sigma = \sqrt{\sigma_2}$ is exactly the standard deviation: $\gamma_1 = 0$ for a symmetric distribution, $\gamma_1 > 0$ for a distribution skewed to the right, $\gamma_1 < 0$ for one skewed to the left. The \textbf{kurtosis} is obtained from the fourth central moment, $\gamma_2' = \sigma_4/\sigma_2^2 = \sigma_4/\sigma^4$. Since for a normal distribution $\gamma_2' = 3$, it is often expressed as the \emph{excess kurtosis}
\begin{equation}
\boxed{\gamma_2 = \frac{\sigma_4}{\sigma^4} - 3}
\label{eq:kurtosis}
\end{equation}
so that $\gamma_2 = 0$ for a normal distribution, $\gamma_2 > 0$ for heavier, slower-decaying tails, and $\gamma_2 < 0$ for lighter, faster-decaying tails. Kurtosis tells us how rapidly the density decreases away from the central region. Skewness thus measures asymmetry and kurtosis the relative importance of the tails compared with a Gaussian; for the exam only a qualitative description of the two is required.

\begin{figure}[!htbp]
\centering
\includegraphics[page=55, width=\linewidth, keepaspectratio]{\pdf}
\caption{Skewness (left): in a positively skewed distribution the mean lies to the right of the median and mode, in a negatively skewed one to the left. Kurtosis (right): leptokurtic ($\gamma_2 > 0$, heavier tails), mesokurtic ($\gamma_2 = 0$, normal) and platykurtic ($\gamma_2 < 0$, lighter tails) distributions.}
\label{fig:skew_kurt}
\end{figure}

\section{The Normal Distribution}
\label{sec:normal}

\subsection{Gauss's law of errors}

By examining many experimental distributions of repeated measurements under the same conditions, and assuming there are no systematic errors, we can identify three general properties of random errors. First, \emph{symmetry}: positive and negative deviations of the same magnitude from the true value are equally probable, so the error distribution is symmetric about zero. Second, \emph{decreasing probability}: small errors are more probable than large ones, and the density decreases as the absolute error grows, approaching zero for infinitely large deviations. Third, the area between the density curve of the deviations and the horizontal axis, from $-\infty$ to $+\infty$, must be one. From these three assumptions and the principle of the arithmetic mean, Gauss showed heuristically that the distribution of the deviations in measurements affected by random errors is
\begin{equation}
\boxed{f(x) = \frac{1}{\sqrt{2\pi}\,\sigma}\,e^{-\frac{(x - \mu)^2}{2\sigma^2}}}
\label{eq:gaussian_pdf}
\end{equation}
which is called the \textbf{Gaussian function}, the normal law of error distribution, or simply the \textbf{normal distribution}. A random variable with this density is said to be normally distributed with mean $\mu$ and variance $\sigma^2$, and we write
\begin{equation}
X \sim \mathcal{N}(\mu, \sigma^2).
\end{equation}

\subsection{Mean and variance of a Gaussian}

Why are $\mu$ and $\sigma^2$ the mean and the variance of this PDF? We can check it with the change of variable based on the \textbf{normalised deviation}
\begin{equation}
\boxed{z = \frac{x - \mu}{\sigma}}
\label{eq:normalised_deviation}
\end{equation}
and with the Gaussian integral $\int_{-\infty}^{+\infty}e^{-z^2/2}\,dz = \sqrt{2\pi}$. For the mean, with $x = \sigma z + \mu$ and $dx = \sigma\,dz$,
\begin{align*}
\int_{-\infty}^{+\infty}x\,f_X(x)\,dx &= \frac{1}{\sqrt{2\pi}}\int_{-\infty}^{+\infty}(\sigma z + \mu)\,e^{-z^2/2}\,dz \\
&= \frac{1}{\sqrt{2\pi}}\left[\sigma\int_{-\infty}^{+\infty}z\,e^{-z^2/2}\,dz + \mu\int_{-\infty}^{+\infty}e^{-z^2/2}\,dz\right] \\
&= \frac{1}{\sqrt{2\pi}}\left[0 + \mu\sqrt{2\pi}\right] = \mu,
\end{align*}
because the first integrand is an odd function. For the variance we compute the mean of the squares with the change of variable $x = \sqrt{2}\sigma y + \mu$:
\begin{align*}
\int_{-\infty}^{+\infty}x^2f_X(x)\,dx &= \frac{1}{\sqrt{\pi}}\left[2\sigma^2\int_{-\infty}^{+\infty}y^2e^{-y^2}\,dy + 2\sqrt{2}\sigma\mu\int_{-\infty}^{+\infty}y\,e^{-y^2}\,dy + \mu^2\int_{-\infty}^{+\infty}e^{-y^2}\,dy\right] \\
&= \frac{1}{\sqrt{\pi}}\left[2\sigma^2\frac{\sqrt{\pi}}{2} + 0 + \mu^2\sqrt{\pi}\right] = \sigma^2 + \mu^2,
\end{align*}
where the first integral is evaluated by parts,
\begin{equation*}
\int_{-\infty}^{+\infty}y^2e^{-y^2}\,dy = 2\left\{\left[-\frac{y}{2}e^{-y^2}\right]_0^{+\infty} + \frac{1}{2}\int_0^{+\infty}e^{-y^2}\,dy\right\} = \frac{\sqrt{\pi}}{2}.
\end{equation*}
Therefore, by Equation~\eqref{eq:variance_continuous}, the variance is $\sigma^2 + \mu^2 - \mu^2 = \sigma^2$.

Gaussian variables have an important additive property: if $X_1, \dots, X_N$ are independent with $X_i \sim \mathcal{N}(\mu_i, \sigma_i^2)$, then
\begin{equation}
\boxed{\sum_{i=1}^{N}X_i \sim \mathcal{N}\Big(\sum_{i=1}^{N}\mu_i, \sum_{i=1}^{N}\sigma_i^2\Big)}
\label{eq:sum_gaussians}
\end{equation}
and if they all have the same distribution $\mathcal{N}(\mu, \sigma^2)$, their sum is $\mathcal{N}(N\mu, N\sigma^2)$ and their mean is $\mathcal{N}(\mu, \sigma^2/N)$, consistently with Equation~\eqref{eq:sigma_mean}.

\subsection{The standard normal distribution and the \texorpdfstring{$z$}{z} table}

A normal random variable $Z$ follows the \textbf{standard normal distribution} if $Z \sim \mathcal{N}(0, 1)$, i.e.\ if its density is
\begin{equation}
\boxed{f_Z(z) = \frac{1}{\sqrt{2\pi}}\,e^{-z^2/2}}
\label{eq:standard_normal}
\end{equation}
This expression contains neither $\sigma$ nor any other quantity depending on how the measurement was performed: only constants. Any normal variable can be brought to this form by \textbf{standardisation}: if $X \sim \mathcal{N}(\mu, \sigma^2)$, then
\begin{equation}
\boxed{Z = \frac{X - \mu}{\sigma} \sim \mathcal{N}(0, 1)}
\label{eq:standardisation}
\end{equation}
Standardisation does not improve the precision of a measurement; it removes the dependence of the distribution on the numerical value of $\sigma$, so that Gaussian measurements with different precision can be compared and combined on a common scale.

A fundamental consequence is that the probability of obtaining a standardised deviation within any two fixed limits does not depend on the precision of the measurement. In particular,
\begin{equation}
\boxed{\Pr(|z| \le 1) \approx 0.683, \qquad \Pr(|z| \le 2) \approx 0.955, \qquad \Pr(|z| \le 3) \approx 0.997}
\label{eq:sigma_rule}
\end{equation}
each obtained as $\frac{1}{\sqrt{2\pi}}\int_{-k}^{+k}e^{-z^2/2}\,dz$. Recalling that the deviation $d = x - x^*$ is related to the standardised one by $z = d/\sigma$, this means that for a normal distribution about 68.3\%, 95.5\% and 99.7\% of the values lie within one, two and three standard deviations of the mean, whatever the value of $\sigma$ (Figure~\ref{fig:normal_two_sigmas}). Geometrically, $\pm\sigma$ are the inflection points of the bell, where $f_X(\mu \pm \sigma) = 0.242/\sigma$ (Figure~\ref{fig:normal_sigma}).

\begin{figure}[!htbp]
\centering
\includegraphics[page=105, width=\linewidth, keepaspectratio]{\pdf}
\caption{Two normal distributions with the same mean $\mu = 100$ and different standard deviations, $\sigma = 2$ (left) and $\sigma = 5$ (right): in both cases 68.3\%, 95.5\% and 99.7\% of the values lie within $\pm\sigma$, $\pm2\sigma$ and $\pm3\sigma$ of the mean.}
\label{fig:normal_two_sigmas}
\end{figure}

\begin{figure}[!htbp]
\centering
\includegraphics[page=56, width=0.6\linewidth, keepaspectratio]{\pdf}
\caption{Geometrical meaning of $\sigma$ for $\mathcal{N}(\mu, \sigma^2)$: the points $\mu \pm \sigma$ are the inflection points of the curve, and the area between them is about 68\%.}
\label{fig:normal_sigma}
\end{figure}

The cumulative distribution function of the standard normal variable is the probability that $Z$ takes a value less than or equal to $x$:
\begin{equation}
\boxed{\Phi(x) = P(Z \le x) = \frac{1}{\sqrt{2\pi}}\int_{-\infty}^{x}e^{-z^2/2}\,dz}
\label{eq:phi_cdf}
\end{equation}
Because the standard normal distribution is symmetric about the vertical axis, $P(Z \le -x) = P(Z \ge x)$, or equivalently
\begin{equation}
\boxed{\Phi(-x) = 1 - \Phi(x)}
\label{eq:phi_symmetry}
\end{equation}
as shown in Figure~\ref{fig:phi_symmetry}.

\begin{figure}[!htbp]
\centering
\includegraphics[page=106, width=0.75\linewidth, keepaspectratio]{\pdf}
\caption{The cumulative distribution function of the standard normal is the area under $f(z)$ to the left of a given value. By symmetry, the area to the left of $-x$, $\Phi(-x) = P(Z \le -x)$ (left), equals the area to the right of $x$, $1 - \Phi(x)$, where $\Phi(x) = P(Z \le x)$ (right).}
\label{fig:phi_symmetry}
\end{figure}

The values of $\Phi$ are tabulated in the \emph{cumulative standard normal table}, where the entries inside the table are probabilities (areas) and the margins give the value of $z$. To find $\Phi(1.32)$, for example, we split $z = 1.3 + 0.02$ into row and column, and read the intersection of row $1.3$ and column $0.02$: $P(Z \le 1.32) = \Phi(1.32) = 0.9066$, meaning that 90.66\% of the probability lies to the left of $z = 1.32$. Exercise~\ref{ex:cell_diameter} shows how to use the table for a normally distributed biological quantity.

\subsection{The central limit theorem}
\label{sec:clt}

Consider $N$ independent and identically distributed random variables $X_i$, with mean $\mu$ and finite variance $0 < \sigma^2 < +\infty$. Two results hold. By the \emph{law of large numbers}, the sample mean $\bar{x}$ converges (almost surely, and therefore in probability) to the expected value $\mu$ as $N$ becomes very large. By the \textbf{central limit theorem}, the distribution of the sample mean $\bar{X} = \frac{1}{N}\sum_i X_i$ approaches a normal distribution with mean equal to the population mean and variance equal to the population variance divided by $N$; equivalently, the standardised mean converges in distribution to a standard normal:
\begin{equation}
\boxed{\bar{X} \xrightarrow[N \to \infty]{d} \mathcal{N}\Big(\mu, \frac{\sigma^2}{N}\Big), \qquad Z = \frac{\bar{X} - \mu}{\sigma/\sqrt{N}} \xrightarrow[N \to \infty]{d} \mathcal{N}(0, 1)}
\label{eq:clt}
\end{equation}
This holds even if the original variables are \emph{not} normally distributed. What do we mean by ``sufficiently large''? Conventionally, at least 30 measurements, a sample size that corresponds to a relative error on $s$ of slightly more than 10\%.

The central limit theorem implies an asymptotically normal convergence of the sample mean towards the population mean. To identify the population mean with the true value of the measured quantity, we implicitly assume that the distribution of the measurements, although its exact shape is unspecified, is symmetric about the true value $x^*$. As a consequence, the uncertainty of the mean, $\sigma/\sqrt{N}$, represents the half-width of an interval centred on $\bar{x}$ that contains the true value with a probability of about 68\% (in the absence of systematic bias), even when this interpretation does not apply to the individual measurements.

Gaussian variables have three further useful properties. Any linear combination of independent Gaussian variables is also normally distributed. The ratio of two independent, zero-mean Gaussian variables with non-zero variances, instead, follows a Cauchy distribution (the standard Cauchy distribution if both are standard normal). Finally, pooling raw measurements from Gaussian distributions with different standard deviations generally gives a non-Gaussian mixture; but standardising each set with its own mean and standard deviation, $Z_j = (X_j - \mu_j)/\sigma_j \sim \mathcal{N}(0, 1)$, gives the same standard normal distribution, so the standardised values can be compared and pooled in a single histogram despite their different precision.

\subsection{Percentiles}
\label{sec:percentiles}

The CDF $F_X(x) = P(X \le x)$ gives the probability that a random variable takes a value less than or equal to a threshold $x$. A \textbf{percentile} identifies the threshold below which a specified percentage of the distribution lies: the 90th percentile, for example, is the value $x_{90}$ such that $P(X \le x_{90}) = 0.90$, i.e.\ 90\% of the observations are expected to fall at or below it. For a continuous distribution with a uniquely defined quantile, $F_X(x) = \alpha \iff x = F_X^{-1}(\alpha)$; so $F_X(x) = 0.84$ means that $x$ is the 84th percentile.

For a standard normal variable and a real number $0 \le \alpha \le 1$, we call $z_\alpha$ the value that leaves a probability $\alpha$ in the upper tail:
\begin{equation}
\boxed{P(Z > z_\alpha) = \alpha \iff \Phi(z_\alpha) = P(Z \le z_\alpha) = 1 - \alpha}
\label{eq:z_alpha}
\end{equation}
$z_\alpha$ is the $(1 - \alpha) \times 100$th percentile: $Z$ is smaller than $z_\alpha$ in $(1 - \alpha) \times 100\%$ of the cases and larger in the remaining $\alpha \times 100\%$. This formula holds for the standard normal; for a general normal variable $X \sim \mathcal{N}(\mu, \sigma^2)$, $F_X(x_\alpha) = \Phi\big((x_\alpha - \mu)/\sigma\big) = 1 - \alpha$, and therefore
\begin{equation}
\boxed{x_\alpha = \mu + \sigma\,\Phi^{-1}(1 - \alpha) = \mu + \sigma z_\alpha}
\label{eq:normal_percentile}
\end{equation}
with $P(X < x_\alpha) = 1 - \alpha$ and $P(X > x_\alpha) = \alpha$ (here $F_X$ denotes the CDF of $X$, while $\Phi$ refers specifically to the standard normal CDF). Exercise~\ref{ex:percentile_protein} computes a percentile of a protein concentration.

\section{The \texorpdfstring{$\chi^2$}{Chi-Squared} and Student's \texorpdfstring{$t$}{t} Distributions}
\label{sec:chi2_t}

\subsection{The chi-squared distribution}

Take $N$ statistically independent standard normal variables, $Z_1, Z_2, \dots, Z_N \sim \mathcal{N}(0, 1)$. It can be shown that the new random variable
\begin{equation}
\boxed{X = Z_1^2 + Z_2^2 + \dots + Z_N^2 = \sum_{i=1}^{N}Z_i^2 \sim \chi_N^2}
\label{eq:chi2_definition}
\end{equation}
with $X \in [0, +\infty)$, is distributed according to the PDF
\begin{equation}
f(X; N) = K_N\,X^{\frac{N}{2} - 1}\,e^{-X/2},
\label{eq:chi2_pdf}
\end{equation}
where the constant $K_N$ is fixed by normalisation and $N$ is the number of \textbf{degrees of freedom}. $X$ is said to follow a $\chi^2$ (chi-square) distribution with $N$ degrees of freedom. Its main properties are
\begin{equation}
\boxed{\mu = N, \qquad \sigma^2 = 2N}
\label{eq:chi2_properties}
\end{equation}
and, if $X_1 \sim \chi_n^2$ and $X_2 \sim \chi_m^2$ are independent, $X_1 + X_2 \sim \chi_{n+m}^2$. As $N \to \infty$ the $\chi^2$ distribution tends to a normal distribution with the same mean and variance, $\chi_N^2 \to \mathcal{N}(N, 2N)$, and in practice the approximation is already reasonably good for $N > 30$.

The CDF is $P(X \le \chi_{N,\alpha}^2) = \int_0^{\chi_{N,\alpha}^2}f(x)\,dx$. Choosing a probability $\alpha$ for the upper tail ($0 \le \alpha \le 1$), we define the \textbf{critical value} $\chi_{N,\alpha}^2$ as the value of $X$ such that the probability of observing a larger value is $\alpha$:
\begin{equation}
\boxed{P(X > \chi_{N,\alpha}^2) = \alpha \iff F_X(\chi_{N,\alpha}^2) = 1 - \alpha}
\label{eq:chi2_critical}
\end{equation}
$\chi_{N,\alpha}^2$ is the $(1 - \alpha)$-quantile of the distribution and depends both on $N$ and on $\alpha$: in repeated measurements of $X$, $(1 - \alpha) \times 100\%$ of the values are expected to lie below it and $\alpha \times 100\%$ above it. Its values are given in the $\chi^2$ percentile table, with $N$ along the rows and $\alpha$ along the columns. Exercise~\ref{ex:localisation_3d} uses it to evaluate a 3D localisation error.

\subsection{Student's \texorpdfstring{$t$}{t} distribution}

Let $Z \sim \mathcal{N}(0, 1)$ and $X \sim \chi_N^2$ be independent. The random variable
\begin{equation}
\boxed{T = \frac{Z}{\sqrt{X/N}} \sim t_N}
\label{eq:t_definition}
\end{equation}
follows a \textbf{Student's $t$ distribution} with $N$ degrees of freedom, whose density is
\begin{equation}
f(t; N) = \frac{T_N}{\left(1 + \dfrac{t^2}{N}\right)^{\frac{N+1}{2}}},
\label{eq:t_pdf}
\end{equation}
where $T_N$ is a normalisation constant chosen so that the total area is one. Its mean is $\mu = 0$ for $N > 1$ (not defined for $N = 1$), and its variance is
\begin{equation}
\boxed{\sigma^2 = \frac{N}{N - 2} \quad \text{for } N > 2}
\label{eq:t_variance}
\end{equation}
(it diverges for $N \le 2$). As $N \to \infty$, $\left(1 + t^2/N\right)^{(N+1)/2} \to e^{t^2/2}$ and $T_N \to 1/\sqrt{2\pi}$, so the $t$ distribution tends to the standard normal, $t_N \to \mathcal{N}(0, 1)$; in practice, again, the approximation is reasonably good for $N > 30$.

For $0 < \alpha < 1$ we define $t_{N,\alpha}$ as the value that leaves a probability $\alpha$ in the right tail,
\begin{equation}
\boxed{P(T > t_{N,\alpha}) = \alpha \iff F_T(t_{N,\alpha}) = P(T \le t_{N,\alpha}) = 1 - \alpha}
\label{eq:t_critical}
\end{equation}
so $t_{N,\alpha}$ is the $(1 - \alpha)$ quantile, or the $100(1 - \alpha)$th percentile, of the distribution; its values are given in the Student's $t$ table. The $t$ distribution is symmetric about zero, so the probability to the left of $-t_{N,\alpha}$ is also $\alpha$: the lower-tail quantile is $-t_{N,\alpha} = t_{N,1-\alpha}$, $F_T(-t_{N,\alpha}) = \alpha$, and
\begin{equation}
\boxed{P(-t_{N,\alpha} \le T \le t_{N,\alpha}) = 1 - 2\alpha}
\label{eq:t_symmetric}
\end{equation}
For instance, with $N = 8$ and $\alpha = 0.10$, $t_{8,0.10} \approx 1.397$.

\subsection{From a normal sample to the \texorpdfstring{$t$}{t} distribution}
\label{sec:t_sample}

Why is this distribution so important? Suppose $X_1, \dots, X_N$ are independent observations from the same normal population, $X_i \sim \mathcal{N}(\mu, \sigma^2)$. Then the sample mean is distributed as $\bar{X} \sim \mathcal{N}(\mu, \sigma^2/N)$, and its standard error $\sigma/\sqrt{N}$ describes how much it varies from sample to sample. The sample variance $S^2 = \frac{1}{N - 1}\sum_i(X_i - \bar{X})^2$ satisfies
\begin{equation}
\boxed{\frac{(N - 1)S^2}{\sigma^2} = \frac{\sum_{i=1}^{N}(X_i - \bar{X})^2}{\sigma^2} \sim \chi_{N-1}^2}
\label{eq:sample_variance_chi2}
\end{equation}
and, for a normal sample, the sample mean and the sample variance are independent. Since $\sigma$ is typically unknown, we replace it with the sample standard deviation $S$, and the standardised sample mean (a standard normal divided by the square root of a $\chi^2_{N-1}$ over its degrees of freedom) follows a Student's $t$ distribution:
\begin{equation}
\boxed{t_{N-1} = \frac{\bar{X} - \mu}{S/\sqrt{N}}}
\label{eq:t_statistic_mean}
\end{equation}
The added uncertainty due to the estimate of $\sigma$ is what leads to the heavier tails of the $t$ distribution.

Why $N - 1$ degrees of freedom? Although the sum in $S^2$ contains $N$ terms, the deviations from the sample mean are not all independent: they must satisfy $\sum_i(X_i - \bar{X}) = 0$ (Equation~\eqref{eq:sum_deviations_zero}). Once $N - 1$ deviations are known, the last one is fixed by this constraint, so the sum of squared deviations has $N - 1$ degrees of freedom. If instead we summed the deviations from the known population mean $\mu$, there would be no estimated mean imposing the constraint, and $\sum_i(X_i - \mu)^2/\sigma^2 \sim \chi_N^2$. The degrees of freedom count the independent information in the sum, not simply the number of terms.

In practice, when the population standard deviation is unknown, the standardised deviation of a sample mean $\bar{x}$ from a hypothesised value $\mu$ is $t = (\bar{x} - \mu)/(s/\sqrt{N})$, where $s$ is the sample standard deviation; if the population is normal, it follows a $t$ distribution with $\nu = N - 1$ degrees of freedom. For small samples we must compare the computed $t$ with the critical values of the $t$ distribution, not of the standard normal, whereas for $N \gtrsim 35$ the normal distribution is often a good approximation. Student's $t$ is used to construct confidence intervals and to perform hypothesis tests on means.

The main distributions met so far are summarised in Table~\ref{tab:distributions_summary}.

\begin{table}[!htbp]
\centering
\caption{Summary of the main distributions.}
\label{tab:distributions_summary}
\begin{tabular*}{\textwidth}{l@{\extracolsep\fill}lllll}
\toprule
Distribution & Notation & Parameters & Mean $\mu$ & Variance $\sigma^2$ & St.\ dev.\ $\sigma$ \\
\midrule
Uniform & -- & $a < b$ & $(a + b)/2$ & $(b - a)^2/12$ & $(b - a)/\sqrt{12}$ \\
Poisson & $X \sim \text{Pois}(\lambda)$ & $\lambda > 0$ & $\lambda$ & $\lambda$ & $\sqrt{\lambda}$ \\
Normal & $X \sim \mathcal{N}(\mu, \sigma^2)$ & $\mu$, $\sigma > 0$ & $\mu$ & $\sigma^2$ & $\sigma$ \\
Chi-square & $X \sim \chi_N^2$ & $N$ d.o.f. & $N$ & $2N$ & $\sqrt{2N}$ \\
Student's $t$ & $X \sim t_N$ & $N$ d.o.f. & $0$ ($N > 1$) & $N/(N - 2)$ ($N > 2$) & $\sqrt{N/(N - 2)}$ \\
\bottomrule
\end{tabular*}
\end{table}

\section{Confidence Intervals}
\label{sec:confidence_intervals}

A sample mean provides a \emph{point estimate} of the population mean $\mu$, but a different sample would generally give a different mean. A \textbf{confidence interval} communicates the uncertainty of the estimate by giving a range. Let $X_1, \dots, X_N$ be a random sample from a population whose distribution depends on an unknown parameter. From the sample we compute a lower limit $L(X_1, \dots, X_N)$ and an upper limit $U(X_1, \dots, X_N)$, which form the confidence interval $\text{CI} = [L, U]$. With the convention used in the course, in which each of the two tails has probability $\alpha$, the interval has \textbf{confidence level} $1 - 2\alpha$: over repeated samples, the procedure produces intervals that contain the fixed true value $\mu$ with probability
\begin{equation}
\boxed{P(L \le \mu \le U) = 1 - 2\alpha}
\label{eq:ci_definition}
\end{equation}
The convention is illustrated in Figure~\ref{fig:ci_tails}.

\begin{figure}[!htbp]
\centering
\includegraphics[page=107, width=0.5\linewidth, keepaspectratio]{\pdf}
\caption{A confidence interval $[L, U]$ with confidence level $1 - 2\alpha$: the central area under the density is $1 - 2\alpha$, and each of the two tails has probability $\alpha$.}
\label{fig:ci_tails}
\end{figure}


To construct the interval we need a statistic with a known sampling distribution, and the choice depends on whether the population standard deviation is known. If $\sigma$ is known (case 1), we use the standard normal variable $Z = (\bar{X} - \mu)/(\sigma/\sqrt{N}) \sim \mathcal{N}(0, 1)$ and standard normal critical values. If $\sigma$ is unknown (case 2), we estimate it with $S$ and use $T = (\bar{X} - \mu)/(S/\sqrt{N}) \sim t_{N-1}$, with Student's $t$ critical values, which account for the extra uncertainty of estimating $\sigma$.

\subsection{Case 1: known population variance}

We express the difference between sample mean and population mean in standard-error units, $Z = (\bar{X} - \mu)/(\sigma/\sqrt{N})$, so that $\mu = \bar{X} - Z\sigma/\sqrt{N}$, and look for endpoints such that $P(L \le \mu \le U) = 1 - 2\alpha$. Substituting the expression for $\mu$,
\begin{equation*}
P\left(L \le \bar{X} - Z\frac{\sigma}{\sqrt{N}} \le U\right) = 1 - 2\alpha \quad \Longrightarrow \quad P\left(\frac{(\bar{X} - U)\sqrt{N}}{\sigma} \le Z \le \frac{(\bar{X} - L)\sqrt{N}}{\sigma}\right) = 1 - 2\alpha,
\end{equation*}
which isolates the standard variable. Now, from the CDF of the standard normal, $\Phi(z_\alpha) = P(Z \le z_\alpha) = 1 - \alpha$, and from its symmetry, $P(Z \ge z_\alpha) = P(Z \le -z_\alpha) = \alpha$; hence
\begin{equation*}
P(-z_\alpha \le Z \le z_\alpha) = P(Z \le z_\alpha) - P(Z \le -z_\alpha) = (1 - \alpha) - \alpha = 1 - 2\alpha.
\end{equation*}
Comparing the two expressions, the limits are $(\bar{X} - L)\sqrt{N}/\sigma = z_\alpha$ and $(\bar{X} - U)\sqrt{N}/\sigma = -z_\alpha$, so the $(1 - 2\alpha) \times 100\%$ confidence interval for $\mu$, knowing $\sigma$, is
\begin{equation}
\boxed{\bar{X} - z_\alpha\frac{\sigma}{\sqrt{N}} < \mu < \bar{X} + z_\alpha\frac{\sigma}{\sqrt{N}}}
\label{eq:ci_known_sigma}
\end{equation}
where $z_\alpha$ is the percentile of the standard normal below which $Z$ falls with probability $1 - \alpha$. The interval is centred at $\bar{X}$, and its \textbf{margin of error} is the cut-off $c = z_\alpha\sigma/\sqrt{N}$: a higher confidence increases the critical value and widens the interval, while a larger sample reduces the standard error and narrows it. The most common values are listed in Table~\ref{tab:z_values}.

\begin{table}[!htbp]
\centering
\caption{Standard normal critical values for the most common confidence levels.}
\label{tab:z_values}
\begin{tabular*}{\textwidth}{c@{\extracolsep\fill}cccc}
\toprule
Confidence level & $2\alpha$ & $\alpha$ & $P(Z \le z_\alpha)$ & $z_\alpha$ \\
\midrule
90\% & 0.10 & 0.05 & 0.950 & 1.645 \\
95\% & 0.05 & 0.025 & 0.975 & 1.960 \\
99\% & 0.01 & 0.005 & 0.995 & 2.576 \\
\bottomrule
\end{tabular*}
\end{table}

How large must the sample be for the $(1 - 2\alpha)$ confidence interval to have a total width no greater than $a$? Subtracting the lower endpoint from the upper one, the width is $W = U - L = 2z_\alpha\sigma/\sqrt{N}$: it depends on the confidence level, on the population variability and on the sample size. Requiring $W \le a$ and solving for $N$,
\begin{equation}
\boxed{N_{\min} = \left(\frac{2z_\alpha\sigma}{a}\right)^2}
\label{eq:sample_size}
\end{equation}
where $a$ is the total width (the margin of error is $a/2$). This formula applies when $\sigma$ is known and the interval uses the standard normal distribution. Exercise~\ref{ex:mitochondria} applies both results to cryo-EM counts.

\subsection{Case 2: unknown population variance}

Assume now that $X_1, \dots, X_N$ are independent observations from a normal population, $X_i \sim \mathcal{N}(\mu, \sigma^2)$, with both $\mu$ and $\sigma^2$ unknown. We estimate them with the sample mean and the sample variance $S^2$, and, as shown in Section~\ref{sec:t_sample}, the statistic $T = (\bar{X} - \mu)/(S/\sqrt{N})$ follows a $t$ distribution with $N - 1$ degrees of freedom, which accounts for the additional uncertainty caused by estimating $\sigma$. We proceed exactly as before. From the definition of $T$, $\mu = \bar{X} - TS/\sqrt{N}$, and the coverage condition $P(L < \mu < U) = 1 - 2\alpha$ becomes
\begin{equation*}
P\left(\frac{(\bar{X} - U)\sqrt{N}}{S} < T < \frac{(\bar{X} - L)\sqrt{N}}{S}\right) = 1 - 2\alpha.
\end{equation*}
For a two-sided interval we divide the total tail probability equally between the two tails, using the critical value $t_{N-1,\alpha}$ with $P(T > t_{N-1,\alpha}) = \alpha$; by symmetry, $P(-t_{N-1,\alpha} < T < t_{N-1,\alpha}) = 1 - 2\alpha$. Setting the bounds equal to these critical values and solving for the endpoints, the $(1 - 2\alpha) \times 100\%$ confidence interval for $\mu$, without knowing $\sigma$, is
\begin{equation}
\boxed{\bar{x} - t_{N-1,\alpha}\frac{s}{\sqrt{N}} < \mu < \bar{x} + t_{N-1,\alpha}\frac{s}{\sqrt{N}}}
\label{eq:ci_unknown_sigma}
\end{equation}
Once the sample has been observed and $\bar{x}$ and $s$ computed, the interval is centred at $\bar{x}$ with margin of error $c = t_{N-1,\alpha}\,s/\sqrt{N}$, where the critical value is read from the Student's $t$ table with $N - 1$ degrees of freedom (Exercise~\ref{ex:ci_fluorescence}).

Table~\ref{tab:ci_summary} summarises the two cases.

\begin{table}[!htbp]
\centering
\caption{Confidence intervals for the population mean $\mu$.}
\label{tab:ci_summary}
\begin{tabular*}{\textwidth}{p{3.9cm}@{\extracolsep\fill}p{2.9cm}p{1.9cm}p{4.3cm}}
\toprule
Assumption & Critical value & Margin $c$ & Confidence interval \\
\midrule
Normal population, $\sigma$ known; $Z = \frac{\bar{X} - \mu}{\sigma/\sqrt{N}} \sim \mathcal{N}(0, 1)$ & $z_\alpha$, with $P(Z > z_\alpha) = \alpha$ & $z_\alpha\dfrac{\sigma}{\sqrt{N}}$ & $\left[\bar{X} - z_\alpha\dfrac{\sigma}{\sqrt{N}},\; \bar{X} + z_\alpha\dfrac{\sigma}{\sqrt{N}}\right]$ \\[3ex]
Normal population, $\sigma$ unknown; $T = \frac{\bar{X} - \mu}{S/\sqrt{N}} \sim t_{N-1}$ & $t_{N-1,\alpha}$, with $P(T > t_{N-1,\alpha}) = \alpha$ & $t_{N-1,\alpha}\dfrac{s}{\sqrt{N}}$ & $\left[\bar{X} - t_{N-1,\alpha}\dfrac{s}{\sqrt{N}},\; \bar{X} + t_{N-1,\alpha}\dfrac{s}{\sqrt{N}}\right]$ \\
\bottomrule
\end{tabular*}
\end{table}

\section{Hypothesis Tests}
\label{sec:hypothesis_tests}

After a measurement we may want to know whether the results support or contradict a hypothesis about the phenomenon that produced them. In other words: would the observed result be plausible if our hypothesis were true? Data from a limited sample can never confirm or reject a hypothesis with absolute certainty, but they can provide evidence for or against it at a specified probability level. Such predictions are called \textbf{statistical hypotheses}: claims about a population, its distribution, or one or more of the parameters describing it, at a probability level defined by us.

A hypothesis test compares two competing claims about a population. The \textbf{null hypothesis} $H_0$ is the baseline claim being tested; the \textbf{alternative hypothesis} $H_1$ is the competing claim, describing the effect or difference of interest. The test uses the sample data to decide whether there is sufficient evidence to reject $H_0$ in favour of $H_1$. This controls the risk of rejecting a true $H_0$, but it does not prove whether $H_0$ is true or false. A hypothesis is \emph{simple} if it specifies the population distribution completely, and \emph{composite} if it allows more than one possible distribution.

The relevant information in the sample is summarised by a \textbf{test statistic}. We determine its distribution assuming that $H_0$ is true, and we use that distribution to define the rejection region; then we compare the observed statistic with this region. Before examining the data we choose two things. The first is the \textbf{significance level}, the probability of falling in the rejection region, i.e.\ of rejecting the hypothesis when it is true: $\alpha$ for one tail, $2\alpha$ for two tails. Common choices are $1 - 2\alpha = 0.955$ and $1 - 2\alpha = 0.997$, corresponding approximately to the central intervals within $2\sigma$ and $3\sigma$ of the mean for a Gaussian; other levels such as $1 - \alpha = 0.990$ and $0.999$ are also widely used, so typical values are $\alpha = 0.05, 0.003, 0.01, 0.001$. The second is the \textbf{rejection region} $C$, the set of outcomes for which $H_0$ is rejected with probability $\alpha$; its complement is the non-rejection (or acceptance) region. If the observed result falls in $C$ we reject $H_0$; otherwise we fail to reject it.

\subsection{Type I and type II errors}

Because a test uses random sample data, its decision can be wrong in two ways. A \textbf{type I error} occurs when $H_0$ is true but we reject it; its probability is the significance level, $P(\text{type I error}) = \alpha$ for a single tail ($2\alpha$ for a two-tailed test). A \textbf{type II error} occurs when $H_0$ is false but we fail to reject it; its probability is denoted by $\beta$. The four possible outcomes are summarised in Table~\ref{tab:test_errors}. Rejecting $H_0$ favours $H_1$, but does not prove that $H_1$ is true; keeping $H_0$ does not prove that it is true either.

\begin{table}[!htbp]
\centering
\caption{Test decisions and their probabilities.}
\label{tab:test_errors}
\begin{tabular*}{\textwidth}{l@{\extracolsep\fill}ll}
\toprule
Test decision & $H_0$ true & $H_0$ false \\
\midrule
Reject $H_0$ & Type I error: $\alpha = P(\text{reject } H_0 \mid H_0 \text{ true})$ & Correct rejection (power): $1 - \beta$ \\
Do not reject $H_0$ & Correct non-rejection: $1 - \alpha$ & Type II error: $\beta = P(\text{not reject } H_0 \mid H_0 \text{ false})$ \\
\bottomrule
\end{tabular*}
\end{table}

The probability of correctly rejecting a false $H_0$,
\begin{equation}
\boxed{\gamma = 1 - \beta = P(\text{reject } H_0 \mid H_0 \text{ false})}
\label{eq:power}
\end{equation}
is the \textbf{power} of the test. For a fixed sample size (and effect), making $\alpha$ smaller makes rejection harder and generally increases $\beta$: the test becomes more cautious about rejecting $H_0$, so genuine effects may be harder to detect. By widening or narrowing the rejection region we can reduce the probability of one type of error only at the cost of increasing the other. Which error matters more depends on the consequences. Suppose, for instance, that $H_0$ denotes a genuine signal event and $H_1$ a background event: a type I error then rejects a genuine signal (signal loss), while a type II error retains a background event as signal (signal contamination). If mistaking noise for a real signal is especially costly, we can design the test to reduce $\beta$. Increasing the sample size can often reduce $\beta$ while keeping $\alpha$ fixed, thereby increasing the power: a larger sample helps the test detect real effects more reliably.

Every decision in a hypothesis test therefore carries some risk of error. We must distinguish the decision we make from the true state of nature, which is unknown: a decision based on sample data cannot establish that state with certainty, only with a certain level of confidence.

\subsection{The steps of a test}

A hypothesis test always follows the same procedure:
\begin{enumerate}
    \item state the null hypothesis $H_0$ and the alternative $H_1$;
    \item choose a test statistic, calculated from the sample;
    \item determine its sampling distribution assuming that $H_0$ is true;
    \item choose a significance level, which sets the maximum acceptable probability of rejecting a true $H_0$;
    \item define the critical region $C$ using that distribution;
    \item calculate the observed statistic $T_{\text{obs}}$: if $T_{\text{obs}} \in C$, reject $H_0$; if $T_{\text{obs}} \notin C$, do not reject it.
\end{enumerate}
Failing to reject $H_0$ does not prove that it is true: it means that the sample did not provide sufficient evidence against it.

Let us apply it to the mean of a normal population. We draw an independent sample $X_1, \dots, X_n$ and want to test whether the population mean equals a specified value $\mu_0$: $H_0\colon \mu = \mu_0$ against $H_1\colon \mu \neq \mu_0$. The test statistic is the sample mean $\bar{X}$. If the variance $\sigma^2$ is \emph{known}, under $H_0$ we have $\bar{X} \sim \mathcal{N}(\mu_0, \sigma^2/n)$, so the standardised statistic is
\begin{equation}
\boxed{Z = \frac{\bar{X} - \mu_0}{\sigma/\sqrt{n}} \sim \mathcal{N}(0, 1)}
\label{eq:z_test}
\end{equation}
If the variance is \emph{unknown}, we use instead
\begin{equation}
\boxed{T = \frac{\bar{X} - \mu_0}{S/\sqrt{n}} \sim t_{n-1}}
\label{eq:t_test}
\end{equation}
After setting the significance level ($\alpha = 0.10, 0.05, 0.01, \dots$), we determine the critical region $C = \{\bar{X}\colon |\bar{X} - \mu_0| \ge c\}$ such that the probability of rejecting $H_0$ when it is true is no greater than the chosen level. Determining $C$ is equivalent to determining the same cut-off $c$ we found for confidence intervals: $c = z_\alpha\sigma/\sqrt{N}$ for known variance and $c = t_{N-1,\alpha}\,s/\sqrt{N}$ for unknown variance (Table~\ref{tab:ci_summary}).

\begin{figure}[!htbp]
\centering
\includegraphics[page=57, width=\linewidth, keepaspectratio]{\pdf}
\caption{One-sided test on the mean. Top: under $H_0$ (red curve), the rejection threshold $t_{N-1,\alpha}$ leaves a probability $\alpha$ in the upper tail. Bottom: under $H_1\colon \mu > \mu_0$ (cyan curve, shifted to the right), the same cut-off determines the probability $\beta$ of a type II error and the power $1 - \beta$.}
\label{fig:hypothesis_power}
\end{figure}

Figure~\ref{fig:hypothesis_power} shows the logic for a one-sided test with $H_1\colon \mu > \mu_0$. The rejection threshold is set under the null hypothesis, where $T \sim t_{N-1}$ and $P(T > t_{N-1,\alpha}) = \alpha$. If $t_{\text{obs}} > t_{N-1,\alpha}$ we reject $H_0$ in favour of $H_1$; if $t_{\text{obs}} < t_{N-1,\alpha}$ we do not reject it. If we reject $H_0$ but it was in fact true, we commit a type I error, with probability $\alpha = P(T > t_{N-1,\alpha} \mid H_0 \text{ true})$; if we keep $H_0$ but $H_1$ was true, we commit a type II error, with probability $\beta = P(T \le t_{N-1,\alpha} \mid H_1 \text{ true})$, determined by the same cut-off applied to the distribution under $H_1$. Exercise~\ref{ex:protein_test} works through this test.

\subsection{The p-value}

The \textbf{p-value} is the smallest significance level at which the observed data would lead us to reject the null hypothesis. Equivalently, assuming $H_0$ is true, it is the probability of obtaining a result at least as inconsistent with $H_0$ as the one observed. A small p-value (for example $0.05$ or less) indicates evidence against $H_0$, and the smaller the p-value, the stronger the evidence. In the fluorescence example of Exercise~\ref{ex:protein_test}, the p-value asks: how surprising would the observed result be if the treatment did not increase fluorescence?

The smallest overall significance level $2\alpha^*$ at which $H_0$ is rejected occurs when the observed statistic lies exactly on the critical boundary. For the known-$\sigma$ $z$-test this means $|\bar{X} - \mu_0| = c = z_{\alpha^*}\sigma/\sqrt{n}$, i.e.\ $z_{\alpha^*} = |\bar{X} - \mu_0|\sqrt{n}/\sigma$, where $z_{\alpha^*}$ is the standard normal value with upper-tail probability $\alpha^*$. Therefore the two-sided p-value is
\begin{equation}
\boxed{p = 2\alpha^* = 2\left[1 - \Phi\left(\frac{|\bar{X} - \mu_0|}{\sigma/\sqrt{n}}\right)\right]}
\label{eq:p_value_z}
\end{equation}
For the unknown-$\sigma$ $t$-test, analogously, $t_{n-1,\alpha^*} = |t_{\text{obs}}|$ and
\begin{equation}
\boxed{p = 2\alpha^* = 2\left[1 - F_{t_{n-1}}\left(\frac{|\bar{X} - \mu_0|}{S/\sqrt{n}}\right)\right]}
\label{eq:p_value_t}
\end{equation}
where $F_{t_{n-1}}$ is the CDF of the $t$ distribution with $n - 1$ degrees of freedom. Here $\alpha^*$ is the probability in each tail, so $2\alpha^*$ is the overall two-sided p-value, and $H_0$ is rejected whenever the chosen overall level $2\alpha$ is at least as large as $p$.

\subsection{One-sided and two-sided tests}

So far the null hypothesis stated that $\mu$ equals $\mu_0$, and we rejected it if the sample mean was much larger \emph{or} much smaller than $\mu_0$. In many situations, however, we want to test whether the population mean is at most $\mu_0$ against the alternative that it is greater, or vice versa. A \textbf{one-sided} (one-tailed) test checks whether a parameter differs from its null value in a specified direction, and its rejection region lies in one tail of the distribution; a \textbf{two-sided} (two-tailed) test checks whether it differs in either direction, and its rejection regions lie in both tails, so its significance level is $2\alpha$. The decision rules, with $z_\alpha$ and $t_{N-1,\alpha}$ the values with upper-tail probability $\alpha$, are collected in Table~\ref{tab:one_two_sided}.

\begin{table}[h]
\centering
\caption{Decision rules for one-sided and two-sided tests on the mean, with $z = \frac{\bar{X} - \mu_0}{\sigma/\sqrt{N}}$ (known variance) or $t = \frac{\bar{X} - \mu_0}{S/\sqrt{N}}$ (unknown variance).}
\label{tab:one_two_sided}
\begin{tabular*}{\textwidth}{l@{\extracolsep\fill}llll}
\toprule
Test & $H_0$ & $H_1$ & Reject $H_0$ if ($\sigma$ known) & Reject $H_0$ if ($\sigma$ unknown) \\
\midrule
Two-sided & $\mu = \mu_0$ & $\mu \neq \mu_0$ & $|z| \ge z_\alpha$ & $|t| \ge t_{N-1,\alpha}$ \\
One-sided right & $\mu \le \mu_0$ (or $\mu = \mu_0$) & $\mu > \mu_0$ & $z \ge z_\alpha$ & $t \ge t_{N-1,\alpha}$ \\
One-sided left & $\mu \ge \mu_0$ (or $\mu = \mu_0$) & $\mu < \mu_0$ & $z \le -z_\alpha$ & $t \le -t_{N-1,\alpha}$ \\
\bottomrule
\end{tabular*}
\end{table}

\subsection{Comparing two sample means}

A common case is the comparison of two independent samples from normal populations with unknown but equal variances, with sizes $n$ and $m$, means $\bar{x}$ and $\bar{y}$ and standard deviations $s_x$ and $s_y$. We test $H_0\colon \mu_1 = \mu_2$ against $H_1\colon \mu_1 \neq \mu_2$. The difference between the sample means, $\bar{X} - \bar{Y}$, estimates the difference between the population means. Since the two populations are assumed to have the same variance, we estimate the common variance by pooling the information from both samples.
