The sums of squared deviations of the two samples are $\sum_i(x_i - \bar{x})^2 = (n - 1)s_x^2$ and $\sum_j(y_j - \bar{y})^2 = (m - 1)s_y^2$; adding them and dividing by the total number of degrees of freedom, $(n - 1) + (m - 1) = n + m - 2$, gives the \textbf{pooled variance}
\begin{equation}
\boxed{s_p^2 = \frac{(n - 1)s_x^2 + (m - 1)s_y^2}{n + m - 2}}
\label{eq:pooled_variance}
\end{equation}
a weighted average of the two sample variances, each weighted by its degrees of freedom. The test statistic is
\begin{equation}
\boxed{T_{\text{obs}} = \frac{\bar{X} - \bar{Y} - 0}{s_p\sqrt{\dfrac{1}{n} + \dfrac{1}{m}}}, \qquad T \sim t_{n+m-2} \text{ under } H_0}
\label{eq:two_sample_t}
\end{equation}
where the degrees of freedom are those used to estimate the variance from the samples. The critical region is determined so that the probability of rejecting $H_0$ when it is true is no greater than the overall level $2\alpha$ (Exercise~\ref{ex:two_sided_test}).

\section{Treating a Direct Measurement}
\label{sec:summary_direct}

Some measurement errors are neither random nor systematic. Two adjacent digits may be transposed when recording a result, for example, producing a value that differs markedly from the others. This motivates the use of a clear rule to flag measurements for further examination. A common screening rule is to flag the values more than three standard deviations from the sample mean:
\begin{equation}
\boxed{|x_i - \bar{x}| > 3s}
\label{eq:three_sigma_rule}
\end{equation}
If the measurements follow a normal distribution, about 0.27\% of them are expected to fall beyond this range by chance alone: roughly 0.16 out of 60 measurements, or 2\,700 out of one million. So an extreme value in a small dataset may merit investigation, while large datasets can naturally contain many such values. Being more than $3s$ from the mean is a reason to investigate a measurement, not by itself a proof that it is wrong or a reason to remove it: a value should be excluded only when there is a sound reason, such as an identified recording or measurement error. This is the same lesson we learnt from Millikan's data in Chapter~\ref{ch:metrology}.

We can now summarise the procedure for direct measurements. First, check the measurement process and the instruments for possible systematic errors, and correct them by changing the instrument or the procedure, or by applying an appropriate correction. Second, repeat the measurement whenever possible, and use the results to estimate the random uncertainty: more repetitions generally improve the statistical reliability of the estimate. If repetitions are not possible, the smallest scale division of the instrument can serve as a conventional estimate of the uncertainty. Third, estimate the measured quantity with the sample mean $\bar{x}$ and, for $N$ repeated measurements, its uncertainty with
\begin{equation}
\boxed{s_{\bar{x}} = \frac{s}{\sqrt{N}}}
\label{eq:uncertainty_mean_summary}
\end{equation}
where $s$ is the sample standard deviation, the unbiased estimator of the population standard deviation. For normally distributed measurements without systematic errors, the interval $\bar{x} \pm s_{\bar{x}}$ has about 68\% coverage. Finally, inspect unusually distant measurements, especially in small datasets: a value more than $3s$ from the mean may warrant investigation, but it should be removed only if there is a sound reason to consider it erroneous, and if measurements are excluded, the mean and the uncertainty must be recalculated.

\section{Indirect Measurements and Propagation of Uncertainty}
\label{sec:indirect}

In an indirect measurement the result $F$ is computed from other measured quantities $x, y, z, \dots$, and we need to know how their uncertainties propagate to $F$.

\subsection{Linear combinations}

We have seen that a linear combination of independent, normally distributed measurements is also normally distributed (Section~\ref{sec:clt}). If
\begin{equation}
F = a_0 + \sum_i a_ix_i,
\end{equation}
then, by Equation~\eqref{eq:variance_linear_combination}, its variance is
\begin{equation}
\boxed{\sigma_F^2 = \sum_i a_i^2\sigma_i^2}
\label{eq:propagation_linear}
\end{equation}
and the standard uncertainty is $\sigma_F$. For a normally distributed result, the interval $F \pm \sigma_F$ contains about 68\% of the probability.

\subsection{General functions}

Any sufficiently smooth function can be approximated by a linear function near the measured values, through its first-order Taylor expansion (Equation~\eqref{eq:taylor_series_expansion}) around the point $(\bar{x}, \bar{y}, \bar{z}, \dots)$:
\begin{equation}
F(x, y, z, \dots) \approx F(\bar{x}, \bar{y}, \bar{z}, \dots) + \frac{\partial F}{\partial x}\bigg|_{\bar{x},\bar{y},\bar{z},\dots}(x - \bar{x}) + \frac{\partial F}{\partial y}\bigg|_{\bar{x},\bar{y},\bar{z},\dots}(y - \bar{y}) + \frac{\partial F}{\partial z}\bigg|_{\bar{x},\bar{y},\bar{z},\dots}(z - \bar{z}) + \dots
\end{equation}
This is a linear combination of the variables, with the partial derivatives as coefficients, so for independent inputs and small uncertainties the variance is approximately
\begin{equation}
\boxed{\sigma_F^2 \approx \sum_i\left(\frac{\partial F}{\partial x_i}\bigg|_{\bar{x}}\right)^2\sigma_i^2 = \left(\frac{\partial F}{\partial x}\right)^2\sigma_x^2 + \left(\frac{\partial F}{\partial y}\right)^2\sigma_y^2 + \left(\frac{\partial F}{\partial z}\right)^2\sigma_z^2 + \dots}
\label{eq:propagation_general}
\end{equation}
with the derivatives evaluated at the measured values. This is the \textbf{uncertainty propagation formula}; it relies on small uncertainties and statistically independent input variables.

\subsection{Products of powers}

A particularly useful case is a product of powers of independent variables,
\begin{equation}
F(x, y, z, \dots) = K\,x^\alpha y^\beta z^\gamma \cdots,
\end{equation}
with none of the variables equal to zero. Its partial derivatives are $\partial F/\partial x = \alpha F/x$, $\partial F/\partial y = \beta F/y$, and so on. Substituting them into the propagation formula gives $\sigma_F^2 \approx \alpha^2\frac{F^2}{x^2}\sigma_x^2 + \beta^2\frac{F^2}{y^2}\sigma_y^2 + \dots$, and therefore, dividing by $F^2$,
\begin{equation}
\boxed{\left(\frac{\sigma_F}{F}\right)^2 \approx \alpha^2\left(\frac{\sigma_x}{x}\right)^2 + \beta^2\left(\frac{\sigma_y}{y}\right)^2 + \dots}
\label{eq:propagation_powers}
\end{equation}
For products of powers it is the \emph{relative} uncertainties that combine in quadrature, each weighted by its exponent. The most common cases are collected in Table~\ref{tab:propagation_examples}.

\begin{table}[!htbp]
\centering
\caption{Examples of uncertainty propagation for independent variables.}
\label{tab:propagation_examples}
\begin{tabular*}{\textwidth}{l@{\extracolsep\fill}l}
\toprule
Function $z$ & Approximate variance $\sigma_z^2$ \\
\midrule
$z = ax + by$ & $\sigma_z^2 = a^2\sigma_x^2 + b^2\sigma_y^2$ \\[1ex]
$z = xy$ & $\sigma_z^2 = y^2\sigma_x^2 + x^2\sigma_y^2$, \quad i.e.\ $\left(\dfrac{\sigma_z}{z}\right)^2 = \left(\dfrac{\sigma_x}{x}\right)^2 + \left(\dfrac{\sigma_y}{y}\right)^2$ \\[2ex]
$z = x/y$ & $\sigma_z^2 = \dfrac{\sigma_x^2}{y^2} + \dfrac{x^2\sigma_y^2}{y^4}$, \quad i.e.\ $\left(\dfrac{\sigma_z}{z}\right)^2 = \left(\dfrac{\sigma_x}{x}\right)^2 + \left(\dfrac{\sigma_y}{y}\right)^2$ \\[2ex]
$z = x^2$ & $\sigma_z^2 = 4x^2\sigma_x^2$ \\
\bottomrule
\end{tabular*}
\end{table}

\subsection{Maximum errors}

Sometimes, instead of statistical uncertainties, we deal with \textbf{maximum errors}, which describe worst-case bounds. If each input is known as $x_i = x_{i,0} \pm \Delta x_i$, a linear combination $F = \sum_i a_ix_i$ changes at worst by
\begin{equation}
\boxed{\Delta F = \sum_i|a_i|\,\Delta x_i}
\label{eq:max_error_linear}
\end{equation}
For a non-linear function we use again the first-order Taylor expansion to estimate the maximum change, $\Delta F \approx \sum_i\left|\partial F/\partial x_i\right|\Delta x_i$, and for products of powers, $F = K\prod_i x_i^{a_i}$, this becomes a bound on the relative error:
\begin{equation}
\boxed{\frac{\Delta F}{|F|} \approx \sum_i|a_i|\frac{\Delta x_i}{|x_i|}}
\label{eq:max_error_powers}
\end{equation}
The difference with Equation~\eqref{eq:propagation_general} matters: maximum errors add in the worst-case direction, whereas independent statistical uncertainties combine through the sum of their squares. For example, if $F = x - 2y$ with $x = 10 \pm 0.2$ and $y = 5 \pm 0.1$, then $\Delta F = 0.2 + 2(0.1) = 0.4$.

A maximum error such as the half-range of Equation~\eqref{eq:half_range}, like any uncertainty, can be expressed in absolute or relative terms. The \textbf{absolute uncertainty} $\Delta x$ is expressed in the same unit as the measured quantity. The \textbf{relative uncertainty} $\Delta x/|x|$ (for $x \neq 0$) is dimensionless, often given as a percentage, and indicates the quality of the measurement relative to the measured value: it allows us to compare the significance of uncertainties across measurements of different magnitude. As an example, take $x = 10 \pm 0.2$ and $y = 5 \pm 0.1$ and their product $z = xy = 50$. For a product the first-order relative maximum error is
\begin{equation*}
\frac{\Delta z}{z} \approx \frac{\Delta x}{x} + \frac{\Delta y}{y} = \frac{0.2}{10} + \frac{0.1}{5} = 0.04,
\end{equation*}
so $\Delta z \approx 50 \times 0.04 = 2$ and $z \approx 50 \pm 2$. The exact range, $9.8 \times 4.9 \le z \le 10.2 \times 5.1$, i.e.\ $48.02 \le z \le 52.02$, confirms the first-order estimate of about $\pm 2$. Exercise~\ref{ex:cell_area} applies the statistical propagation to the area of a cell.

% =============================================================================
% CHAPTER 5: IMAGING AND POSITIONING SENSORS
% =============================================================================
\chapter{Imaging and Positioning Sensors}
\label{ch:imaging}

In this last chapter we apply what we have learnt about sensors, noise and uncertainty to two families of instruments. The first is that of \textbf{optical sensors}: we follow the historical evolution of light detectors, from the photomultiplier tube to single-pixel solid-state detectors, up to the cameras with millions of pixels that we use every day in the laboratory. The second is that of systems that measure \textbf{position, distance, motion and occupancy}, from satellite navigation to LiDAR, echolocation and simple presence sensors.

\section{Light as a Wave and as a Particle}
\label{sec:light_review}

We introduced light in Section~\ref{sec:light}, and we only recall the essentials here. Light is an electromagnetic wave with a corpuscular behaviour: the electric and magnetic fields oscillate in two mutually perpendicular planes, both perpendicular to the propagation direction ($\mathbf{E} \perp \mathbf{B} \perp \mathbf{k}$). Its main properties are the \textbf{intensity}, the \textbf{propagation direction}, the \textbf{frequency} and \textbf{wavelength}, and the \textbf{polarisation}, and each of them carries information that can be exploited for imaging and to learn something about the sample. Polarisation, for instance, is used to increase the contrast and see the boundary between a membrane and its surroundings.

We usually describe light only through its electric field, a sinusoidal signal $E(x,t) = E_0\cos(kx - \omega t + \phi)$ (Equation~\eqref{eq:wave_harmonics}), with wave number $k = 2\pi/\lambda$ and angular frequency $\omega = 2\pi f = 2\pi/T$. We will mostly write the frequency as $f$ rather than $\nu$, because in handwriting $\nu$ is easily confused with the speed $v$; and we must not confuse the frequency with the period $T$, which is its inverse. When light enters a medium with refractive index $n$, its frequency stays fixed while its wavelength and phase velocity change, $\lambda = \lambda_0/n$ and $v_{\text{p}} = c/n$ (Equation~\eqref{eq:phase_velocity}). Since $n$ depends on the wavelength, Snell's law bends each colour by a different angle, and a prism disperses white light into its components.

\subsection{Phase and polarisation}

The \textbf{phase} of a light wave is the angle-like quantity that represents the fraction of the cycle covered up to time $t$. Two waves can be in phase, or out of phase by any amount, for example by $180^\circ$ or by $40^\circ$ (Figure~\ref{fig:phase_polarisation}, top). Phase is widely used in microscopy, as in phase-contrast microscopy, or to distinguish two different fluorophores in a sample by measuring the phase difference between their signals.

The \textbf{polarisation} is the orientation of the oscillation of the electric field as the wave travels through space, i.e.\ the plane in which $\mathbf{E}$ oscillates. Since $\mathbf{E}$ is a vector, we can write it through its components in the $xy$ plane perpendicular to the propagation. In \textbf{linear polarisation} the field oscillates along a single direction, so only one component is present (for example $E_x$, with $E_y = 0$). In \textbf{circular polarisation} both components are present, with the same amplitude and shifted by a quarter of a cycle ($\pi/2$), so that the tip of the field rotates along a circle. The general case is \textbf{elliptical polarisation} (Figure~\ref{fig:phase_polarisation}, bottom). For our purposes it is enough to remember that polarisation tells us the plane in which the electric field oscillates.

\begin{figure}[!htbp]
\centering
\includegraphics[page=58, width=0.8\linewidth, keepaspectratio]{\pdf}
\caption{Top: a sine wave with its period, frequency and amplitude, and pairs of waves in phase, $180^\circ$ and $40^\circ$ out of phase. Bottom: linear, circular and elliptical polarisation of the electric field.}
\label{fig:phase_polarisation}
\end{figure}

\subsection{Wavefronts and Huygens' principle}

Consider a monochromatic wave propagating in space. A \textbf{wavefront} is a surface on which the wave has the same phase at a given instant: for example, all the points of a crest reach their maximum electric field at the same time. In a \textbf{plane wave} the wavefronts are parallel planes that advance perpendicularly to their surfaces (Figure~\ref{fig:wavefronts}). This is an idealisation, which holds only if we imagine the wave as infinitely extended. In practice we often deal with point sources of light, and a fluorophore is exactly that: light from a point source in a uniform, isotropic medium spreads radially outward, exactly as sound does, and its wavefronts are concentric spheres, shown as circles in cross-section. This is a \textbf{spherical wave}.

\begin{figure}[!htbp]
    \centering
    \begin{subfigure}[b]{0.5\linewidth}
        \centering
        \includegraphics[page=59, width=\linewidth, keepaspectratio]{\pdf}
        \caption{Plane wave.}
        \label{fig:plane_wave}
    \end{subfigure}
    \hspace{1cm}
    \begin{subfigure}[b]{0.3\linewidth}
        \centering
        \includegraphics[page=60, width=\linewidth, keepaspectratio]{\pdf}
        \caption{Spherical wave.}
        \label{fig:spherical_wave}
    \end{subfigure}
    \caption{Wavefronts of a plane wave, parallel planes advancing perpendicularly to their surfaces (A), and of a spherical wave emitted by a point source, concentric spheres (B).}
    \label{fig:wavefronts}
\end{figure}

How the wavefront evolves is described by \textbf{Huygens' principle}: every point of a wavefront acts as a source of secondary spherical wavelets. After a time $\Delta t$ each wavelet has expanded by a distance $v\Delta t$, where $v$ is the wave speed in the medium, and the new wavefront is the surface tangent to the forward edges of these wavelets, where they interfere constructively. This picture is important in practice: when we design an optical system, or simply choose a lens to control where and how light is focused, we are actually shaping the wavefront. A lens does nothing but curve the wavefront so that the light converges to a spot.

\subsection{Diffraction}

\textbf{Diffraction} is the spreading of light as it passes through an opening or around the edge of an obstacle. The Huygens--Fresnel principle explains how this happens: each point of the wavefront within the opening acts as a source of secondary wavelets, the contributions of the points outside the opening are lost, and beyond the opening the wavelets overlap and interfere, determining how the intensity varies with direction (Figure~\ref{fig:diffraction_slits}).

With a single slit of width $d$, in the forward direction ($\vartheta = 0$) all contributions arrive in phase and form a bright central maximum. At certain angles, instead, the contributions from different points of the slit cancel in pairs and produce dark fringes, whose positions are given by
\begin{equation}
\boxed{d\sin\vartheta_{\min} = m\lambda, \qquad m = \pm1, \pm2, \dots}
\label{eq:single_slit_minima}
\end{equation}
The position of the minima depends on the size of the slit and on the wavelength. As with the prism, then, different wavelengths are bent at different angles. With two slits separated by a distance $p$ the picture is slightly more complicated: fringes appear, with a maximum at zero angle and further maxima at the angles
\begin{equation}
\boxed{p\sin\vartheta_{\max} = m\lambda, \qquad m = 0, \pm1, \pm2, \dots}
\label{eq:grating_equation}
\end{equation}
where the integer $m$ is the \textbf{diffraction order}. The zero order is always a maximum, and in practice we typically see the first few orders.

\begin{figure}[!htbp]
\centering
\includegraphics[page=61, width=\linewidth, keepaspectratio]{\pdf}
\caption{Diffraction of a plane monochromatic wave by one slit (left), with minima at $d\sin\vartheta_{\min} = m\lambda$, and by two slits (right), with maxima at $p\sin\vartheta_{\max} = m\lambda$.}
\label{fig:diffraction_slits}
\end{figure}

A \textbf{diffraction grating} generalises this idea: it has many parallel grooves separated by a fixed distance $p$, the \textbf{grating period} (often given as lines per millimetre). Light from neighbouring grooves interferes constructively when the difference in path length is a whole number of wavelengths, and for light arriving perpendicular to the grating the bright maxima are again given by Equation~\eqref{eq:grating_equation}, with the angles $\vartheta_m$ measured from the grating normal. At the zero order all wavelengths overlap at $\vartheta = 0$, so the central maximum appears white. Each higher order $m = \pm1, \pm2, \dots$ spreads white light into a spectrum, and within the same order red light appears farther from the central maximum than blue light, because its wavelength is longer and, by Equation~\eqref{eq:grating_equation}, $\sin\vartheta_{\max}$ grows with $\lambda$ (Figure~\ref{fig:diffraction_grating}). This is very useful: if we have a source of light and we do not know which wavelengths it contains, we can place a diffraction grating in front of it and spread the light out on a screen or on a detector. We will come back to this in Section~\ref{sec:camera_specs}.

\begin{figure}[!htbp]
\centering
\includegraphics[page=62, width=\linewidth, keepaspectratio]{\pdf}
\caption{A diffraction grating spreads white light into its spectrum. Each order beyond the zero order contains all colours, with red diffracted at larger angles than blue.}
\label{fig:diffraction_grating}
\end{figure}

\subsection{Light as a particle and the photoelectric effect}

When we discussed the kilogram (Equation~\eqref{eq:planck_energy_quantum}) we saw that a quantum of light of frequency $f$ carries an energy proportional to $f$,
\begin{equation}
\boxed{E = hf}
\label{eq:photon_energy_hf}
\end{equation}
so light can be regarded not only as a wave but also as a stream of particles, the \textbf{photons}, whose energy comes in discrete packets. For visible light it is convenient to express this energy in electronvolts, and with $\lambda$ in nanometres the relation becomes
\begin{equation}
\boxed{E[\text{eV}] = \frac{1240\text{ eV nm}}{\lambda[\text{nm}]}}
\label{eq:photon_energy_ev}
\end{equation}
so visible photons carry a few electronvolts.

The corpuscular nature of light shows up clearly in the \textbf{photoelectric effect} (Figure~\ref{fig:photoelectric_effect}). When light impinges on a conductor (a metal), the energy of a photon can be enough to extract an electron. This happens when the photon energy $hf$ is larger than the energy $W$ that binds the electron, called the \textbf{work function}, which depends on the material. If $hf > W$, the electron is emitted with kinetic energy
\begin{equation}
\boxed{hf = W + K = W + \frac{1}{2}m_ev^2}
\label{eq:photoelectric_effect}
\end{equation}
For potassium, for instance, $2.0\text{ eV}$ are needed to eject an electron: red light at $700\text{ nm}$ ($1.77\text{ eV}$) extracts no electrons, whatever its intensity, while green light at $550\text{ nm}$ ($2.25\text{ eV}$) and violet light at $400\text{ nm}$ ($3.1\text{ eV}$) do, and the higher the photon energy, the faster the emitted electrons.

\clearpage
\begin{figure}[h]
\centering
\includegraphics[page=63, width=\linewidth, keepaspectratio]{\pdf}
\caption{The photoelectric effect. A photon with energy larger than the work function extracts an electron from a metal, and the excess energy becomes kinetic energy. The extracted electrons can be collected as a current (from Prof.\ Pisano's slides).}
\label{fig:photoelectric_effect}
\end{figure}

This should immediately ring a bell: extracting an electron means that we can measure a current, and this is an opportunity for sensing light. For imaging we can therefore use both faces of light. Its wave properties (intensity, phase, wavelength, polarisation) carry information about the sample, while its particle nature allows us to convert light into an electrical signal. All the detectors we are going to see exploit this conversion, each in a different way.

\section{Light Sensors and the Photomultiplier Tube}
\label{sec:light_sensors}

The most spectacular light sensor in the world, which we are still unable to reproduce, is the \textbf{retina}. Its sensing part is made of rods and cones, which are extremely sensitive (down to single photon detection) and are the basis for differential sensitivity, colour vision and spatial vision. They are followed by the retinal circuitry, which performs signal conditioning and processing before the signal travels along the axons of the ganglion cells and the optic nerve. Part of the visual processing even happens in regions of the brain of which we are not conscious. This is revealed by the so-called \emph{blindsight}: some patients who are blind, because the part of the visual pathway we are aware of is damaged, can still cross a room avoiding every obstacle placed in front of them, because another part of the brain keeps processing the visual information without them knowing it.

Our instruments are far less powerful, but they follow the same logic: a sensing element, a conditioning stage and a processing stage. When a detector absorbs a photon, the photon transfers its energy to the detector material, and the detectors we introduce here use this energy to produce a measurable electrical signal. We will see three families: \textbf{photomultiplier tubes} (PMTs), where the photon is absorbed by a metal (the photocathode); \textbf{photodiodes}, where the photon is absorbed by a semiconductor; and \textbf{image sensors} (CCD and CMOS), where the photon is absorbed by an array of semiconductor light-sensitive pixels.

\subsection{Photomultiplier tubes}

The \textbf{photomultiplier tube} (PMT) is the oldest of the detectors we will see. PMTs are no longer very widespread, but they help to visualise the detection process and to understand how the technology evolved. A PMT exploits the photoelectric effect to detect light, and amplifies the signal through a chain of multiplications of the photoelectrons. PMTs provide high sensitivity (down to single photons), high speed (nanosecond response to an impulse) and low noise.

A PMT is a vacuum tube that hosts an optical window receiving the incoming light (usually borosilicate or UV-transmitting glass), a \textbf{photocathode} where the emission of electrons happens, focusing elements that steer the photoelectrons, an electron multiplier stage made of a series of \textbf{dynodes} (usually 8--12) that multiply the electrons by secondary emission in a cascade, and a final \textbf{anode}, or collector (Figure~\ref{fig:pmt_scheme}). A high voltage (1--2\,kV) is held between the photocathode and the anode, divided into several steps along the dynode chain, so that each dynode is at a negative potential with respect to the following one.

\clearpage
\begin{figure}[h]
\centering
\includegraphics[page=65, width=0.65\linewidth, keepaspectratio]{\pdf}
\caption{Scheme of a photomultiplier tube: the incoming photon hits the photocathode, and the photoelectron is multiplied along the dynode chain, kept at increasing potentials by a voltage-dropping resistor chain, before being collected at the anode (from Prof.\ Pisano's slides).}
\label{fig:pmt_scheme}
\end{figure}

The detection process works as follows. Photons enter the tube through the input window and strike the photocathode, which emits electrons into the vacuum through the photoelectric effect: these are the \textbf{photoelectrons}. A focusing electrode directs them toward the first dynode, and the potential difference accelerates them so much that, when they strike the dynode, they release additional electrons by secondary emission; this is why a very high voltage is needed. The process repeats across the dynodes, each electron extracting more electrons at every step, so that the number of electrons is multiplied into an amplified cascade, hence the name \emph{photomultiplier}. Finally, the anode collects the electrons from the last dynode, generating an electrical pulse, a current spike, which the detection electronics register and process.

The photocathode is usually made of alkali antimonides, which release the first electron easily. Its spectral response and its noise depend on the material: SbKCs, for instance, is used for the visible and GaAs for the near infrared (Figure~\ref{fig:pmt_photocathode}). This is why, every time we buy a sensor, we should not be scared to open its \emph{specifications} (the ``spec sheet''): they tell us how the sensor responds depending on the wavelength, and if our light falls where the response is low, we know that we should change sensor. The figure of merit here is the \textbf{quantum efficiency}, the fraction of incoming photons that produce a photoelectron:
\begin{equation}
\boxed{\text{Q.E.} = \frac{\text{photoelectrons}}{\text{incoming photons}}}
\label{eq:quantum_efficiency}
\end{equation}
which is typically about 35\% for a PMT. This definition is universal and holds for any photodetector, from PMTs to photodiodes to cameras. For PMTs the response is also often reported as \textbf{radiant sensitivity} (mA/W), the current produced per unit of incident light power. The dynodes, in turn, provide on average $\delta$ secondary electrons for each impinging electron; $\delta$ depends on the energy of the impinging electron, and therefore on the voltage division. Dynodes are made of metal alloys (e.g.\ beryllium--copper) coated with alkali antimonides, materials that easily release electrons when struck by other electrons.

\begin{figure}[!htbp]
    \centering
    \begin{subfigure}[b]{0.55\linewidth}
        \centering
        \includegraphics[page=66, width=\linewidth, keepaspectratio]{\pdf}
        \caption{Radiant sensitivity of different photocathodes.}
        \label{fig:pmt_spectral_response}
    \end{subfigure}
    \hfill
    \begin{subfigure}[b]{0.38\linewidth}
        \centering
        \includegraphics[page=67, width=\linewidth, keepaspectratio]{\pdf}
        \caption{Secondary emission ratio $\delta$.}
        \label{fig:pmt_dynode_delta}
    \end{subfigure}
    \caption{The spectral response of a PMT depends on the photocathode material and window (A); the number of secondary electrons $\delta$ released by a dynode grows with the energy of the impinging electron (B) (from Prof.\ Pisano's slides).}
    \label{fig:pmt_photocathode}
\end{figure}

\subsection{Response, gain and noise of a PMT}

The temporal response of a PMT is described by two parameters (Figure~\ref{fig:pmt_response}). The \textbf{response pulse width} is the full width at half maximum (FWHM) of the anode current pulse delivered in response to an ideal light pulse, and the \textbf{rise time} is the time the output takes to go from 10\% to 90\% of its final value, in response either to a step or to a delta stimulus. PMTs have rise times of the order of nanoseconds, and nowadays even below the nanosecond: this speed is one of the reasons why they are still used in some applications. These curves should look familiar: they are essentially the impulse and step responses of a low-pass filter (Figure~\ref{fig:first_order_transient_responses}), only a bit wider and more S-shaped, because the cascade of electrons needs some time to travel through the tube before the output is collected. What we learnt about low-pass filters applies to almost all sensors.

\clearpage
\begin{figure}[!htbp]
    \centering
    \begin{subfigure}[b]{0.38\linewidth}
        \centering
        \includegraphics[page=68, width=\linewidth, keepaspectratio]{\pdf}
        \caption{Anode current upon a pulse.}
        \label{fig:pmt_anode_pulse}
    \end{subfigure}
    \hfill
    \begin{subfigure}[b]{0.55\linewidth}
        \centering
        \includegraphics[page=69, width=\linewidth, keepaspectratio]{\pdf}
        \caption{Pulse width and rise times.}
        \label{fig:pmt_rise_time}
    \end{subfigure}
    \caption{Temporal response of a PMT: an example of anode current in response to a light pulse (A), and the definitions of response pulse width to a delta stimulus, rise time to a step and rise time to a delta stimulus (B) (from Prof.\ Pisano's slides).}
    \label{fig:pmt_response}
\end{figure}

The \textbf{gain} of a PMT is the ratio between the anode current and the photocathode current. If each of the $N$ dynodes multiplies the electrons by $\delta$, then
\begin{equation}
\boxed{G = \delta^N \approx K\,V^{\alpha N}}
\label{eq:pmt_gain}
\end{equation}
where $K$ is a constant, $V$ the supply voltage and $\alpha$ a coefficient between $0.6$ and $0.8$ that depends on the dynode material. PMTs provide a substantial gain, up to $10^7$--$10^8$, which is even higher than that of some solid-state detectors; the order of magnitude is what we should remember. The gain grows with the number of dynodes and depends strongly on the supply voltage (Figure~\ref{fig:pmt_gain}): a higher voltage between the electrodes increases the probability of extracting electrons at each step.

\clearpage
\begin{figure}[h]
\centering
\includegraphics[page=70, width=0.7\linewidth, keepaspectratio]{\pdf}
\caption{The gain of a PMT grows strongly with the supply voltage and with the number of dynode stages (from Prof.\ Pisano's slides).}
\label{fig:pmt_gain}
\end{figure}

Like every photodetector, a PMT also has a \textbf{dark current}: a small current that flows even in the dark, and that is not linked to the arrival of photons. It is mainly due to thermal energy, which occasionally excites an electron and produces a fake pulse. Its causes include thermionic emission from the photocathode and dynode surfaces, ohmic leakage between the anode and the other electrodes or pins, scintillation from the glass envelope or the electrode supports, field emission, ionisation of residual gases (ion feedback), and scintillation of the glass caused by cosmic rays, radioisotopes contained in the glass or environmental gamma rays. The average dark current of any detector can be measured simply by acquiring in a room where all lights are switched off.

Can we detect any wavelength with a PMT? It depends on the photocathode. Very high-energy photons, such as the gamma rays (above about $100\text{ keV}$) of medical applications, fall outside the spectral sensitivity of the PMT, and a higher energy is not necessarily better. In this case the PMT is coupled with a \textbf{scintillator}, a material that produces luminescence when it receives ionising radiation and converts the high-energy photons into lower-energy photons that the photocathode can detect (Figure~\ref{fig:pmt_scintillator}). Scintillators come in many forms: crystals (e.g.\ bismuth germanate, BGO), organic materials (polyethylene naphthalate), gases (N, Xe) and glasses (borosilicate). An important application is \textbf{positron emission tomography} (PET), where scintillators are used to detect the gamma rays emitted by the tracer. The same idea can be used even when the light is within the sensitivity range, simply to bring it to the interval where the photocathode is optimal and increase the quantum efficiency.

\clearpage
\begin{figure}[h]
\centering
\includegraphics[page=71, width=0.7\linewidth, keepaspectratio]{\pdf}
\caption{A scintillator converts high-energy gamma radiation into scintillation photons, which are then detected by the photomultiplier (from Prof.\ Pisano's slides).}
\label{fig:pmt_scintillator}
\end{figure}

\subsection{From PMTs to solid-state detectors}

PMTs, then, are very sensitive and very fast, but they are expensive, bulky and fragile (they contain a vacuum tube), they need a very high voltage to accelerate the electrons, and they are sensitive to magnetic fields, which deflect the moving electrons: they cannot be used where strong magnetic fields are present. These drawbacks explain the move to detectors based on semiconductors, which offer a \textbf{lower cost}, a \textbf{reduced footprint} and an \textbf{easier use}, without the need for a very high voltage. Table~\ref{tab:pmt_vs_photodiode} compares a PMT with a conventional photodiode.

\begin{table}[!htbp]
\centering
\caption{Comparison between photomultiplier tubes and conventional photodiodes.}
\label{tab:pmt_vs_photodiode}
\begin{tabular*}{\textwidth}{p{3.2cm}@{\extracolsep\fill}p{5.4cm}p{5.4cm}}
\toprule
Feature & PMT & Conventional photodiode \\
\midrule
Sensitivity to very weak light & Very high: internal electron multiplication can make single-photon detection possible & Lower: the signal is small, so detecting very weak light depends more on the readout amplifier \\
Output & Amplified current or pulses & Current proportional to the incident light, without internal gain \\
Size and robustness & Usually larger and more fragile; contains a vacuum tube & Compact, solid-state and generally robust \\
Operating voltage & Requires high voltage & Usually requires low voltage \\
Magnetic fields & Sensitive to magnetic fields & Generally much less sensitive \\
Cost & Often more expensive & Typically simpler to operate and less expensive \\
Best suited to & Ultra-low-noise photon measurements (mostly discontinued) & Moderate or bright light, compact instruments and imaging arrays \\
\bottomrule
\end{tabular*}
\end{table}

\section{Photodiodes, APDs, SPADs and SiPMs}
\label{sec:photodiodes}

\subsection{Conductors, semiconductors and insulators}
\label{sec:energy_bands}

To understand why solid-state detectors are built from semiconductors, we recall how materials are classified according to their electrical conductance. In a solid, where many atoms are placed close together, electrons occupy ranges of allowed energies called \textbf{energy bands}, and the conductivity of the material depends on which bands are occupied and on whether electrons can move into nearby empty states. The \textbf{valence band} is the highest energy band filled with electrons, and its upper edge is $E_v$. A completely filled band carries no net current; when an electron leaves it, it creates a \textbf{hole}, and since other valence electrons can move into the empty state, the hole behaves as a mobile positive charge. The \textbf{conduction band} is the next higher band of allowed energies, empty at absolute zero, with lower edge $E_c$: electrons excited into this band can drift under an applied electric field and contribute to the current. The difference between the two edges is the \textbf{band gap}, $E_g = E_c - E_v$.

In a \textbf{conductor} a band is partially filled, or two bands overlap (the conduction band lies within the valence band), so electrons can readily move into nearby empty states and carry current when an electric field is applied. In an \textbf{insulator} a large band gap separates the two bands, and a lot of energy is needed to excite charges into the conduction band. A \textbf{semiconductor} lies in between: a relatively small band gap separates the valence and conduction bands, about $1.1\text{ eV}$ in silicon at room temperature (Figure~\ref{fig:energy_bands}). Thermal excitation can promote a small fraction of electrons across the gap, and an absorbed photon can do the same if its energy satisfies
\begin{equation}
\boxed{E_{\text{ph}} = h\nu \ge E_g}
\label{eq:bandgap_condition}
\end{equation}
In either case the electron enters the conduction band and leaves behind a hole in the valence band, creating an \textbf{electron--hole pair}. Both carriers can carry current, and this is the basis of semiconductor light detection.

\begin{figure}[!htbp]
\centering
\includegraphics[page=64, width=0.7\linewidth, keepaspectratio]{\pdf}
\caption{Energy bands of a conductor, where valence and conduction bands overlap (left), of a semiconductor, where a photon with $h\nu \ge E_G$ can promote an electron across the gap (centre), and of an insulator, with a large band gap (right).}
\label{fig:energy_bands}
\end{figure}

Semiconductors are the materials of choice for photon detection precisely because their band gap is comparable with the energy of visible photons. The band gap of silicon is $E_{G,\text{Si}} = 1.12\text{ eV}$, while a green photon ($\lambda \approx 500\text{ nm}$) carries $E_{\text{ph}} = hf = 2.50\text{ eV}$: when it hits silicon, it can promote an electron to the conduction band and leave a hole in the valence band. This is also the principle of photovoltaics. Typical semiconductor elements, such as silicon and germanium, are in group 14 (once known as group IV) of the periodic table; alternatively, combinations of groups 13--15 (group III--V) are used, such as GaN or GaAs (for example for LEDs). The choice depends on the application: depending on whether we need to detect photons in the infrared or closer to the UV, we have to choose the right detector, or the right camera, made of the right material.

\subsection{Photon detection with a semiconductor}

We have just seen that a photon with $h\nu \ge E_g$, absorbed in a semiconductor, creates an electron--hole pair. If we apply a voltage across the semiconductor, the resulting electric field drives the photogenerated electrons and holes toward opposite contacts, producing a \textbf{photocurrent} (Figure~\ref{fig:semiconductor_detection}). If we simply take a piece of semiconductor and do nothing else, however, some carriers recombine with carriers of the opposite charge before reaching the contacts, reducing the amount of collected charge, and we may see nothing at all.

\begin{figure}[!htbp]
\centering
\includegraphics[page=72, width=0.75\linewidth, keepaspectratio]{\pdf}
\caption{An absorbed photon creates an electron--hole pair in a semiconductor; an applied voltage drives the two carriers toward opposite contacts, producing the photocurrent $I_{\text{ph}}$.}
\label{fig:semiconductor_detection}
\end{figure}

\subsection{The depletion region and the p--n junction}

To reduce the losses from recombination, a photodiode is designed so that the incoming photons can reach, and be absorbed in, a \textbf{depletion region}: a region that contains very few mobile charge carriers in the dark and a built-in electric field, which quickly drives the electron and the hole toward opposite contacts, helping them reach the contacts before they recombine. The depletion region is the sensitive region of the detector.

A depletion region forms at the junction between two semiconductor regions, one rich in mobile electrons and the other rich in mobile holes. We obtain them by \textbf{doping}, i.e.\ by including in the crystal elements with an excess of negative (n-doping) or positive (p-doping) charges (Figure~\ref{fig:doping}). In the common case of silicon, if we implant boron atoms in one part of the crystal we obtain a \textbf{p-type} material: an electron is missing in one of the bonds with the surrounding silicon atoms, i.e.\ there is a hole of positive charge. If we implant antimony (Sb) atoms in another part we obtain an \textbf{n-type} material, with an additional electron that cannot take part in any bond and carries a negative charge.

\clearpage
\begin{figure}[h]
\centering
\includegraphics[page=73, width=0.75\linewidth, keepaspectratio]{\pdf}
\caption{Crystal lattice of pure silicon, of p-type silicon doped with boron (a missing electron, i.e.\ a hole) and of n-type silicon doped with antimony (an additional electron).}
\label{fig:doping}
\end{figure}

When the two materials are put in contact to form a \textbf{p--n junction}, the free charges diffuse across the interface: the free electrons of the n side move toward the p side and recombine with the holes. Near the junction we are left with fixed ions, negative on the p side and positive on the n side, and with very few mobile carriers (Figure~\ref{fig:depletion_region}). These fixed charges of opposite sign create a \textbf{built-in electric field} $\vec{E}$, and the process reaches an equilibrium in which diffusion is counterbalanced by a voltage difference, the \textbf{built-in voltage}. This is very convenient: when an absorbed photon creates an electron--hole pair in this region, the field immediately drives the electron and the hole toward opposite contacts. The separation reduces the chance of recombination, and the motion of the charges produces a photocurrent.

\begin{figure}[!htbp]
\centering
\includegraphics[page=74, width=0.38\linewidth, keepaspectratio]{\pdf}
\caption{At the p--n junction, fixed ions of opposite sign leave a depletion region with a built-in electric field (top); an electron--hole pair created there by a photon is separated by the field (bottom).}
\label{fig:depletion_region}
\end{figure}

At the end of the story the p--n junction is a diode, and it can be operated in two ways (Figure~\ref{fig:pn_bias}). In \textbf{forward bias} the applied voltage narrows the depletion region, and the forward current rises rapidly as the voltage increases. In \textbf{reverse bias} the depletion region widens and, in the dark, only a small reverse current (the dark, or leakage, current) flows below the breakdown voltage. Reverse bias is the operating mode used for detection: the diode ordinarily does not conduct, but when a photon of sufficient energy enters the depletion region it creates an electron--hole pair, and the reverse-bias field sweeps the carriers out of the region, creating a current. When we operate one of these detectors, therefore, we must make sure that the electrodes are connected in the right direction and that we apply the right voltage.

\begin{figure}[!htbp]
\centering
\includegraphics[page=75, width=\linewidth, keepaspectratio]{\pdf}
\caption{A p--n junction in reverse bias (left), where the depletion region widens, and in forward bias (right), where it narrows; in the centre, the I--V curve of the diode in the dark.}
\label{fig:pn_bias}
\end{figure}

The location where the photon is absorbed determines whether the pair contributes to the photocurrent (Figure~\ref{fig:pn_absorption}). Inside the depletion region the built-in field separates the pair: the electron drifts toward the n side and the hole toward the p side, producing a photocurrent in the external circuit. In the neutral p and n regions, a minority carrier can diffuse to the depletion region and then be swept across by the field, but the farther from the junction it is generated, the more likely it is to recombine before reaching it.

\begin{figure}[!htbp]
\centering
\includegraphics[page=76, width=0.85\linewidth, keepaspectratio]{\pdf}
\caption{Electron--hole pairs generated in the neutral regions may recombine (left), whereas those generated in the depletion region are separated by the field (right).}
\label{fig:pn_absorption}
\end{figure}

The same principle has two applications (Figure~\ref{fig:photodiode_solar_cell}). A \textbf{photodiode} uses the photocurrent as a signal to measure the incident light, while a \textbf{solar cell} uses the illuminated junction to deliver electrical power to a load. All the light detectors and solar cells we see around are based on the p--n junction or on its variations.

\begin{figure}[h]
\centering
\includegraphics[page=77, width=0.8\linewidth, keepaspectratio]{\pdf}
\caption{Two applications of the same principle: in a photodiode the meter measures the photocurrent (left), in a solar cell the junction powers a load (right).}
\label{fig:photodiode_solar_cell}
\end{figure}

\subsection{PIN photodiodes and avalanche photodiodes}

A first evolution of the p--n junction is the \textbf{PIN photodiode}, which has an intrinsic (undoped) semiconductor region between the p-type and the n-type regions, creating a wider depletion region (Figure~\ref{fig:pin_apd}). In a wider depletion region, electron--hole pairs generated deeper in the silicon can also be collected before they recombine: the intrinsic region helps to absorb light and collect charge, and improves the quantum efficiency, especially at longer wavelengths, which penetrate deeper into silicon. The photocurrent is produced without internal multiplication, so a downstream amplification step is needed. The operation is simple and low-noise, and PIN photodiodes are suited to moderate light levels.

An \textbf{avalanche photodiode} (APD) adds another region. Like a PIN photodiode, it can have a wide absorption region, intrinsic or lightly doped, but it also has a \textbf{multiplication region} (the avalanche area), where a strong electric field, obtained thanks to a strong reverse bias, accelerates the charge carriers enough to create additional electron--hole pairs by \textbf{impact ionisation}. This brings us back to the photomultiplier: the carriers create an avalanche that multiplies the photocurrent, so the signal is amplified locally, inside the detector and in a very small footprint. The layers must be properly designed to obtain this region, and a fairly strong reverse bias is required, although not as strong as the voltage of a PMT. APDs are more sensitive to weak light and have a higher gain, but they require a higher bias and introduce additional noise.

\clearpage
\begin{figure}[h]
    \centering
    \begin{subfigure}[b]{0.5\linewidth}
        \centering
        \includegraphics[page=78, width=\linewidth, keepaspectratio]{\pdf}
        \caption{PIN photodiode.}
        \label{fig:pin_photodiode}
    \end{subfigure}
    \hspace{1cm}
    \begin{subfigure}[b]{0.3\linewidth}
        \centering
        \includegraphics[page=79, width=\linewidth, keepaspectratio]{\pdf}
        \caption{Avalanche photodiode.}
        \label{fig:apd}
    \end{subfigure}
    \caption{A PIN photodiode has an intrinsic region that widens the depletion region (A); an APD also has a multiplication region, where impact ionisation creates an avalanche of carriers (B).}
    \label{fig:pin_apd}
\end{figure}

A standard photodiode (p--n or PIN) therefore converts light directly into a current with no internal gain, whereas an APD uses a high reverse voltage to multiply the charge carriers internally, for a much higher sensitivity to weak light. A few specifications are central to understanding the performance of a photodiode, and we find them in any catalogue: the \textbf{spectral sensitivity}, which depends on the material and on its coating; the \textbf{noise equivalent power} (NEP), the minimum detectable signal; and the \textbf{junction capacitance}, which inversely influences the frequency response, i.e.\ how fast the detector responds. For an APD, the spectral sensitivity is given as the photocurrent produced per watt of light incident on the active area at each wavelength (in A/W), and the datasheet also reports the \textbf{breakdown voltage}: APDs operate below it, and we need to know it to set the bias properly.

\subsection{Single-photon avalanche diodes}

PMTs and APDs detect light with a certain probability: the more photons arrive, the higher the probability of detecting them, and typically many photons are needed. What if we want to see a single photon? Then we need a \textbf{single-photon avalanche diode} (SPAD), a photodiode designed to detect individual photons. The difference with an APD lies in the operating voltage (Figure~\ref{fig:spad_operating_modes}): a photodiode works at low reverse voltage, an APD at higher reverse voltage in the so-called linear mode, and a SPAD beyond the breakdown voltage, in \textbf{Geiger mode}. The applied reverse bias creates such an intense electric field in the depletion region that a single absorbed photon can trigger a rapid, self-sustaining avalanche of charge carriers, producing a fast-rising, readily measurable current pulse.

\begin{figure}[h]
\centering
\includegraphics[page=80, width=0.6\linewidth, keepaspectratio]{\pdf}
\caption{Operating regions on the I--V curve: a photodiode works at low reverse bias, an APD at higher reverse bias (linear mode) and a SPAD beyond the breakdown voltage $V_{\text{BD}}$ (Geiger mode).}
\label{fig:spad_operating_modes}
\end{figure}

The operation of a SPAD has three steps. In the \textbf{avalanche}, the electric field accelerates a photogenerated carrier, which creates additional electron--hole pairs through impact ionisation and triggers a self-sustaining avalanche. In the \textbf{quenching and reset} step, the circuit lowers the bias below breakdown to stop the avalanche, and then restores the operating bias. The interval after a detection during which the SPAD cannot register another event is the \textbf{dead time}. As always there is a trade-off: we gain sensitivity to single photons, but we pay with a dead time due to quenching and reset. Note also that when several photons arrive together, a single SPAD produces at most one pulse: more photons increase the probability of a pulse, while its height remains approximately constant. A SPAD can only tell us whether a photon arrived or not.

Still, we are on nanosecond time scales, and SPADs are extremely fast: they can record individual photon arrivals with \textbf{subnanosecond timing precision}. In \textbf{fluorescence lifetime imaging}, this allows each detected fluorescence photon to be timed relative to the excitation pulse. The fluorescence signal of a fluorophore decays exponentially in time, and different fluorophores typically have different decay rates: measuring the fluorescence at different time points with a SPAD, we can tell which fluorophore we are seeing, even for lifetimes below the nanosecond.

\subsection{From one pixel to many: SPAD arrays and SiPMs}

All the solid-state detectors seen so far are made of a single pixel; even PMTs, with a large or small window, see only through one window. If we want spatial information, i.e.\ to know whether a photon arrived here or there, we need many pixels. SPADs can be arranged in an array, and how the array is read out determines what it measures (Figure~\ref{fig:spad_array_sipm} and Table~\ref{tab:spad_array_sipm}). In a \textbf{SPAD imaging array} each pixel is read out separately, so the detector records where a photon was detected, and combining the detections across pixels produces an image. In a \textbf{silicon photomultiplier} (SiPM), instead, many SPADs, called microcells, are connected in parallel to a common output. Each microcell can fire independently, and when several fire together their current pulses add, so the area of the output pulse indicates how many microcells fired. A SiPM cannot tell us where the photons arrived, but it can tell us whether one, two, three or four photons arrived, i.e.\ it measures the intensity as a number of photons.

\begin{figure}[!htbp]
\centering
\includegraphics[page=81, width=0.75\linewidth, keepaspectratio]{\pdf}
\caption{Readout of a SPAD array, where each pixel has its own output (left), and of a SiPM, where all microcells are connected in parallel to a shared output (right).}
\label{fig:spad_array_sipm}
\end{figure}

\begin{table}[!htbp]
\centering
\caption{SPAD arrays and silicon photomultipliers.}
\label{tab:spad_array_sipm}
\begin{tabular*}{\textwidth}{p{2.6cm}@{\extracolsep\fill}p{5.7cm}p{5.7cm}}
\toprule
 & SPAD array & SiPM \\
\midrule
Readout & Pixels are usually read individually, or in small groups & Many microcells are connected in parallel to a shared output \\
What it measures & Where photons arrive, and often when each arrives: useful for imaging and time-resolved measurements & The combined response of many microcells: useful for detecting weak light and estimating how many photons arrived together \\
Typical trade-off & Preserves pixel-level spatial information, but requires a more complex readout & Provides a strong, simple output, but usually loses information about which microcell detected each photon \\
\bottomrule
\end{tabular*}
\end{table}

Each fully charged microcell of a SiPM releases approximately the same charge when it fires. The SiPM produces a current pulse $I(t)$, and the readout electronics convert it into a voltage pulse $V(t)$, so if we connect the SiPM to an oscilloscope we see voltage (mV) versus time (ns) (Figure~\ref{fig:sipm_example}). Each trace is one event recorded under focused laser illumination, and the traces group into discrete levels, because statistically one, two, three\dots{} photons arrive. The area under each trace is proportional to the released charge, and the histogram of the pulse areas shows peaks labelled 1, 2, 3 and 4 p.e.\ (photoelectron equivalents), corresponding to one, two, three and four fired microcells; the horizontal distance between neighbouring peaks is the pulse area produced by a single microcell. The histogram is essentially the oscilloscope picture turned by ninety degrees: we can really count how many photons arrived.

\begin{figure}[h]
\centering
\includegraphics[page=82, width=0.85\linewidth, keepaspectratio]{\pdf}
\caption{Oscilloscope traces of an FBK SiPM (left) and histogram of the pulse areas (right), with peaks corresponding to one to four fired microcells (Yang et al., \emph{Sci.\ Rep.}\ 12, 15060, 2022).}
\label{fig:sipm_example}
\end{figure}

\section{Imaging Sensors: CCD, EMCCD and CMOS Cameras}
\label{sec:cameras}

Building a detector with the sensitivity of a SPAD array or of a SiPM is expensive and complicated, and very often we do not need to count photons one by one: we have plenty of photons, and we only care about knowing precisely where they arrived. An image shows where light falls and how bright it is at each location, and a camera samples the light with a grid of pixels: a $1024 \times 1024$ sensor, for example, measures the light at more than one million locations. To place so many pixels we have to change technology, and use either \textbf{CCD} (charge-coupled device) or \textbf{CMOS} (complementary metal--oxide--semiconductor) sensors. In both, during an exposure each pixel collects the charge generated by the absorbed photons, and the camera converts that charge into a digital brightness value for that position in the image. Some modern, advanced CMOS cameras can detect single photons, but when the goal is to measure \emph{when} individual photons arrive, SPAD arrays with timing electronics remain the preferred choice: they record both the detection position and the arrival time with subnanosecond precision, as needed, for example, in fluorescence lifetime imaging.

\subsection{CCD and EMCCD cameras}

In a CCD the charges generated during the \textbf{acquisition} (or exposure) \textbf{time}, from milliseconds to seconds, are accumulated next to the pixel, and each pixel behaves as a potential well. At the end of the acquisition, the charges are gradually moved along the chip, row by row, until they reach a readout and amplification stage, where the analog signal is converted into a digital one. One amplification is performed for all pixels, with a time-encoded strategy governed by a clocking circuit, so every pixel is read in the same way. A useful picture is that of buckets collecting rain, which are then carried one after the other on a conveyor belt to a single weighing scale (Figure~\ref{fig:ccd_readout}).

\begin{figure}[h]
\centering
\includegraphics[page=83, width=\linewidth, keepaspectratio]{\pdf}
\caption{In a CCD each pixel is a potential well arranged in rows and columns (left); the charges are shifted along the chip by clocked electrodes (centre) to a single readout stage, like buckets of rain carried by a conveyor belt to a weighing scale (right).}
\label{fig:ccd_readout}
\end{figure}

Conventional CCD cameras achieve a low read noise by reading out slowly: a faster readout requires a wider amplifier bandwidth, which increases the read noise and can obscure weak signals. \textbf{Electron-multiplying CCDs} (EMCCDs) solve this problem by amplifying the signal charge in a multiplication stage \emph{before} it reaches the readout amplifier. This reduces the relative impact of the read noise and allows weak signals to be detected even at high readout speeds. Practically all the CCDs we find in microscopy are EMCCDs.

\subsection{CMOS cameras}

A \textbf{CMOS camera} is a device in which each individual pixel possesses its own readout amplifier transistor, which performs a first stage of conversion, and the pixels are output with a separate analog-to-digital converter for each column. For this reason, in CMOS cameras we speak of \textbf{active pixels}. CMOS sensors are easier and cheaper to fabricate, and they have advantages in readout speed and digitisation; all smartphone cameras are CMOS sensors. Early CMOS sensors had a higher noise than CCD sensors, but recent CMOS sensors provide comparable, or even better, performance. Inside each pixel there are microlenses that collect as many photons as possible and, in the most advanced sensors, a \textbf{trench structure} between the photodiodes, which prevents a photoelectron generated in one pixel from leaking into, or exciting, a neighbouring pixel (crosstalk; Figure~\ref{fig:cmos_trench}).

\begin{figure}[!htbp]
\centering
\includegraphics[page=84, width=0.65\linewidth, keepaspectratio]{\pdf}
\caption{Cross-section of a qCMOS image sensor: microlenses focus the light on the photodiodes, and a trench structure suppresses the flow of photoelectrons to neighbouring pixels (Hamamatsu).}
\label{fig:cmos_trench}
\end{figure}

The architecture of a CMOS camera has a price: part of the signal chain is pixel-dependent and part is column-dependent, and we can see it simply by acquiring a dark image with the sensor (Figure~\ref{fig:cmos_dark_noise}). The image should be uniformly black, but it shows vertical stripes, the \emph{bar-code} effect, linked to the column-wise analog-to-digital conversion, and a \emph{salt-and-pepper} noise, due to the pixel-dependent behaviour of the first amplification stage.

\begin{figure}[!htbp]
\centering
\includegraphics[page=86, width=0.6\linewidth, keepaspectratio]{\pdf}
\caption{Dark image of a Zyla 4.2 CMOS camera, showing the salt-and-pepper noise and the bar-code effect.}
\label{fig:cmos_dark_noise}
\end{figure}

\subsection{EMCCD versus CMOS}

Up to about ten years ago, CCDs typically had a lower read noise than CMOS sensors and were the best choice for very weak signals. This is why, if we open an older spectroscopy instrument, we usually find an EMCCD or a CCD camera inside. Commercial EMCCDs, however, typically have far fewer pixels. Thanks to the way they are built, with each pixel read out (almost) independently, commercial CMOS cameras have been developed with an extremely high readout speed (up to $560\text{ MHz}$). Manufacturers can therefore put many more pixels on the sensor while keeping the same frame rate as a CCD, or reach a higher frame rate. In microscopy, at a fixed resolution (i.e.\ pixel size at the sample), more pixels translate into a more extended field of view (Figure~\ref{fig:fov_comparison}), and CMOS cameras offer a better signal-to-noise ratio at very high frame rates. Their disadvantages are a pixel-dependent noise, a pixel-dependent gain and the presence of \textbf{hot pixels}. These can create artefacts and, because of the readout noise, reduce the localisation precision wherever pixels with high noise are involved, which is a problem, for example, when localising single molecules.

\begin{figure}[!htbp]
\centering
\includegraphics[page=85, width=0.75\linewidth, keepaspectratio]{\pdf}
\caption{Field of view of an EMCCD camera ($1024 \times 1024$), of a second-generation sCMOS camera ($2048 \times 2048$) and of the ORCA-Quest 2 ($4096 \times 2304$), at the same pixel size.}
\label{fig:fov_comparison}
\end{figure}

The debate between the two technologies was particularly lively when super-resolution microscopy was emerging. A comparison published about ten years ago (Figure~\ref{fig:snr_emccd_cmos}) showed that sCMOS read noise is very low compared to that of CCDs, but EMCCD technology was able to effectively overcome the read noise, rendering EMCCDs sensitive to single photons: for very few photons (below about four photoelectrons) the EMCCD performed better, above that the CMOS. A few years later, comparisons at the same speed, i.e.\ at the same readout rate per pixel, showed that CMOS cameras were already performing better than CCDs in terms of signal-to-noise ratio.

\begin{figure}[h]
\centering
\includegraphics[page=87, width=0.75\linewidth, keepaspectratio]{\pdf}
\caption{Simplified image SNR as a function of the signal for two generations of sCMOS cameras (Flash 2.8 and Flash 4.0), an EMCCD (iXon 897, gain 120) and a CCD (HQ2) (Long et al., \emph{Optics Express} 20, 17759, 2012).}
\label{fig:snr_emccd_cmos}
\end{figure}

CMOS technology has since advanced impressively fast. The best CMOS cameras today reach a read noise below one photoelectron, which makes it possible to distinguish one, two, three photoelectrons, almost as with a SiPM (Figure~\ref{fig:cmos_single_photon}). With a modern CMOS camera single molecules appear as clearer spots than with an EMCCD, both for the better signal-to-noise ratio and for the larger active area and resolution. This is why CMOS cameras are now the most widely used, and it makes no sense anymore to choose an EMCCD, not even for spectroscopy. Their remaining problem is the pixel-dependent noise and gain, together with the hot pixels, and the solution is to \textbf{characterise the camera}.

\begin{figure}[!htbp]
\centering
\includegraphics[page=88, width=0.6\linewidth, keepaspectratio]{\pdf}
\caption{Probability of detecting a given number of photoelectrons for different values of the readout noise: with a read noise of $0.3$ electrons the individual photoelectron levels become distinguishable.}
\label{fig:cmos_single_photon}
\end{figure}

\section{Characterising a CMOS Camera}
\label{sec:cmos_characterization}

In Section~\ref{sec:sensor_characteristics} we postponed the discussion of noise. We can now take it up again on a concrete example, which is also a good way to review what we learnt about variances. Characterising a CMOS camera means finding, for each pixel, its \textbf{gain} $g$ and its \textbf{read noise} $\sigma_R$, and locating the hot pixels.

The signal of each pixel is affected by three main noise sources. The \textbf{shot noise} $\sigma_S$ is linked to the fact that photons arrive on the detector following a Poisson statistic (Figure~\ref{fig:poisson_gauss}). The \textbf{read noise} $\sigma_R$, also called the ``offset variance'', follows a Gaussian statistic. The \textbf{dark noise} $\sigma_D$, the dark-current shot noise, is a random fluctuation caused by thermally generated electrons. To a first approximation, especially when the exposure time is not very long, as in microscopy, where it is typically of the order of a millisecond or below, the dark noise can be neglected. The electronic \textbf{offset} (or bias), instead, is not a noise but a systematic contribution to the measured signal.

Since the noise sources are independent, the total variance is the variance of a sum of independent random variables, which equals the sum of their variances (Equation~\eqref{eq:variance_linear_combination}):
\begin{equation}
\sigma_{\text{tot}}^2 = \text{Var}\left(\sum_{i=1}^{N}X_i\right) = \sum_{i=1}^{N}\text{Var}(X_i),
\end{equation}
so that the total noise of a pixel is
\begin{equation}
\boxed{\sigma_{\text{tot}} = \sqrt{\sigma_S^2 + \sigma_R^2}}
\label{eq:cmos_total_noise}
\end{equation}

\subsection{Offset and read noise}

What can we actually measure with a camera? Two things: dark images, collected when no photons arrive, and images at different light intensities. From the dark images we obtain the \textbf{offset} of each pixel: we put the camera in the dark, collect many frames, compute the average offset of each pixel, and subtract it from every future image. The offset is just a systematic contribution and is removed this way. The noise, however, cannot be cancelled, and we have to estimate it: the variance of the offset is exactly the read noise. From the images at different intensities, which require a fairly uniform source whose intensity can be tuned, we obtain the variance of the signal, $\sigma_{\text{tot}}$, and the gain.

If $x_i$ is the value of a pixel in frame $i$, acquired in the dark, the offset mean value of that pixel and the offset variance are
\begin{equation}
\mu = \frac{1}{N}\sum_{i=1}^{N}x_i, \qquad \sigma^2 = \sigma_R^2 = \frac{1}{N}\sum_{i=1}^{N}(x_i - \mu)^2.
\label{eq:offset_mean_variance}
\end{equation}
These are population quantities, which we would obtain with infinitely many frames; we cannot stay in front of the camera for the age of the universe, so we only have a sample. As we demonstrated in Section~\ref{sec:n_minus_one}, the sample variance $\sigma^2_{\text{sample}}$ computed with $N$ in the denominator is biased, $\mathbb{E}[\sigma^2_{\text{sample}}] = \frac{N - 1}{N}\sigma^2$, and we must use the unbiased sample variance
\begin{equation}
\boxed{s^2 = \frac{N}{N - 1}\,\sigma^2_{\text{sample}}}
\label{eq:offset_unbiased_variance}
\end{equation}
i.e.\ divide by $N - 1$ instead of $N$. The larger the number of frames we average, the better: if we compute the offset variance on an increasing number of frames, it eventually reaches a plateau, because we are approaching the population value (Figure~\ref{fig:offset_variance_frames}).

\begin{figure}[!htbp]
\centering
\includegraphics[page=89, width=0.6\linewidth, keepaspectratio]{\pdf}
\caption{Offset variance as a function of the number of averaged frames: the estimate stabilises as the number of frames grows.}
\label{fig:offset_variance_frames}
\end{figure}

\subsection{Gain}

The output signal $s$ of a pixel, the number we read in analog-to-digital units (ADU) when we switch on the camera, is linked to the number of photoelectrons $n_e$ through the gain,
\begin{equation}
\boxed{s = g\,n_e, \qquad [g] = \frac{\text{ADU}}{e^-}}
\label{eq:cmos_gain}
\end{equation}
where, in this whole discussion, we neglect the quantum efficiency (i.e.\ we set it to one). The photoelectrons inherit the shot noise of the photon flux, and since the shot noise is Poissonian, and the mean and the variance of a Poisson distribution are equal, $\sigma_e^2 = n_e$. The shot noise in the output signal is $\sigma_S = g\,\sigma_e$, so the total noise of Equation~\eqref{eq:cmos_total_noise} becomes
\begin{equation}
\sigma_{\text{tot}}^2 = \sigma_S^2 + \sigma_R^2 = (g\,\sigma_e)^2 + \sigma_R^2 = g^2n_e + \sigma_R^2.
\end{equation}
Substituting $n_e = s/g$ we obtain a relation that contains only measurable quantities and the gain:
\begin{equation}
\boxed{\sigma_{\text{tot}}^2 - \sigma_R^2 = g\,s}
\label{eq:gain_relation}
\end{equation}
Here $s$ is the average signal measured in ADU minus the offset. Measuring $\sigma_{\text{tot}}$, $\sigma_R$ and $s$ for each pixel at $K$ different light intensity levels, the gain of each pixel is the value that minimises the squared residuals of Equation~\eqref{eq:gain_relation}:
\begin{equation}
g = \underset{g}{\arg\min}\sum_{k=1}^{K}\left[\left(\sigma_{\text{tot},k}^2 - \sigma_{R,k}^2\right) - s_kg\right]^2.
\label{eq:gain_least_squares}
\end{equation}
This is a least squares minimisation. Thanks to the least squares solution theorem, using the pseudo-inverse matrix, it can be rewritten as
\begin{equation}
\boxed{g = \left(S^TS\right)^{-1}S^TV}, \qquad S = \begin{pmatrix} s_1 \\ \vdots \\ s_K \end{pmatrix}, \qquad V = \begin{pmatrix} \sigma_{\text{tot},1}^2 - \sigma_{R,1}^2 \\ \vdots \\ \sigma_{\text{tot},K}^2 - \sigma_{R,K}^2 \end{pmatrix}.
\label{eq:gain_pseudoinverse}
\end{equation}
Since every image is a matrix, it is convenient to work with matrices and compute the gain of all pixels simultaneously; with MATLAB or Python this is very fast, once the stack of dark images and the stacks of images at different intensities have been collected. We do not need to remember the matrix formulation, but it is not really difficult. An equivalent strategy is to plot, for every pixel, $\sigma_{\text{tot}}^2 - \sigma_R^2$ against the signal $s$: the gain is simply the slope of this line, obtained with a fit for every single pixel.

\subsection{An example: the Zyla 4.2 camera}

Figure~\ref{fig:read_noise_histogram} shows the result of this characterisation for a Zyla 4.2 (Andor) CMOS camera, about ten years ago: the histogram of the number of pixels as a function of the offset variance. The distribution is not Gaussian, with a long tail toward high variances. From its most probable value, about $8\text{ ADU}^2$, and the gain, we obtain the read noise in electrons, $\sqrt{8}/2.2 \approx 1.3\,e^-$, comparable with the $1.1\,e^-$ declared in the camera specifications. At that time this was a very good camera; nowadays the best cameras have, on average, a read noise below one electron.

\begin{figure}[!htbp]
\centering
\includegraphics[page=90, width=0.75\linewidth, keepaspectratio]{\pdf}
\caption{Number of pixels as a function of the offset variance (exposure time $10\text{ ms}$) for a Zyla 4.2 camera. The read noise in electrons is obtained by dividing the square root of the offset variance by the gain.}
\label{fig:read_noise_histogram}
\end{figure}

The gain can be mapped pixel by pixel, since the camera is a matrix (Figure~\ref{fig:gain_map}), and its histogram (Figure~\ref{fig:gain_histogram}) tells us that the gain of this camera is about $2.2\text{ ADU}/e^-$ (mean $2.17$, standard deviation $0.33\text{ ADU}/e^-$). The measured characteristics of the camera, at $50\text{ ms}$ integration time and $560\text{ MHz}$ readout clock, are an average offset of about $100\text{ ADU}$, an average offset variance of about $17\text{ ADU}^2$, an average gain of about $2.2\text{ ADU}/e^-$ and a gain variance of about $0.3\text{ ADU}/e^-$.

\begin{figure}[!htbp]
\centering
\includegraphics[page=91, width=0.7\linewidth, keepaspectratio]{\pdf}
\caption{Map of the gain of each pixel of the Zyla 4.2 camera.}
\label{fig:gain_map}
\end{figure}

\subsection{Bit depth, full-well capacity and dynamic range}

The specifications of a CMOS camera always report its \textbf{bit depth} $n$, the number of digital values available to represent the signal: an $n$-bit analog-to-digital converter has $2^n$ digital levels, but the maximum digital value is $2^n - 1$, since counting starts at zero. Typical values are 8, 12 and 16 bits, the latter being the highest found in commercial cameras, with $2^{16} = 65\,536$ levels. In some cameras the bit depth can be changed, and it should be chosen properly. We open the camera, look at the sample in the standard conditions of our acquisition, and check the counts at the minimum and at the maximum signal. If the signal varies, say, between $50$ and $300\text{ ADU}$, it makes no sense to keep the bit depth at 16 bits: choosing 8-bit instead of 12- or 16-bit acquisition can increase the frame rate (frames per second) of a CMOS camera, and give us more freedom from that point of view.

The bit depth and the \textbf{full-well capacity} (FWC), the maximum charge that can be stored in a pixel, are linked. Assuming that the full ADC range maps to the full-well capacity, with zero electronic offset,
\begin{equation}
\boxed{\text{FWC} \approx \frac{2^n - 1}{g}}
\label{eq:full_well_capacity}
\end{equation}
with the FWC in electrons and the conversion gain $g$ in $\text{ADU}/e^-$. Finally, the \textbf{dynamic range} is the ratio between the full-well capacity and the read noise:
\begin{equation}
\boxed{\text{DR} = \frac{\text{FWC}}{\sigma_{\text{read}}}}
\label{eq:camera_dynamic_range}
\end{equation}
For our camera, with a 16-bit ADC, a mean conversion gain of $2.17\text{ ADU}/e^-$ and a read noise of about $1\,e^-$ RMS, the estimated full-well capacity is about $30\,200\,e^-$, and the specifications declare a dynamic range of $33\,000{:}1$, which corresponds to the maximum value divided by the minimum gain, $65\,536/2 \approx 33\,000$ (Figure~\ref{fig:gain_histogram}).

\clearpage
\begin{figure}[h]
\centering
\includegraphics[page=92, width=\linewidth, keepaspectratio]{\pdf}
\caption{Histogram of the gain of the Zyla 4.2 camera, together with the definitions of bit depth, full-well capacity and dynamic range and the corresponding camera specifications.}
\label{fig:gain_histogram}
\end{figure}

\subsection{Hot pixels and the use of the characterisation}

Looking at the gain map, we immediately notice that the image is mostly green, i.e.\ around $2$, but there are some very dark blue spots, with a gain close to zero, which correspond to a small separate group in the gain histogram (Figure~\ref{fig:hot_pixels}). In Chapter~\ref{ch:errors} we saw that a value more than three standard deviations from the mean deserves to be investigated (Equation~\eqref{eq:three_sigma_rule}). Here we are at probably ten standard deviations, and there are too many of these values compared with what the statistics would predict: something does not belong to what we are trying to describe. Indeed, these pixels coincide exactly with the defective pixels declared by the manufacturer. They are \textbf{hot pixels}: they are always switched on, with a very high current, so they look bright, but in a fake way, not because photons arrived. What we can do is to remove their information, which has no meaning, and replace it with the mean value of the pixels around each hot pixel.

The parameters obtained from the characterisation can be used, for example, to be really precise in the localisation of single molecules. The localisation depends on the distribution of the intensity recorded by the pixels, and if this distribution is distorted by pixel-to-pixel differences, the localisation is not precise. Huang et al.\ (\emph{Nature Methods} 10, 653) developed a CMOS-specific algorithm that accounts for the pixel-dependent readout noise and gain. It is based on a maximum likelihood estimator (MLE), a kind of evolution of least squares minimisation that accounts for the noise, whose likelihood function is the convolution of a Poisson distribution, representing the shot noise, with a Gaussian distribution, representing the read noise. Plugging the gain and the noise of each pixel into the algorithm improves the localisation of every single molecule.

\begin{figure}[h]
\centering
\includegraphics[page=93, width=0.6\linewidth, keepaspectratio]{\pdf}
\caption{Number of pixels as a function of the gain: the hot pixels form a small group far from the main distribution.}
\label{fig:hot_pixels}
\end{figure}

\section{Camera Specifications in Practice}
\label{sec:camera_specs}

\subsection{Quantum efficiency, sensitivity, threshold and saturation}

Besides gain and noise, another key characteristic of a camera is its \textbf{quantum efficiency} (Equation~\eqref{eq:quantum_efficiency}), which tells us how well the sensor converts light of different wavelengths into signal. The spectral response can be quite different from one camera to another, so it is important to check where our fluorophore emits: if it emits far in the red, where a camera has a low quantum efficiency, we may be losing most of our photons and we should probably choose another camera. The maximum quantum efficiency is always around 80--85\%, so some photons are always lost, whatever the wavelength.

The general characteristics we defined for sensors in Section~\ref{sec:sensor_characteristics} (sensitivity, resolution, threshold, saturation, dead band) can be identified for cameras too, and many of them can be computed from the specifications given by the manufacturer. Consider a Basler a2A5328-35cm CMOS camera at $541\text{ nm}$. A CMOS pixel collects charge during an exposure, and in its linear range the mean digital output increases in proportion to the number of incident photons $N_\gamma$: the slope of this response curve is the sensitivity (Figure~\ref{fig:basler_sensitivity}). The chain goes from photons to electrons to digital counts. The quantum efficiency is $\eta = 67.3\%$, so $N_e = \eta N_\gamma$, and the camera gain is $G = 0.413$ digital counts per electron. After subtracting the dark signal, the mean output is $\text{ADU} = G\eta N_\gamma$, and the sensitivity is therefore
\begin{equation}
\boxed{S = \frac{\Delta\text{ADU}}{\Delta N_\gamma} = G\eta} = 0.413 \times 0.673 \approx 0.28\text{ counts per photon}.
\label{eq:camera_sensitivity}
\end{equation}
On average, 100 additional photons increase the output by about 28 counts. If the quantum efficiency were one, as we assumed in the characterisation, the sensitivity would coincide with the gain.

\begin{figure}[!htbp]
\centering
\includegraphics[page=94, width=0.5\linewidth, keepaspectratio]{\pdf}
\caption{Response of a Basler a2A5328-35cm camera at $541\text{ nm}$: in the linear range, the mean output (in digital numbers) grows with the mean number of photons per pixel, with slope equal to the sensitivity, and flattens at saturation.}
\label{fig:basler_sensitivity}
\end{figure}

What input change corresponds to one digital count? The smallest output increment is one ADU (also called digital count, or digital number), so using the resolution formula of Equation~\eqref{eq:resolution_lsb_formula} with $\Delta y_{\text{LSB}} = 1\text{ ADU}$,
\begin{equation}
\Delta N_\gamma = \frac{\Delta y_{\text{LSB}}}{S} = \frac{1}{0.28} \approx 3.6\text{ incident photons per pixel}.
\end{equation}
This is the \textbf{intensity resolution}: on average, we can distinguish two signals differing by about $3.6$ photons per pixel, although noise limits how reliably such a small change can be distinguished in a single exposure.

The \textbf{detection threshold} is reached when the mean signal due to light equals the typical fluctuation of the pixel output, i.e.\ when the signal-to-noise ratio is $\text{SNR} = 1$. For this camera the threshold corresponds to an average of $2.7$ collected electrons per pixel, and accounting for the quantum efficiency the corresponding light level is
\begin{equation}
N_{\gamma,\text{threshold}} = \frac{2.7\text{ electrons}}{0.67\text{ electrons/photon}} \approx 4.1\text{ photons}.
\end{equation}
