When we buy a camera we should therefore be aware of the experiment we have to do, check the quantum efficiency at the wavelength of our fluorophore, and ask ourselves how many photons we expect. For most biological applications four photons are not a problem: we have many more. It may become an issue in the quantum world, for instance in quantum biology, where we usually want to see single photons.

What is more typical in our experiments is the opposite problem, \textbf{saturation}: we increase the laser power, but the signal of the camera does not go up, as it should. In that condition the output no longer tells us what is happening on the sensor, and we have to check that we are not saturating the camera. The same table of specifications gives a saturation capacity of $9\,661$ collected electrons per pixel, and applying the same conversion we find the incident light level at saturation:
\begin{equation}
N_{\gamma,\text{sat}} = \frac{9\,661\text{ electrons}}{0.67\text{ electrons/photon}} \approx 14\,350\text{ photons per exposure}.
\end{equation}

\subsection{Dead time and readout speed}

Cameras are fast, but not infinitely fast: they also have a \textbf{dead time}, during which they reset and read the information and cannot see anything. A fast signal falling in this interval is lost. In the Hamamatsu ORCA-Flash4.0 V3, for example, at 100 frames per second with a Camera Link interface, each row starts a new exposure every $10\text{ ms}$: with an $8\text{ ms}$ exposure, the row collects light for $8\text{ ms}$ and is inactive for the remaining $2\text{ ms}$, and a flash falling entirely in that gap is missed by that row. We therefore need to know the readout time and the maximum speed of the camera.

Table~\ref{tab:orca_quest_specs} shows some of the features we find when we open the specifications of a camera, and what they mean for imaging. The \textbf{pixel size} is fundamental, because it is related to the resolution we want at the sample, and the magnification of the objective must be chosen according to the pixel size of the camera. The readout mode is another important choice. We can read every pixel very fast, at the price of a higher read noise, or more slowly, with a lower read noise. There is always a trade-off, and with really weak signals we have to accept a slower readout.

\begin{table}[h]
\centering
\caption{Some specifications of the Hamamatsu ORCA-Quest CMOS camera and their meaning for imaging.}
\label{tab:orca_quest_specs}
\begin{tabular*}{\textwidth}{p{2.8cm}@{\extracolsep\fill}p{4.6cm}p{6.4cm}}
\toprule
Feature & Specification & What it means for imaging \\
\midrule
Pixel array and size & $4096 \times 2304$ pixels (9.4\,MP), each $4.6\,\mu\text{m}$ square & At a given magnification, pixel size determines specimen sampling, and array size determines the field of view \\
Quantum efficiency & 85\% at $460\text{ nm}$ (peak) & At this wavelength, 100 incident photons produce about 85 collected electrons on average \\
Standard scan (fast readout) & $0.43\,e^-$ RMS read noise at 120 full frames/s & A new frame every $8.3\text{ ms}$ makes this mode useful for following rapid changes \\
Ultra-quiet scan (slow readout) & $0.27\,e^-$ RMS read noise at 5 full frames/s & Slower readout makes adjacent photoelectron levels easier to distinguish in faint signals \\
Full-well capacity & $7\,000\,e^-$ per pixel & Sets the upper limit on the charge a pixel can collect before saturation \\
Dark current & $0.016\,e^-/\text{pixel/s}$ at $-20\;^\circ\text{C}$ & During a $10\text{ s}$ exposure, a pixel accumulates only $0.16$ electrons on average from dark current \\
\bottomrule
\end{tabular*}
\end{table}

\subsection{Colour cameras}

So far we have treated cameras as if they collected a single colour. Our smartphone, however, takes colour pictures: how is colour measured? In a rather unsophisticated way, because so far no better way has been found. The cheapest method is to place optical filters on the pixels, with a \textbf{colour filter array} (CFA); the typical arrangement is the \textbf{Bayer filter} (Figure~\ref{fig:bayer_filter}). In this way each pixel detects only one wavelength band (red, green or blue), and the full colour image is reconstructed in post-processing. The trade-off is a lower spatial resolution, and also a loss of photons, since every filter discards part of the light.

\begin{figure}[!htbp]
    \centering
    \begin{subfigure}[b]{0.32\linewidth}
        \centering
        \includegraphics[page=95, width=\linewidth, keepaspectratio]{\pdf}
        \caption{Bayer filter.}
        \label{fig:bayer_pattern}
    \end{subfigure}
    \hfill
    \begin{subfigure}[b]{0.62\linewidth}
        \centering
        \includegraphics[page=96, width=\linewidth, keepaspectratio]{\pdf}
        \caption{Colour filters on a CMOS sensor.}
        \label{fig:bayer_sensor}
    \end{subfigure}
    \caption{A Bayer colour filter array: each pixel receives only red, green or blue light (A); the filters sit between the microlens and the photodiode of each active pixel of a CMOS sensor (B).}
    \label{fig:bayer_filter}
\end{figure}

In microscopy colour cameras are never used, even for multichannel (multicolour) imaging. We keep using monochrome cameras and select the colours with the microscope. One possibility is to alternate the collection of the different colours in time, placing the appropriate filters in the optical path. Another is to collect the channels simultaneously with two cameras and a splitter: for green and red, for example, a mirror that reflects red and transmits green sends the red light to one camera and the green light to the other. Finally, since different colours in biology usually mean different labelled proteins, which are often located in different positions, we can use a filter that passes both signals, image the two channels simultaneously and distinguish them because we know what we are labelling.

\subsection{Cameras for spectroscopy}

A sensor made of many pixels is also useful for spectroscopy, because pixelated detectors can capture a spectrum of light, detecting multiple wavelengths at once. The different wavelengths of an optical stimulus are usually separated with a diffraction grating (Section~\ref{sec:light_review}) and then focused on an image sensor, so that every pixel receives a different set of wavelengths and the ``image'' encodes a spectrum: entering with white light, we find the blue in a certain range of pixels, the green in another and the red in another (Figure~\ref{fig:spectrometer}). The spectral resolution (in nm) depends on the grating periodicity, on the length of the spectrometer and on the pixel size of the camera.

\begin{figure}[!htbp]
\centering
\includegraphics[page=97, width=0.5\linewidth, keepaspectratio]{\pdf}
\caption{Scheme of a spectrometer: the light entering through a slit is collimated, separated by a grating and focused on an image sensor, where each pixel receives a different wavelength.}
\label{fig:spectrometer}
\end{figure}

\section{Satellite-Based Positioning Systems}
\label{sec:gnss}

How do we measure position, motion and occupancy? Different technologies infer them from different physical signals: radio-signal timing in satellite-based positioning systems, optical distance measurement in LiDAR, acoustic echoes in echolocation and ultrasound, and a variety of signals in occupancy and motion sensors. We give an overview of what exists, starting from satellite positioning.

\subsection{GPS and the GNSS constellations}

All of us use the GPS without any issues. The \textbf{Global Positioning System} (GPS) determines the three-dimensional position of a receiver on Earth using radio signals transmitted by satellites (at least four). Each satellite transmits its position and precise timing information, and the receiver measures the signal travel time to estimate its distance from each satellite. We commonly call all these systems GPS, but the technically correct name is \textbf{GNSS} (Global Navigation Satellite Systems), the general term for satellite-based systems that provide worldwide positioning, navigation and timing (PNT) services. Each GNSS consists of a constellation of satellites orbiting Earth on different orbital planes, designed so that multiple satellites are simultaneously visible from almost any location. The four main constellations, controlled by different organisations, are summarised in Table~\ref{tab:gnss_constellations}.

\begin{table}[!htbp]
\centering
\caption{The main GNSS constellations.}
\label{tab:gnss_constellations}
\begin{tabular*}{\textwidth}{p{2.6cm}@{\extracolsep\fill}p{3.6cm}p{4.4cm}p{3cm}}
\toprule
System & Origin & Orbital planes and satellites & Altitude \\
\midrule
GPS (NAVSTAR), United States & Originally developed for military applications & 6 planes; at least 24 satellites & $\sim 20\,200\text{ km}$ \\
GLONASS, Russia & Originally developed by the Soviet Union & 3 planes; 24 satellites & $\sim 19\,100\text{ km}$ \\
Galileo, European Union & Developed primarily for civilian applications & 3 planes; 24 operational satellites $+$ 6 spares & $\sim 23\,222\text{ km}$ \\
BeiDou (BDS), China & Civilian and military applications & 3 planes for the MEO; 30 satellites: 24 MEO, 3 IGSO, 3 GEO & $\sim 21\,528\text{ km}$ (MEO), $\sim 35\,786\text{ km}$ (IGSO/GEO) \\
\bottomrule
\end{tabular*}
\end{table}

The very first system was the GPS, developed, like many advanced technologies, for military purposes. Europe arrived quite late: Galileo has been operating only for a few years. The Chinese system is the most articulated, combining satellites on orbits at different altitudes.

A GNSS consists of three main segments. The \textbf{space segment} is the constellation of satellites orbiting Earth, which continuously transmit radio signals containing precise timing information and orbital data. The \textbf{control segment} is a network of ground stations that monitor and manage the constellation: they track the satellite orbits, monitor the onboard clocks, and upload updated navigation data, the \textbf{ephemerides} (an ephemeris is a set of orbital data that allows a satellite's position to be calculated at a given time) and clock corrections. Finally, the \textbf{user segment} is made of us, the GNSS receivers, which collect and process the satellite signals to determine position, velocity and time.

\subsection{Operating principle and sources of error}

In a simplified explanation (a whole course would be needed to explain how geolocalisation really works), positioning relies on three steps. The first is \textbf{precise timing}: GNSS satellites carry highly stable atomic clocks, continuously monitored and corrected by the ground stations, and each satellite broadcasts radio-frequency signals containing the precise transmission time and its orbital data. The second is \textbf{distance estimation}: the receiver estimates the signal travel time $\Delta t$ from each satellite, and since the signals are electromagnetic waves travelling at the speed of light, the corresponding apparent distance, called the \textbf{pseudorange}, is
\begin{equation}
\boxed{\rho = c\,\Delta t = c\,(t_R - t_T)}
\label{eq:pseudorange}
\end{equation}
where $t_R$ is the reception time (receiver clock) and $t_T$ the transmission time (satellite clock). One pseudorange tells us how far we are from one satellite, but it is not enough. The third step is \textbf{position determination}: the receiver combines the pseudoranges from multiple satellites (\textbf{trilateration}), and the position is calculated from the intersection of the spherical wavefronts (Figure~\ref{fig:gnss_trilateration}). The illustration with three intersecting spheres represents idealised trilateration; in practice the receiver must estimate four unknowns, the three spatial coordinates $(x, y, z)$ and the receiver clock offset $\Delta t_{\text{clock}}$, so it needs at least four equations, i.e.\ at least four satellites.

\begin{figure}[!htbp]
\centering
\includegraphics[page=98, width=0.45\linewidth, keepaspectratio]{\pdf}
\caption{Idealised trilateration: the receiver lies at the intersection of the spheres centred on the satellites, with radii equal to the measured distances. A fourth satellite is needed in practice to estimate the receiver clock offset.}
\label{fig:gnss_trilateration}
\end{figure}

The position is not measured directly: it is reconstructed from signal travel-time measurements through the model of Equation~\eqref{eq:pseudorange}, so GNSS is an example of indirect measurement, and errors propagate into the result. Timing errors are due to clock errors, since the receiver and satellite clocks are not perfectly synchronised, and the measured time difference does not correspond exactly to the actual propagation time. Atmospheric delays, which change from place to place, and interference with the many other signals continuously sent by all these satellite systems also affect the reconstructed position, so the apparent distance is not identical to the geometric distance. Timing has to be extremely precise: GNSS radio signals travel at about $3 \times 10^8\text{ m/s}$, i.e.\ $30\text{ cm/ns}$, so
\begin{equation}
1\text{ ns of timing error} \approx 0.30\text{ m},
\end{equation}
and a timing error of only about $3\text{ ns}$ already gives an error of one metre. Relying on quartz oscillators, consumer-grade GPS receivers such as smartphones typically achieve positioning accuracies of 5--10\,m under favourable conditions.

To mitigate these errors and be even more precise, the \textbf{differential GPS} (DGPS) uses a reference station at a precisely known location (Figure~\ref{fig:dgps}). The reference station receives the same satellite signals as the nearby GPS receivers; by comparing its measured position with its known position, it estimates the positioning errors, which are used to generate corrections for nearby receivers. Differential measurements reduce the errors shared by nearby receivers, and DGPS can achieve sub-metre accuracy under suitable conditions.

\begin{figure}[!htbp]
\centering
\includegraphics[page=99, width=0.65\linewidth, keepaspectratio]{\pdf}
\caption{Differential GPS: a reference station at a known position compares its measured position with the true one, and the resulting corrections are applied to nearby receivers.}
\label{fig:dgps}
\end{figure}

Beyond navigation, GPS can help us to study animal behaviour. Modern receivers are compact and light enough to be attached to animals, allowing their movements to be tracked over time, and combined with wireless communication technologies the tracking devices can also transmit the location data remotely. GPS tracking is widely used to investigate animal migration routes and movement patterns, foraging strategies and habitat use, interactions and social behaviour, human--wildlife interactions and conflicts, and for wildlife conservation and management.

\section{Sensing Distance: LiDAR, Echolocation and Ultrasound}
\label{sec:distance}

Distance measurements are used in engineering, environmental monitoring and life sciences. Different technologies use the propagation of electromagnetic or acoustic waves to obtain spatial information over distances ranging from centimetres to kilometres. Two common approaches are \textbf{optical ranging} (LiDAR), which uses light, and \textbf{acoustic ranging} (sonar and ultrasound), which uses sound waves, mechanical rather than electromagnetic waves. In both cases the distance is estimated from the propagation time of a signal, and both rely on \textbf{active sensing}: unlike GNSS, the same system generates the signal, emits it, and analyses its interaction with the environment.

\subsection{LiDAR}

\textbf{LiDAR} (Light Detection and Ranging) is an active optical sensing technique that uses laser light to measure distances to surrounding objects. It is based on the \textbf{time-of-flight} (ToF) principle: a short laser pulse is emitted toward a target, part of the light is reflected or scattered back to the detector, and the distance is calculated from the measured round-trip travel time $\Delta t$:
\begin{equation}
\boxed{D = \frac{c\,\Delta t}{2}}
\label{eq:lidar_distance}
\end{equation}
where $c$ is the speed of light. LiDAR often uses near-infrared lasers (e.g.\ $905$ or $1550\text{ nm}$), and the reflected light is detected with the very fast photodetectors we met in Section~\ref{sec:photodiodes}, APDs or, for very weak signals, SPADs, both with response times around or below one nanosecond. The distance resolution depends on the pulse duration and on the timing resolution, while the spatial sampling density depends on the scanning and pulse repetition rates (Figure~\ref{fig:lidar_tof}).

\clearpage
\begin{figure}[h]
\centering
\includegraphics[page=100, width=0.25\linewidth, keepaspectratio]{\pdf}
\caption{Time-of-flight principle of a scanning LiDAR: the laser pulse is sent toward the targets, and the distance is computed from the round-trip time of the reflected light.}
\label{fig:lidar_tof}
\end{figure}

The difference with GNSS is immediately clear. In GNSS the signal travels one way, from the satellite to the receiver, and $D = c\Delta t$; in LiDAR the signal makes a round trip, and $D = c\Delta t/2$.

LiDAR is widely used for atmospheric and environmental monitoring. In atmospheric applications the laser light is scattered by molecules, aerosols and cloud particles, so the scattered light is informative in itself: the analysis of the backscattered signal provides information on their spatial distribution and altitude, and allows aerosols, clouds and atmospheric pollution to be monitored. In oceanographic applications, underwater LiDAR uses wavelengths that can penetrate water (typically green light), for shallow-water bathymetry and marine habitat mapping; scattering and absorption attenuate the signal and limit the measurement range.

The context in which we hear about LiDAR most often is \textbf{autonomous driving}. Scanning laser beams, often many of them to improve the spatial resolution, measure the distances to the surrounding objects, and the multiple measurements are combined into a three-dimensional point cloud of the environment (Figure~\ref{fig:lidar_car}). LiDAR complements cameras and radar for obstacle detection, localisation and navigation. Why, then, is reliable autonomous driving still challenging, if LiDAR can be so fast and precise? Its limitations come from the variability of the outside world: adverse weather conditions (fog, rain, snow), which can create very strong artefacts; reflective or poorly reflecting surfaces, for which the system is blind; occlusions and a limited field of view; and the real-time processing and interpretation of complex environments, where very close targets may appear in very short times. The technology keeps improving, but the reliance on reflection is intrinsic to the physics of the method. On top of the technical limitations there are also ethical questions: would we accept that a car decides for us, for example, which way to steer when every choice harms someone?

\begin{figure}[h]
\centering
\includegraphics[page=101, width=0.6\linewidth, keepaspectratio]{\pdf}
\caption{A vehicle-mounted LiDAR scans the environment with several laser beams to build a three-dimensional map of the surroundings.}
\label{fig:lidar_car}
\end{figure}

As for any sensor, we can define the performance of a LiDAR. Its \textbf{resolution} includes a spatial (angular) resolution in the $x$ and $y$ directions, which determines how closely spaced two objects can be while remaining distinguishable; a range resolution along the propagation direction $z$, which depends on the laser pulse duration and on the temporal response of the detector; and a temporal resolution, which determines how frequently the environment is sampled or updated. Its \textbf{sensitivity} depends on the target reflectivity, distance and speed and on the signal-to-noise ratio: very close or very fast objects can be hard to detect, as can weak or partially obstructed ones. Its \textbf{reliability} depends on external conditions: fog, rain and snow attenuate and scatter the laser light, and the surface reflectivity and geometry affect the returned signal. Finally, the laser power cannot be increased at will, because eye-safety requirements limit the permissible emission: we cannot shine a laser into people's eyes.

To get a feeling for the time scales, how long does it take for a laser pulse to travel to an obstacle $1\text{ m}$ away and return (Figure~\ref{fig:lidar_one_metre})? From Equation~\eqref{eq:lidar_distance},
\begin{equation}
\Delta t = \frac{2D}{c} = \frac{2 \times 1\text{ m}}{3 \times 10^8\text{ m/s}} \approx 6.7\text{ ns},
\end{equation}
so the system must be able to resolve times of a few nanoseconds. Exercise~\ref{ex:lidar_uncertainty} shows how a timing uncertainty translates into a distance uncertainty.

\begin{figure}[!htbp]
\centering
\includegraphics[page=102, width=0.4\linewidth, keepaspectratio]{\pdf}
\caption{A LiDAR pulse travelling to an obstacle $1\text{ m}$ away and back takes about $6.7\text{ ns}$.}
\label{fig:lidar_one_metre}
\end{figure}

\subsection{Echolocation}

In the animal kingdom there are many examples of the use of sound waves to obtain information about location. \textbf{Echolocation} (biosonar) is an active sensing mechanism that uses reflected sound waves to obtain spatial information about the surrounding environment: bats and toothed whales (e.g.\ dolphins) use it for navigation, obstacle detection and locating prey. Dolphins emit short, high-frequency acoustic clicks; the sound waves propagate through water, are reflected by the surrounding objects, and the returning echoes provide information about the distance, direction and characteristics of the objects (Figure~\ref{fig:echolocation}). The same physical principle is used in artificial \textbf{SONAR} (Sound Navigation and Ranging), as in submarines, and in ultrasonic distance sensors. Sperm whales produce the strongest sounds in nature: their extremely brief acoustic pulses can reach instantaneous intensities millions of times greater than those experienced at a loud rock concert, yet they last only about $100\,\mu\text{s}$.

\begin{figure}[!htbp]
\centering
\includegraphics[page=103, width=0.5\linewidth, keepaspectratio]{\pdf}
\caption{Dolphins locate objects from the echoes of their acoustic clicks; the same principle is used in artificial SONAR.}
\label{fig:echolocation}
\end{figure}

Humans, too, can develop echolocation skills, producing clicks and interpreting their echoes. In a study with functional magnetic resonance imaging (Thaler, Arnott and Goodale, \emph{PLOS ONE}, 2011), expert human echolocators, compared with control participants, activated brain regions typically associated with visual processing rather than auditory ones. This is a fascinating example of neuroplasticity: people who have developed echolocation can use reflected sounds to build spatial representations of their environment, recruiting brain regions normally associated with vision. It does not mean that they literally see with their ears, but it shows how auditory information can be processed to support spatial perception.

As in LiDAR, the distance is estimated from the round-trip travel time of the waves, with the speed of sound in place of the speed of light:
\begin{equation}
\boxed{d = \frac{v_{\text{sound}}\,\Delta t}{2}}
\label{eq:echo_distance}
\end{equation}
The approximate speeds are $3 \times 10^8\text{ m/s}$ for light, $343\text{ m/s}$ for sound in air and $1500\text{ m/s}$ for sound in seawater, where it is much faster.

\subsection{Ultrasound}

\textbf{Ultrasound} consists of mechanical sound waves with frequencies above the human hearing range (typically above $20\text{ kHz}$). In an ultrasound sensor a piezoelectric transducer converts electrical signals into ultrasonic pulses, and the returning echoes back into electrical signals. Ultrasound waves are reflected and scattered by objects and by interfaces between materials with different acoustic properties, and the distance to a reflecting object is again estimated from the round-trip travel time, Equation~\eqref{eq:echo_distance}, with the speed of sound in the propagation medium. The spatial resolution depends on the pulse duration, the wavelength and the beam width. Higher frequencies generally improve the spatial resolution but increase the attenuation, reducing the penetration depth. Applications include \textbf{ultrasonic ranging} for distance and obstacle detection, typically at frequencies around $40\text{ kHz}$ in air, and \textbf{medical ultrasound}, the non-invasive imaging of internal structures at frequencies in the MHz range: at $2\text{ MHz}$, for example, the approximate resolution is $0.8\text{ mm}$ axially and $1\text{ mm}$ laterally. Table~\ref{tab:sound_frequencies} compares the frequencies used by animals and by medical imaging.

\begin{table}[!htbp]
\centering
\caption{Frequencies of echolocation and medical ultrasound.}
\label{tab:sound_frequencies}
\begin{tabular*}{\textwidth}{l@{\extracolsep\fill}cc}
\toprule
Sound source & Frequency (kHz) & Frequency (MHz) \\
\midrule
Sperm whale (echolocation) & 2--30 & 0.002--0.030 \\
Bottlenose dolphin (echolocation) & 40--150 & 0.04--0.15 \\
Bat (echolocation) & 20--200 & 0.02--0.2 \\
Medical ultrasound imaging & 2\,000--15\,000 & 2--15 \\
Human hearing (upper limit) & 20 & 0.02 \\
\bottomrule
\end{tabular*}
\end{table}

\section{Occupancy and Motion Sensors}
\label{sec:occupancy}

\textbf{Occupancy sensors} detect the presence or absence of people or animals within a monitored area, whereas \textbf{motion sensors} detect movement, but may fail to detect occupants who remain stationary. The two are not the same: a sensor can detect occupancy without detecting motion, and it can detect motion without identifying the object. Everyday applications include automatic lighting and heating control, vehicle seat occupancy detection (for safety in the car) and touchless faucets; scientific applications include animal behaviour and movement studies, wildlife monitoring with camera traps and the monitoring of activity near water sources.

\subsection{Capacitive sensors}

The human body interacts with electric fields because of its electrical conductivity and dielectric properties. In a \textbf{capacitive sensor}, an electrode, always on, generates an electric field in the surrounding region; the presence of a person or an object, such as a hand, modifies the field and changes the measured capacitance (Figure~\ref{fig:capacitive_sensor}). By detecting the capacitance variation
\begin{equation}
\Delta C = C_{\text{measured}} - C_{\text{reference}},
\end{equation}
the sensor identifies proximity or occupancy.

\begin{figure}[!htbp]
\centering
\includegraphics[page=104, width=0.7\linewidth, keepaspectratio]{\pdf}
\caption{Field lines of a capacitive sensor without (left) and with (right) a finger nearby: the presence of the body modifies the electric field and the measured capacitance (Srivatava et al., \emph{J.\ Mater.\ Sci.: Mater.\ Electron.}\ 36, 664, 2025).}
\label{fig:capacitive_sensor}
\end{figure}

\subsection{Camera-based sensors}

The most widespread sensors are camera-based. Camera sensors (CCD or CMOS) acquire images at successive time intervals, and motion is detected by comparing consecutive frames, or by identifying changes relative to a reference background image: software compares the image at time $t_1$ with the image at time $t_2$ and decides whether something moved,
\begin{equation}
\Delta I(x,y) = I(x,y,t_2) - I(x,y,t_1).
\end{equation}
Image processing can also identify objects, estimate occupancy and track their movements. The camera measures light intensity, while motion is inferred from changes in the acquired images: this is another example of indirect measurement. The advantages are a high spatial resolution, detailed scene information and the possibility of detecting, classifying and tracking multiple objects and distinguishing one from another. The limitations are that performance depends on illumination, visibility and occlusions, that image processing is demanding in terms of computation and memory, and that privacy and data management may be important concerns.

\subsection{Photoelectric and passive optical sensors}

Simpler systems detect presence or proximity with a light source and a detector. \textbf{Photoelectric sensors} detect the presence or movement of objects by measuring changes in the received light intensity: a light source (e.g.\ an LED) illuminates the monitored region, a photodetector converts the received light into an electrical signal, and the presence or movement of an object modifies the light reaching the detector, either by interrupting the beam between an emitter and a receiver (\emph{through-beam} detection) or by changing the amount of light reflected toward the receiver (\emph{reflective} detection). A change in the detector output triggers an action when a predefined threshold is crossed. This is again active sensing, because the system itself generates the optical signal used for detection. Applications include touchless faucets, automatic doors, object counters and proximity switches.

An even simpler approach uses the changes in ambient illumination. A \textbf{light-dependent resistor} (LDR), or photoresistor, changes its electrical resistance according to the incident light intensity; when an object blocks or modifies the incoming light, the resistance changes, and the resulting electrical signal can trigger an action when a threshold is crossed. Such a sensor operates with ambient light, without a dedicated light source, but it cannot reliably distinguish the movement of an object from changes in the environmental illumination, and it typically has a slower response than photodiodes or phototransistors. It is used in light-activated switches, simple object detectors and interactive toys.

Table~\ref{tab:occupancy_summary} summarises these techniques, together with \textbf{passive infrared} (PIR) sensors, which detect the changes in thermal infrared radiation in the environment.

\begin{table}[!htbp]
\centering
\caption{Summary of occupancy and motion sensing techniques.}
\label{tab:occupancy_summary}
\begin{tabular*}{\textwidth}{p{3.6cm}@{\extracolsep\fill}p{5cm}p{5cm}}
\toprule
Sensing technique & Measured quantity & Inferred information \\
\midrule
Capacitive sensor & Capacitance change, $\Delta C$ & Presence or proximity \\
Camera-based sensor & Spatial light-intensity distribution, $I(x,y,t)$ & Occupancy, motion, object identity \\
Photoelectric sensor & Received light intensity & Object presence or passage \\
Passive infrared (PIR) sensor & Changes in thermal infrared radiation & Motion of thermally contrasting objects \\
\bottomrule
\end{tabular*}
\end{table}
